Asymptotically optimal regret in communicating Markov decision processes
Victor Boone victor.boone@inria.fr

Abstract
In this paper, we present a learning algorithm that achieves asymptotically optimal regret for Markov decision processes in average reward under a communicating assumption. That is, given a communicating Markov decision process M M , our algorithm has regret K ⁡ ( M ) ​ log ⁡ ( T ) + o ( log ⁡ ( T ) ) K(M)\log(T)+\oh(\log(T)) where T T is the number of learning steps and K ⁡ ( M ) K(M) is the best possible constant. This algorithm works by explicitly tracking the constant K ⁡ ( M ) K(M) to learn optimally, then balances the trade-off between exploration (playing sub-optimally to gain information), co-exploration (playing optimally to gain information) and exploitation (playing optimally to score maximally). We further show that the function K ⁡ ( M ) K(M) is discontinuous, which is a consequence challenge for our approach. To that end, we describe a regularization mechanism to estimate K ⁡ ( M ) K(M) with arbitrary precision from empirical data. Keywords: Markov decision processes, Average reward, Regret, Reinforcement learning.



Victor Boone

1
Introduction

There is a Markov decision process, and there is a learning algorithm doing its best at picking actions to gather as much reward as possible.
The algorithm is learning, in that it is unaware of the structure of the Markov decision process; The algorithm learns forever and there is no reset mechanism whatsoever, in that the time horizon is infinite and that rewards are not discounted.
As such, the so-described system is a Markov decision process in average reward of which the reward and transition functions are unknown to the algorithm.
In this paper, we measure the learning performance by the
regret
, that compares the amount of reward collectible by an ominous algorithm that knows the Markov decision process in advance and plays optimal actions, to the learning algorithm that has to learn everything.
Following
Auer et al. 2009
and all the subsequent works on regret minimization in average reward Markov decision processes, we define the regret after
T
learning steps as

Tg^{*}-\sum_{t=1}^{T}R_{t}

(1)

where
g^{*}
is the optimal gain of the unknown Markov decision process
Puterman 2014
and
R_{t}
is the reward earned by the learning algorithm at time
t
.
The smaller the regret is, the better the algorithm performs.

In this work, we are interested in
asymptotic
regret guarantees in the
model dependent
setting and in
expectation
.
That is, the unknown Markov decision process
M
is fixed and we want to design a learning algorithm
\Lambda
such that the expectation of (
1
) under
M
and
\Lambda
, denoted
\textrm{Reg}(T;M,\Lambda)
, is as small as possible when
T\to\infty
.
This very problem goes back to the bandit literature, especially to the seminal paper of
Lai and Robbins 1985
.
Despite an immense literature in the surroundings of bandit problems, where the problem is mostly solved—see the book of
Lattimore and Szepesvári 2020
for instance, gaping holes are left in the more general world of Markov decision processes.
Existing works either stick to a strong ergodic assumption
Agrawal et al. 1988
;
Burnetas and Katehakis 1997
;
Graves and Lai 1997
;
Auer and Ortner 2006
;
Pesquerel and Maillard 2022
, or provide partial solutions
Tranos and Proutiere 2021
, or have no proof of asymptotic optimality
Auer et al. 2009
;
Filippi et al. 2010
;
Saber et al. 2024
.
It was only recently
Boone and Maillard 2025
that a general lower bound for the asymptotic regret of “reasonable” algorithms (see
Section

2.2.2
and
Definition

3
) has been described under the weaker
communicating
assumption (
2
): For every reasonable learning algorithm, we have

\textrm{Reg}(T;M,\Lambda)\geq K(M)\log(T)+\oh(\log(T))\hskip 20.00003pt\text{as\penalty\ $T\to\infty$}

(2)

where
K(M)\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
is a constant that describes the learning difficulty of
M
and that we recall in
Theorem

6
.

In this paper, we show that the bound of (
2
) is tight: We describe an algorithm
\Lambda
such that
\textrm{Reg}(T;M,\Lambda)=K(M)\log(T)+\oh(\log(T))
for all communicating Markov decision process
M
simulatenously, therefore solving the regret minimization problem at first order under the communicating assumption.

2
Preliminaries

In this section, we provide some background on average reward Markov decision processes and the associated regret minimization problem.

General notations.

Given a set
\mathcal{X}
, we denote
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
the set of probability distributions over
\mathcal{X}
.
We write
p\ll q
if
p\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
is absolutely continuous with respect to
q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
and
p\sim q
if
p\ll q
and
q\ll p
.
Given two finite sets
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, a transition kernel from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{Y}
, or
kernel
, is a map
k:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})
and we write
k(y|x)
the probability that
Y=y
under
Y\sim k(x)
.
Given
k:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})
a transition kernel, we write
\lVert k\rVert_{\infty}:=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\lVert k(x)\rVert_{1}
its infinity norm.
We write
\mathrm{KL}(p||q):=\int\log(\frac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}q})\penalty\ \mathrm{d}p
the Kullback-Leibler divergence from distribution
q
to distribution
p
.
Given a vector
u\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{d}
, we write
\mathrm{dmin}(u):=\min\left\{u_{i}:u_{i}>0\right\}
its definite minimum.
The span of a vector
u\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
is denoted
\mathrm{sp}(u):=\max(u)-\min(u)
.

2.1
Markov decision processes in average reward

A
Markov decision process
(or
model
) is a tuple
M\equiv(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,\nu)
where
\mathcal{S}
is the state space and
\mathcal{A}\equiv\prod_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{A}(s)
is the action space, together forming the pair space
\mathcal{Z}:=\prod_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\{s\}\times\mathcal{A}(s)
;
p:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
is the transition kernel and
\nu:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{P}([0,1])
is the reward function.
Its mean reward function is
r:\mathcal{Z}\to[0,1]
and is given by
r(z):=\int x\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\nu(z)(x)
.
We write
S_{t},A_{t},R_{t}
the
t
-th step state, action and reward observed when interacting with
M
, and we further denote
Z_{t}:=(S_{t},A_{t})
the played state-action pair at time
t
.
By definition,

S_{t+1}\sim p(Z_{t})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptR_{t}\sim\nu(Z_{t})

are sampled independently of each other and independently of the previous history of play, given by
H_{t}:=(S_{1},A_{1},R_{1},\ldots,S_{t})
, making the system memoryless.
The space of histories is denoted
\mathcal{H}:=\bigcup_{t\geq 1}((\mathcal{Z}\times[0,1])^{t-1}\times\mathcal{S})
.
Given
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
a subset of pairs, we write
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}):=\{s\in\mathcal{S}:\{s\}\times\mathcal{A}(s)\cap\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset\}
the projection of
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
onto
\mathcal{S}
.
All throughout the paper, we will assume that
M
has a finite state-action space, see
1
below.

Assumption 1
.

The pair space
\mathcal{Z}
is fixed and finite.

2.1.1
Randomized policies, their gain, bias & gap functions

While new states and rewards are generated by the Markov decision process, actions are chosen by an exterior mechanism, and the simplest way to choose actions are
policies
.
A (stationary) randomized policy is a kernel
\pi:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
from states to probabilities over legal actions, and it is said deterministic if of the form
\pi:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{A}
.
We write
\Pi^{\mathrm{SR}}
and
\Pi
the respective sets of randomized and deterministic policies.

Once a policy
\pi\in\Pi^{\mathrm{SR}}
, a model
M
and an initial state
s_{0}\in\mathcal{S}
are fixed, the distribution of
H_{t}
is completely determined for
t\geq 1
and we write
\mathbf{P}^{M,\pi}_{s_{0}}(-)
and
\mathbf{E}^{M,\Lambda}_{s_{0}}[-]
the probability and expectation operators over the induced probability space on
\mathcal{H}
.
Note that a fixed policy defines a Markov reward process on
\mathcal{S}
, and in particular a Markov chain.
We say that a policy is
ergodic
,
recurrent
or
unichain
if the associated Markov chain is respectively ergodic (irreducible and aperiodic), irreducible or if it has a unique stationary probability distribution (unique recurrent component), see
(
Levin and Peres 2017
, §1)
for more details on the terminology.

Given a policy
\pi\in\Pi^{\mathrm{SR}}
, its
gain

g^{\pi}
is the amount of reward that the policy collects in average and in the long run; Its
bias

b^{\pi}
is the amount of reward that it collects in addition to the gain, visually:

\displaystyle g^{\pi}(s;M):=\lim_{T\to\infty}\mathbf{E}_{s}^{M,\pi}\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}R_{t}\right]\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ b^{\pi}(s;M):=\Clim_{T\to\infty}\mathbf{E}_{s}^{M,\pi}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(R_{t}-g^{\pi}(S_{t};M)\right)\right]

(3)

where
\Clim
is the Cesàro-limit.
The
gap function
of a policy
\pi\in\Pi^{\mathrm{SR}}
is
\Delta^{\pi}(s,a;M):=g^{\pi}(s;M)+b^{\pi}(s;M)-r(s,a)-p(s,a)b^{\pi}(M)
.
When too heavy to carry, the dependence on
M
may be dropped in notations to lighten up everything.
Following standard Markov decision process theory, all these objects are well-defined, see
(
Puterman 2014
, §9)
for e.g.

2.1.2
Communicating Markov decision processes and diameter

A central assumption that is adopted in the following pages is the communicativity of the underlying model (
2
), meaning that one can travel from any state to any other in finite time under the right policy, i.e., that the diameter is finite.
Equivalently, it can be shown that the diameter is finite if, and only if every policy
\pi\in\Pi^{\mathrm{SR}}
of full support, i.e., every policy such that
\pi(a|s)>0
for all
(s,a)\in\mathcal{Z}
, is recurrent.
The space of fully supported randomized policies, or
fully randomized policies
, is denoted
\Pi^{\mathrm{SFR}}
.

Assumption 2
.

The model
M
is communicating, i.e., it has finite diameter:

D(M):=\max_{s\neq s^{\prime}}\min_{\pi\in\Pi}\mathbf{E}_{s}^{M,\pi}\left[\inf\left\{t\geq 1:S_{t}=s^{\prime}\right\}\right]<\infty.

(4)

2
is fairly standard in the literature on regret minimization for average reward Markov decision processes, and it can be found in
Auer et al. 2009
;
Filippi et al. 2010
;
Tossou et al. 2019
;
Bourel et al. 2020
;
Fruit et al. 2020
;
Zhang and Ji 2019
;
Boone and Gaujal 2023
;
Saber et al. 2024
;
Boone and Gaujal 2025
for example.
This assumption is much weaker than the well-known ergodic assumption, found in
Agrawal et al. 1988
;
Burnetas and Katehakis 1997
for e.g..
Indeed, if a Markov decision process is ergodic, every state is recurrent under every policy, so that the diameter (
2
) is automatically finite.
The converse is wrong: All the examples of Markov decision processes that we provide in this work (see
Figures

1
,
2
,
3
,
4
and
2
) are communicating but not ergodic.

2.1.3
Optimal policies, gain, bias and the Bellman gaps

Given a model
M
, a policy
\pi^{*}\in\Pi^{\mathrm{SR}}
is said
gain optimal
, written
\pi^{*}\in\Pi^{*}(M)
, if it achieves maximal gain over all possible policies, i.e.,

g^{\pi^{*}}(s;M)=g^{*}(s;M):=\max_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathrm{SR}}}g^{\pi}(s;M)

(5)

regardless of the initial state
s\in\mathcal{S}
.
The vector
g^{*}(M)\in\mathbf{R}^{\mathcal{S}}
is the optimal gain vector.
A policy
\pi^{*}
is said
bias optimal
if it maximizes the bias among gain optimal policies, i.e., if
\pi^{*}\in\Pi^{*}(M)
and
b^{\pi^{*}}(s;M)=b^{*}(s;M):=\max_{\pi\in\Pi^{*}(M)}b^{\pi}(s;M)
for all initial state
s\in\mathcal{S}
.
The vector
b^{*}(M)\in\mathbf{R}^{\mathcal{S}}
is the optimal bias vector.
According to standard theory
(
Puterman 2014
, §9)
, deterministic gain and bias optimal policies exist.
Lastly, the
Bellman gaps
are the gaps of bias optimal policies, given by

\Delta^{*}(s,a;M):=g^{*}(s;M)+b^{*}(s;M)-r(s,a)-p(s,a)b^{*}(M).

(6)

Under the communicating assumption (
2
), it is well-known that the optimal gain does not depend on the initial state with
\mathrm{sp}(g^{*}(M))=0
and that Bellman gaps are non-negative.

2.1.4
Optimal and weakly optimal state-action pairs

We borrow the classification of pairs from
Boone and Maillard 2025
.

Definition 0

(Optimal, weakly optimal and sub-optimal pairs)
.

Let
M\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p,\nu)
be a communicating model (
2
).
A pair
z\in\mathcal{Z}
is said
weakly optimal
if
\Delta^{*}(z)=0
, written
z\in\mathcal{Z}^{*}(M)
;
A pair
z\equiv(s,a)\in\mathcal{Z}
is said
optimal
if it is recurrent under some gain optimal policy, i.e., there exists
\pi\in\Pi^{*}(M)
such that
\mathbf{P}_{s}^{M,\pi}(\forall n,\exists m\geq n:Z_{m}=z)=1
, and in that case we write
z\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
.

When a model
M
is communicating, it is known that
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)\subseteq\mathcal{Z}^{*}(M)
with equality if, and only if there exists a recurrent gain optimal policy
Boone and Maillard 2025
.
In spirit, optimal pairs are all the pairs that are played infinitely often by gain optimal policies, while weakly optimal pairs that are not optimal correspond to their transient counterparts.
Weakly optimal pairs are pairs that can be played without increasing the regret, see
Definition

2
and the discussion on (
7
).
In light of this property, a pair
z\notin\mathcal{Z}^{*}(M)
is said
sub-optimal
.

2.2
Learning algorithms, regret minimization and lower bounds

Formally, a
learning algorithm
is a kernel
\Lambda:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{A})
, mapping histories to probabilities over actions.
Similarly to policies, the choice of a learning algorithm
\Lambda
, a Markov decision process
M
and an initial state
s_{0}\in\mathcal{S}
completely determines the distribution of the history
H_{t}
for all
t\geq 1
; We write
\mathbf{P}_{s_{0}}^{M,\Lambda}(-)
and
\mathbf{E}_{s_{0}}^{M,\Lambda}[-]
the probability and expectation operators over the induced probability space on
\mathcal{H}
.
The
visit count
at time
t\geq 1
of a pair
z\in\mathcal{Z}
is the number of time this pair has been visited prior to time
t
, denoted
N_{t}(z):=\sum_{i=1}^{t-1}\mathbf{1}(Z_{i}=z)
.

2.2.1
The expected regret

In this paper, we measure the learning performance of learning algorithms with the regret.

Definition 0

(Regret,
Auer et al. 2009
)
.

The
expected regret
of a learning algorithm
\Lambda
in the model
M
at horizon
T\geq 1
and from the initial state
s_{0}\in\mathcal{S}
is given by

\textrm{Reg}(T;M,\Lambda,s_{0}):=\mathbf{E}_{s_{0}}^{M,\Lambda}\left[Tg^{*}(s_{0};M)-\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}R_{t}\right].

Under the communicating assumption (
2
), it appears that the initial state
s_{0}\in\mathcal{S}
is not important conceptually, and we will often willingly forget it in equations.

The idea behind the definition of the regret
Definition

2
is that by writing
\mathbf{E}[R_{t}|S_{t},A_{t}]=g^{*}(S_{t})+b^{*}(S_{t})-b^{*}(S_{t+1})-\Delta^{*}(S_{t},A_{t})
and summing for
t\geq 1
, we have

\mathbf{E}_{s_{0}}^{M,\Lambda}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}R_{t}\right]=Tg^{*}(s_{0})+b^{*}(s_{0})-\mathbf{E}_{s_{0}}^{M,\Lambda}\left[b^{*}(S_{T+1})\right]-\mathbf{E}_{s_{0}}^{M,\Lambda}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Delta^{*}(Z_{t})\right].

(7)

In (
7
), the quantity
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Delta^{*}(Z_{t})
is non-negative by non-negativity of Bellman gaps.
It follows that for every learning algorithm
\Lambda
, we have
\mathbf{E}^{M,\Lambda}[\sum_{t=1}^{T}R_{t}]\leq Tg^{*}+\mathrm{sp}(b^{*})
.
Similarly, we obtain that
\mathbf{E}^{M,\pi}[\sum_{t=1}^{T}R_{t}]\geq Tg^{*}-\mathrm{sp}(b^{*})
for every bias optimal policy
\pi
.
So,
Tg^{*}
is a good comparison proxy for the performance of the optimal policy—hence the definition of the regret.
Note that (
7
) also shows that the expected regret is the sum of the Bellman gaps up to a bounded error, with
\textrm{Reg}(T;M,\Lambda,s_{0})=\mathbf{E}_{s_{0}}^{M,\Lambda}[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Delta^{*}(Z_{t})]+\OH(1)
, meaning that the regret only grows when sub-optimal pairs are played.

2.2.2
Consistent algorithms and lower bound of
Boone and Maillard 2025

Every regret lower bound requires a form of assumption on the class of learning algorithms that we consider.
The point is to discard algorithms that overspecialize their performance to a happy few Markov decision processes.
Following a large panel of the literature, we restrict the focus to
consistent
algorithms (see
Definition

3
), also known as strong consistency or uniformly fast convergence, see
Lattimore and Szepesvári 2020
for e.g.

Definition 0

(Consistency)
.

A learning algorithm
\Lambda
is said
consistent
on a space of Markov decision processes
\mathcal{M}
if, for all
\eta>0
, all
M\in\mathcal{M}
and
s_{0}\in\mathcal{S}
,
\textrm{Reg}(T;M,\Lambda,s_{0})=\oh(T^{\eta})
.

Consistency is the assumption under which
Boone and Maillard 2025
state their regret lower bound
\textrm{Reg}(T;M,\Lambda)\geq K(M)\log(T)+\oh(\log(T))
that we recall in
Theorem

6
.

The constant
K(M)\in[0,\infty]
is the solution of an optimization problem that is about gathering a sufficient amount of information for a minimal cost.
That optimization problem goes over
\mu
within the polytope of invariant measures
\mathrm{Inv}(M)
(
Definition

4
); The objective function is
\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\Delta^{*}(z)
, that is directly related to the regret; And
\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
must further satisfy an information constraint, consisting in that
\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\geq 1
for every model
M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)
.
In that information constraint,
\mathrm{Cnf}(M)
is the confusing set (
Definition

5
), which is in the collection of models that coincide with
M
on optimal pairs
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
and such that
M
and
M^{\dagger}
do not share any gain optimal policy.
That information constraint can be interpreted as a rejection principle: Enough information must be gathered in order to “reject” every confusing model.

Definition 0

(Invariant measures)
.

An
invariant measure
of a Markov decision process
M
is a vector
\mu\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{\mathcal{Z}}
such that, for all
s\in\mathcal{S}
, we have

\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\mu(s,a)=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)p(s|z).

Definition 0

(Confusing set,
Boone and Maillard 2025
)
.

Fix
\mathcal{M}
a space of Markov decision process.
The
confusing set
of a communicating model
M\in\mathcal{M}
is

\mathrm{Cnf}(M):=\left\{M^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{M}:M^{\dagger}\gg M,M^{\dagger}=M\mathrm{\penalty\ on\penalty\ }\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)\mathrm{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\Pi^{*}(M^{\dagger})\cap\Pi^{*}(M)=\emptyset\right\}

where
M^{\dagger}\gg M
means that
\nu^{\dagger}(z)\gg\nu(z)
and
p^{\dagger}(z)\gg p(z)
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, or equivalently, that
\support(\nu^{\dagger}(z))\supseteq\support(\nu(z))
and
\support(p^{\dagger}(z))\supseteq\support(p(z))
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
.

With
Definition

4
and
Definition

5
in hand, we can state the lower bound of
Boone and Maillard 2025
.

Theorem 6

(
Boone and Maillard 2025
)
.

Fix
\mathcal{M}
a space of Markov decision process.
For every learning algorithm
\Lambda
that is consistent on
\mathcal{M}
, for all
M\in\mathcal{M}
that is communicating (
2
) and
s_{0}\in\mathcal{S}
, we have
\textrm{Reg}(T;M,\Lambda,s_{0})\geq K(M)\log(T)+\oh(\log(T))
where
K(M)\in[0,\infty]
is given by

\inf\left\{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\Delta^{*}(z):\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)\mathrm{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)}\left\{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\right\}\geq 1\right\}

(8)

where
\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z)):=\mathrm{KL}(\nu(z)||\nu^{\dagger}(z))+\mathrm{KL}(p(z)||p^{\dagger}(z))
.

Any measure
\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
achieving
K(M)
is called an
optimal exploration measure
.
1
1

1

We show that such measures exist in
Appendix

B
.

In the remaining of this paper, we show that the lower bound described in
Theorem

6
is tight.
We provide a consistent learning algorithm
\Lambda
such that
\textrm{Reg}(T;M,\Lambda)\leq K(M)\log(T)+\oh(\log(T))
under mild condition on the ambient space
\mathcal{M}
.
As a consequence, the constant
K(M)
given above is the right measure of the learning difficulty of the communicating model
M
in the asymptotic regime
T\to\infty
.

3
A framework of asymptotically optimal algorithms:
ECoE

To begin our paper, we present the framework of
ECoE
, standing for
E
xploration-
Co
exploration-
E
xploitation.
That framework of algorithms is inspired from the lower bound of
Theorem

6
and from intermediate results of
Boone and Maillard 2025
.
We will start by describing the framework from a high level perspective, then provide an instance
\Lambda
of
ECoE
in
Section

5
that we prove to be asymptotically optimal.
More specifically, we prove that our algorithm
\Lambda
satisfies
\textrm{Reg}(T;M,\Lambda,s_{0})\leq K(M)\log(T)+\oh(\log(T))
for all communicating model
M
and initial state
s_{0}\in\mathcal{S}
.

The
ECoE
framework combines three main components:

1.

Exploration
, that is about playing sub-optimal pairs (
Section

2.1.4
) to obtain information.
It turns out the cost of exploration is of order
\log(T)
and accounts for the dominant part of the regret lower bound.
Accordingly,
K(M)
and the associated optimization problem (
8
) describe the optimal way to explore the environment
M
.
In
Section

3.1
, we explain how to obtain an optimal exploration policy from (
8
).

2.

Co-Exploration
, that makes sure that
all
optimal pairs (
Definition

1
) are visited over-logarithmically.
The cost of co-exploration is
\oh(\log(T))
, that is negligible in front of the cost of exploration.
For general communicating Markov decision processes, co-exploration brings new challenges that are absent from the ergodic setting and multi-armed bandits.
They are discussed in more details in
Section

3.2
.

3.

Exploitation
, that is about scoring maximally when there is no apparent need to collect information.
In
Section

3.3
, we explain how to choose the exploitation policy and how to determine whether information is lacking or not.

3.1
Retrieving a good exploration policy by estimating
K(M)

The regret lower bound of
Theorem

6
is only about gathering information outside of
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
; Indeed, as the information constraint “
\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\geq 1
” only involves models
M^{\dagger}
that coincide with
M
on
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
by definition, the bound of
Theorem

6
tells nothing about the behavior of consistent learning algorithms outside of
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
.
So, essentially,
Theorem

6
claims that the dominant part of the regret is scaling logarithmically and is due to exploration exclusively.

In fact, it also says
how
to explore optimally.

Indeed,
Equation

8
can be rewritten in terms of an optimization problem over policies.
The policy obtained by solving it can be used to explore optimally; Such a policy is called an optimal exploration policy.
The precise alternative form for
K(M)
is found in
Proposition

7
, where we show that the computation of the lower bound is about finding a policy
\pi
that optimizes the ratio of the gain-loss
g^{*}(M)-g^{\pi}(M)
and of the amount of gathered information
\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu^{\pi}(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))
under that policy.

Proposition 7

(Policy-wise formulation of
K(M)
)
.

Fix
\mathcal{M}
a space of Markov decision processes.
Let
M\in\mathcal{M}
be a communicating model.
For
\pi\in\Pi^{\mathrm{SFR}}
a fully randomized policy (
Section

2.1.2
), denote by
\mu^{\pi}
its unique probability invariant measure in
M
(i.e., the unique probability invariant measure of the Markov chain that
\pi
induces on
\mathcal{Z}
, see
Proposition

A.10
(1)).
We have

K(M)=\inf_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathrm{SFR}}}\left\{\frac{g^{*}(M)-g^{\pi}(M)}{\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu^{\pi}(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))}\right\}

(9)

with the convention
x/0=+\infty
for
x\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.

Note that for
\pi\in\Pi^{\mathrm{SFR}}
, we have
\mu^{\pi}(s,a)=\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\mathbf{E}_{s}^{M,\pi}[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}(Z_{t}=(s,a))]
.

The infimum in (
9
) is not a minimum in general, meaning that optimal exploration is not guaranteed to be reached by fully uniform policies.
This concern is minor, because the learning algorithm will only have access to a noisy version of
M
, so getting an approximation of
K(M)
is fine anyway—provided that the approximation can be arbitrarily precise.
The take-away is that

Equation

9
encourages a natural approach: Compute a fully randomized exploration policy that optimizes the cost/information gain trade-off well enough and use it to explore efficiently.
So, provided that we can solve (
9
), the problem of optimal exploration is dealt with.

Proof of
Proposition

7
.

If
K(M)=+\infty
, there is nothing to prove.
Assume
K(M)<\infty
.

Denote
C(\mu;M):=\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))
the amount of information gathered by a measure
\mu\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.
Note that every element
\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
is of the form
\lambda\mu^{\prime}
with
\lambda\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
and
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z})
.
So, from (
8
), we have

K(M)=\inf_{\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z})}\left\{\frac{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\Delta^{*}(z)}{C(\mu;M)}\right\}

with the convention
x/0=+\infty
for
x\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
.
Now, we replace
\mathrm{Inv}(M)\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z})
by
\mathcal{I}_{>0}(M):=\{\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(M):\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z})\mathrm{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\support(\mu)=\mathcal{Z}\}
the set of fully supported probability invariant measures of
\mu
.
It is obvious that

K(M)\leq\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{I}_{>0}(M)}\{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\Delta^{*}(z)/C(\mu;M)\}

and we focus on the reverse inequality, that is obtained by approximating probability invariant measures by fully supported ones using
Theorem

A.11
.
Given
\epsilon>0
, let
\mu_{\epsilon}\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z})
be such that
\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu_{\epsilon}(z)\Delta^{*}(z)/C(\mu_{\epsilon};M)\leq K(M)+\epsilon
.
By
Theorem

A.11
, for all
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, there exists
\mu_{\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{I}_{>0}(M)
such that
\lVert\mu_{\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}-\mu_{\epsilon}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}
and
\mu_{\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}\geq(1-\epsilon^{\prime})\mu_{\epsilon}
.
So,

\displaystyle\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{I}_{>0}(M)}\left\{\frac{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\Delta^{*}(z)}{C(\mu;M)}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\epsilon>0}\inf_{\epsilon^{\prime}>0}\left\{\frac{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu_{\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}(z)\Delta^{*}(z)}{\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)}\mu_{\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))}\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{\leq}\inf_{\epsilon>0}\inf_{\epsilon^{\prime}>0}\left\{\frac{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu_{\epsilon}(z)\Delta^{*}(z)+\epsilon^{\prime}\lVert\Delta^{*}\rVert_{\infty}}{(1-\epsilon^{\prime})\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)}\mu_{\epsilon}(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))}\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{(\ddagger)}{\leq}\inf_{\epsilon>0}\inf_{\epsilon^{\prime}>0}\left\{\frac{1}{1-\epsilon^{\prime}}\left(K(M)+\epsilon+\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}\lVert\Delta^{*}\rVert_{\infty}}{C(\mu_{\epsilon};M)}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{(\lx@sectionsign)}{=}\inf_{\epsilon>0}\left\{K(M)+\epsilon\right\}=K(M)

where

(\dagger)
follows by definition of
\mu_{\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}
;

(\ddagger)
follows by definition on
\mu_{\epsilon}
; and

(\lx@sectionsign)
follows from
C(\mu_{\epsilon};M)>0
, that holds because
\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu_{\epsilon}(z)\Delta^{*}(z)/C(\mu_{\epsilon};M)<\infty
.

We conclude using
Proposition

A.10
to rewrite the optimization problem over
\Pi^{\mathrm{SFR}}
:

\displaystyle\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{I}_{>0}(M)}\left\{\frac{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\Delta^{*}(z)}{C(\mu;M)}\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{=}\inf_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathrm{SFR}}}\left\{\frac{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu^{\pi}(z)\Delta^{*}(z)}{C(\mu^{\pi};M)}\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{(\ddagger)}{=}\inf_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathrm{SFR}}}\left\{\frac{\lim\mathbf{E}_{s_{0}}^{M,\pi}\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}(Z_{t}=z)\Delta^{*}(z)\right]}{C(\mu^{\pi};M)}\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{(\lx@sectionsign)}{=}\inf_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathrm{SFR}}}\left\{\frac{\lim\mathbf{E}_{s_{0}}^{M,\pi}\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(g^{*}+b^{*}(S_{t})-b^{*}(S_{t+1})-R_{t}\right)\right]}{C(\mu^{\pi};M)}\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{(\$)}{=}\inf_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathrm{SFR}}}\left\{\frac{g^{*}(s_{0})-g^{\pi}(s_{0})}{C(\mu^{\pi};M)}\right\}\overset{(\#)}{=}\inf_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathrm{SFR}}}\left\{\frac{g^{*}-g^{\pi}}{C(\mu^{\pi};M)}\right\}

where

(\dagger)
follows from
Proposition

A.10
(2);

(\ddagger)
follows from
Proposition

A.10
(1) and introduces
s_{0}\in\mathcal{S}
arbitrary;

(\lx@sectionsign)
expands the gaps as
\Delta^{*}(Z_{t})=g^{*}(S_{t})+b^{*}(S_{t})-r(Z_{t})-p(Z_{t})b^{*}
;

(\$)
follows by definition of the gain of
\pi
; and

(\#)
uses that
s_{0}\in\mathcal{S}
is arbitrary.

\blacksquare

3.2
Co-exploration: Traveling between components of optimal pairs

By construction of
K(M)
, exploration is not meant to gather information on optimal pairs.

Boone and Maillard 2025
show that optimal pairs are treated differently than others, in that they are visited over-logarithmically.
They show that, for all optimal pair
z\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
, all consistent learning algorithm
\Lambda
and provided that
\mathcal{M}
is rich enough, we have

\liminf_{T\to\infty}\frac{\mathbf{E}_{s_{0}}^{M,\Lambda}\left[N_{T}(z)\right]}{\log(T)}=+\infty.

(10)

To guarantee that (
10
) is satisfied,
ECoE
is careful at its choice for the exploitation policy, for how long it iterates it, and when it iterates it, for the consequence of (
10
) is that during play,
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
must be covered uniformly fast.
That motivates the ideas thereafter.

1.

The exploitation policy
\pi^{+}
must be of maximal support among
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
.
Ideally, we want to pick
\pi^{+}(-|s)
uniform on
\{a\in\mathcal{A}(s):(s,a)\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)\}
.

2.

The exploitation policy is iterated up to
regeneration
, i.e., we wait for a return to the initial state it was first iterated from.
By doing so, we guarantee that all the states of the current recurrent classes are visited with positive probability.

3.2.1
An issue: The number of travels

One difficulty arise—one that does not exist for ergodic Markov decision processes: The set of optimal pairs
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
may be split into several components that may only be accessible by playing sub-optimal pairs (see
Definition

8
for a formal definition of components).
One such example is provided in
Figure

1
.

1
2
0
0
0.5+\theta
0.5

Figure 1:

A class of Markov decision processes with deterministic transitions parameterized by
\theta
where co-exploration is troublesome.
Arrows are choices of actions that deterministically lead to the pointed state and labels are rewards.

Consider the class of Markov decision processes
\mathcal{M}:=\{M_{\theta}:\lvert\theta\rvert\leq\frac{1}{2}\}
where
M_{\theta}
is the model with Bernoulli rewards described
Figure

1
.
Note that the policy alternating between the states
1
and
2
is never gain optimal for
M_{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}
.
Now, consider
M_{0}\in\mathcal{M}
and let
\Lambda^{*}
be an asymptotically optimal algorithm for
\mathcal{M}
, i.e., assume that
\Lambda^{*}
is such that
\textrm{Reg}(T;M_{\theta},\Lambda^{*})\leq K(M_{\theta})\log(T)+\oh(\log(T))
for all
\theta\in\Theta
.
In
M_{0}
, optimal pairs consists in the two loops
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M_{0})=\{(1,1),(2,2)\}
and are separated by sub-optimal transitions of null reward.
In the first hand, the algorithm
\Lambda^{*}
must alternate infinitely often between the states
1
and
2
because (
10
) is satisfied.
On the second hand, we see that the confusing set of
M_{0}
relatively to
\mathcal{M}
is empty, so that
K(M_{0})=0
.
Accordingly,
\textrm{Reg}(T;M_{0},\Lambda^{*})=\oh(\log(T))
.
So,

\mathbf{E}^{M_{0},\Lambda^{*}}\left[N_{T}(1,2)+N_{T}(2,1)\right]=\oh(\log(T)).

Interestingly,
M_{0}
is the same example as in
(
Ortner 2010
, Figure 2)
that is used to motivate the use of episodes in
UCYCLE

Ortner 2010
,
UCRL2

Auer et al. 2009
and all the algorithms that followed.
These algorithms make
\Omega(\log(T))
episodes, and because of it, we see that on
M_{0}
above, they switch
\Omega(\log(T))
times between the loops on the states
1
and
2
.
Therefore, the regret of these algorithms is
\Omega(\log(T))
on
M_{0}
and they are not asymptotically optimal—provided that
Theorem

6
is tight, which is yet to be shown.

Definition 0

(Component of pairs)
.

Let
M\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p,\nu)
be a model and let
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
be a subset of pairs.
A communicating component of
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
, or just
component
, is a maximal set
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
such that the restrained model
M^{\prime\prime}:=(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime\prime},p|_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime\prime}},\nu|_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime\prime}})
is well-defined
2
2

2

By
well-defined
, we mean that by writing
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime\prime}\equiv\prod_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}}\{s^{\prime\prime}\}\times\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}(s^{\prime\prime})
, we have
\support p(z^{\prime\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}
for all
z^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}(s^{\prime\prime})\neq\emptyset
for all
s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}
.
and communicating.

3.2.2
Reducing the number of travels with the square trick

To reduce the number of travels between the several possibles components (
Definition

8
) of
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
,
ECoE
uses a variant of the doubling trick (see
Auer et al. 2009
) that we call the
square trick
(
ST
).

3.

Before exploiting, we check if it is worth traveling to another component of
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
:
We decompose
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
into components
\mathcal{Z}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{Z}_{c}
, and denote
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\in\left\{\mathcal{Z}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{Z}_{c}\right\}
the component the algorithm is currently on.
If

\min_{1\leq i\leq c}\min_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}}N(z)<\sqrt{\min_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}}N(z)},

(
ST
)

then, we travel to the least visit component of
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
; Otherwise, we exploit on the current component
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
as described in points (1) and (2) upstream.

Note the ressemblance of (
ST
) with the forced exploration rule of the D-Tracking rule of
(
Garivier and Kaufmann 2016
, §3.1)
in the setting of best-arm identification.
Similarly to the D-Tracking rule, the square root of the square trick (
ST
) is not really important, and
\sqrt{\min_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}}N(z)}
in (
ST
) could be replaced by
(\min_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}}N(z))^{\alpha}
for arbitrary
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

3.3
The exploration test: Testing if information is lacking or not

The last component that we need to discuss is how the algorithm should decide whether information is lacking or not.
By
(
Boone and Maillard 2025
, Corollary 7)
, provided that
\Lambda
is a consistent learning algorithm, for all
M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)
, we have

\mathbf{E}^{M,\Lambda}\left[\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}N_{T}(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\right]\geq\log(T)+\oh(\log(T)).

(11)

Equation

11
translates the idea that consistent learning algorithms must gather enough information in order to “reject” confusing model.
The term
\mathbf{E}^{M,\Lambda}[\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}N_{T}(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z)]
is an expected log-likelihood ratio.
One recognizes in
\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}N_{T}(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))
the dominant term in the likelihood that
M^{\dagger}
is to generate empirical data resembling
M(z)
after
N_{T}(z)
samples at the pair
z\in\mathcal{Z}
for every
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, as stated by Sanov’s theorem.
Overall, a good proxy for the above is to state that
\Lambda
lacks information under history
H_{T}
if

\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)}\left\{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}N_{T}(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\right\}<(1+f(H_{T}))\log(T)

(12)

where
f:\mathcal{H}\to\mathbf{R}_{+}
is some well-chosen vanishing function that accounts for second order errors in Sanov’s theorem.
Therefore, we will require
ECoE
to explore “often enough” whenever information is lacking in the sense of (
12
).

3.4
The algorithmic scheme of
ECoE

Combining the ideas of
Sections

3.1
,
3.2
and
3.3
, we end up with an algorithmic scheme that we name
ECoE
and summarize in
Algorithm

1
.

1:

for
phases
k=1,2,\ldots

do

2:

Estimate optimal pairs
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
out of empirical data;

3:

Compute the exploitation policy
\pi^{+}
as uniform over
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
, see
Section

3.3
, (1);

4:

Compute the exploration policy
\pi^{-}
by estimating
K(M)
as in (
9
);

5:

if

S_{t}
is not recurrent under
\pi^{+}
or lacking information in the sense of (
12
)

then

6:

Explore with
\pi^{-}
one step;

\triangleright
Vanilla exploration

7:

else

if

(
ST
) one component of
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
is sub-visited

then

8:

Explore with
\pi^{-}
one step;

\triangleright
Exploration travel triggered by co-exploration

9:

else

10:

Exploit by iterating
\pi^{+}
until regeneration, see
Section

3.3
(2);

\triangleright
Exploitation

11:

end

if

12:

end

for

Algorithm 1

The
ECoE
framework, informal.

The algorithm
ECoE
works into phases.
For every phase,
ECoE
estimates the set of optimal pairs
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
, an exploitation policy
\pi^{+}
and an exploration policy
\pi^{-}
.
If
ECoE
is outside of the recurrent components of
\pi^{+}
, it explores.
Otherwise,
ECoE
checks if it is lacking information, in which case it explores by iterating
\pi^{-}
once.
Otherwise,
ECoE
checks if information is unbalanced on
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
, in which case it tries to reach the least visited component using the exploration policy
\pi^{-}
—it would be better to use a specifically designed policy
\pi^{\pm}
that travels within the environment more efficiently than
\pi^{-}
, and this is definitively something to take into consideration for any serious implementation of
ECoE
, but the regret analysis will be easier this way.
Otherwise, the current amount of information is considered to be enough everywhere, and
ECoE
exploits by playing
\pi^{+}
until regeneration.

4
Estimating the regret lower bound
K(M)
out of noise

There is a fundamental issue with the algorithmic scheme
ECoE
described in
Algorithm

1
.

Algorithm

1
aims for asymptotically optimal regret by approximating the regret lower bound frontally.
At the beginning of every phase,
ECoE
estimates the optimal pairs
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
that it later splits into communicating components, computes an exploitation policy and computes an exploration policy by estimating
K(M)
.
This is where the issue lies: Obviously,
ECoE
does not have access to
M
, so all these estimations must be done out of empirical data.

The naive approach is to estimate
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
and
K(M)
via the maximum likelihood estimator of
M
, i.e., as
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(\hat{M})
and
K(\hat{M})
respectively.
This approach fails completely.
Indeed, the set of optimal pairs
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
and the regret lower bound
K(M)
are both discontinuous functions of
M
and
M\approx\hat{M}
does not guarantee that
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)\approx\mathcal{Z}^{**}(\hat{M})
nor
K(M)\approx K(\hat{M})
.
So, in order to estimate optimal pairs
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
and the regret lower bound
K(M)
out of the empirical estimate
\hat{M}
, we need smooth approximates of these two; This is the aim of this section.
We begin by describing the discontinuity issue in
Section

4.1
, then define regularized versions of
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
and
K(M)
in
Sections

4.2
and
4.3
.
The well-behavior of our regularized lower bound is described in
Section

4.4
.

4.1
Difficulties due to the discontinuity of the lower bound

The discontinuity of the lower bound is due to the discontinuity of
\mathcal{Z}^{*}(M)
, the set of weakly optimal pairs (
Definition

1
), therefore producing discontinuities of optimal pairs
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
and of the confusing set
\mathrm{Cnf}(M)
.
To be fair, such discontinuities are already present in the simpler settings of multi-armed bandits and ergodic Markov decision processes.
Yet, nobody ever complained about it.
As a matter of fact, algorithms such as
IMED
of
Honda and Takemura 2015
,
KLUCB
of
Cappé et al. 2013
or
TS
of
Thompson 1933
;
Agrawal and Goyal 2012
;
Kaufmann et al. 2012
(for bandits) and
IMED-RL

Pesquerel and Maillard 2022
(for ergodic Markov decision processes) all are asymptotically optimal and none need to handle the discontinuity of
K(M)
like we do in this work.
But because of the design of
ECoE
, we have no choice.
Indeed,
ECoE
requires the computation of
K(M)
and
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
for every of its three components.
For exploration purposes,
ECoE
approximates a near-optimal exploration policy using (
9
) via the computation of
K(M)
;
For co-exploration purposes,
ECoE
explicitly needs
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
to balance the visits counts on components of
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
via the square trick (
ST
);
For exploitation purposes,
ECoE
explicitly needs
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
because its exploitation policy is uniform on
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
.

So, these discontinuities must be dealt with by regularizing
K(M)
and
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
.
In
Section

4.1.1
, we discuss an example of discontinuity to motivate our regularization solution.
Especially, we stress that it all boils down to the smoothing of
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
.
The regularization principle is stated in
Section

4.1.2
and is extensively discussed in the next
Section

4.2
.

4.1.1
An example of discontinuity with deterministic transtions

In this paragraph, we provide an explicit example with a discontinuity of
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
and of
K(M)
, see
Figure

2
.

M
1
2
\boldsymbol{*}
\boldsymbol{\dagger}
\boldsymbol{\ddagger}
\boldsymbol{\lx@sectionsign}
0.5
0.5
0.5
0.1
M_{\theta}
1
2
\boldsymbol{*}
\boldsymbol{\dagger}
\boldsymbol{\ddagger}
\boldsymbol{\lx@sectionsign}
0.5-\theta
0.5-\theta
0.5
0.1

Figure 2:

An example of discontinuity of the regret lower bound.
The displayed transitions are deterministic and the labels represent the means of the attached Bernoulli rewards.
Actions are distinguished by unique symbols for better readability of the contraction.
Optimal pairs are colored in
red
.

In
Figure

2
, we present two models
M
and
M_{\theta}
that are statistically indistinguishable when
\theta\to 0
.
On
M\equiv M_{0}
, the optimal pairs are
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)=\{(1,*),(2,\dagger),(2,\ddagger)\}
and are displayed in red.
On
M_{\theta}
for
\theta>0
, the optimal pairs are
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M_{\theta})=\{(2,\dagger)\}
, also displayed in red.
So, we observe a discontinuity of
\mathcal{Z}^{**}
at
\theta=0
.
Note that this discontinuity drastically changes the way the model can be navigated.
For
M\equiv M_{0}
, the state
1
can be reached at any time with zero-cost but for
M_{\theta}
, it can only be reached by playing sub-optimal pairs.
In addition, this discontinuity of optimal pairs produces a discontinuity in confusing sets, since
M_{\theta}\notin\mathrm{Cnf}(M)
but
M\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M_{\theta})
.
Taking the ambient set
\mathcal{M}:=\{M_{\theta}:\theta\in[-0.5,0.5]\}
with Bernoulli rewards, we can derive the regret lower bounds in closed form:

K(M)=\frac{4}{10\penalty\ \mathrm{kl}(0.1,0.5)}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptK(M_{\theta})\underset{\theta\to 0^{+}}{\sim}\frac{\theta}{\mathrm{kl}(0.5-\theta,0.5)}\underset{\theta\to 0^{+}}{\sim}\frac{1}{2\theta}

where
\mathrm{kl}(x,y):=x\log(\frac{x}{y})+(1-x)\log(\frac{1-x}{1-y})
is the Kullback-Leibler divergence between
\mathrm{Ber}(x)
and
\mathrm{Ber}(y)
.
So,
K
is discontinuous at
M\equiv M_{0}
.
Because
M
and
M_{\theta}
are indistinguishable as
\theta\to 0
, it follows that when the underlying model is
M
, no learner is capable of claiming that the current model isn’t
M_{\theta}
for
\theta>0
with overwhelming confidence.
Yet, in this example, when one is doubting about whether the model is
M_{\theta}
or
M
, it means that the pairs
(1,*)
and
(2,\dagger)
are nearly optimal.
In that case, these pairs can arguably be used to navigate the model more easily while scoring near optimally.
After all, if the true model is some
M_{\theta}
for
\theta>0
rather than
M_{0}
, then the optimality of
(1,*)
and
(2,\dagger)
will be ruled out at some point.

4.1.2
A need for a notion of near-optimal pairs

From this discussion, we make the following observations.

1.

If the optimality of a pair is uncertain, it should be considered optimal: Navigation is made easier and if the pair is actually sub-optimal, its optimality will be rejected eventually.

By following the above principle, we will solve the discontinuity problems due to the navigation constraints as well as those due to the discontinuity of the confusing set.

4.2
Retrieving optimal pairs under noise via the leveling transform

To smooth the regret lower bound, the central technique is to level the Markov decision process to make all near-optimal pairs simultaneously optimal, so that
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
can be retrieved from
M^{\prime}\approx M
.
The idea of the leveling operation (
Definition

9
) is to increase the rewards at pairs that are nearly optimal by just the right amount.
By “nearly optimal”, we mean pairs with Bellman gaps below a given threshold
\epsilon>0
.

Definition 0

(Leveling transform)
.

Let
M\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p,r)
a Markov decision process and fix
\epsilon>0
.
The
\epsilon
-leveled
transform of
M
is the model
[M]_{\epsilon}\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p,[r]_{\epsilon})
with reward function

[r]_{\epsilon}(z):=r(z)+\mathbf{1}(\Delta^{*}(z;M)<\epsilon)\Delta^{*}(z;M).

An example of the leveling operation is provided in
Figure

3
.
For the model
M
on
Figure

3
, the two optimal pairs correspond to the two loops
1\to 1
and
2\to 2
with associated gain
g^{*}=0.5
.
In the noisy copy
M^{\prime}
, only the left loop
1\to 1
is optimal with gain
g^{*}=0.52

After leveling
M^{\prime}
with threshold
\epsilon=0.05
, the right loop
2\to 2
becomes optimal again and the optimal gain is
g^{*}=0.52
.
So,
M
and
[M]_{\epsilon}
have the same optimal pairs and gain optimal policies.
On this example, we observe that leveling with the right threshold recovers the set of optimal pairs and of gain optimal policies.

M
1
2
0.6
0.1
0.5
0.5
M^{\prime}
1
2
0.59
0.12
0.52
0.49
[M^{\prime}]_{0.05}
1
2
0.59
0.12
0.52
0.52

Figure 3:

An example of leveling transform.
On the left, a Markov decision process
M
with two states and two actions per state.
Transitions are deterministic and represented by arrows.
Labels are rewards.
The model
M^{\prime}
is a noisy version of
M
.

Motivated by this example, we define the
\epsilon
-leveled optimal pairs as the optimal pairs of the
\epsilon
-leveled transform.

Definition 0

(Leveled optimal pairs)
.

Let
M\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p,r)
be a Markov decision process and fix
\epsilon>0
.
The
\epsilon
-leveled optimal pairs
of
M
are
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon}(M):=\mathcal{Z}^{**}([M]_{\epsilon})
.

In
Proposition

12
, we show that this leveling operation works as intended.
Provided that
\epsilon>0
is small enough, that
M^{\prime}
is close enough to
M
relatively to a well-chosen metric (
Definition

11
) and that
\epsilon
is large enough relatively to the distance between
M^{\prime}
and
M
, then the optimal structure of the
\epsilon
-leveled transform of
M^{\prime}
is the same as
M
at first order:
[M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon}
and
M
have same gain optimal optimal policies and the same optimal pairs.

Definition 0

(Distances between models)
.

For
M\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p,\nu)
and
M^{\prime}\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
two Markov decision processes with the same pair space, we introduce the following distances:

\displaystyle\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert

\displaystyle:=\max_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\left\{\lvert r^{\prime}(z)-r(z)\rvert+\lVert p^{\prime}(z)-p(z)\rVert_{1}\right\};\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}

\displaystyle\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}

\displaystyle:=\max_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\left\{\lvert r^{\prime}(z)-r(z)\rvert+\lVert p^{\prime}(z)-p(z)\rVert_{1}+\infty\cdot\mathbf{1}(p^{\prime}(z)\not\sim p(z))\right\}.

The operator
\lVert-\rVert^{*}
will be called the
support-aware norm
.

In
Proposition

12
downstream, we express the properties of the leveling transform relatively to two classical quantities of the Markov decision process.
The first is the
gain gap
, given by
\Delta^{g}(M):=\mathrm{dmin}\left\{\lVert g^{*}(M)-g^{\pi}(M)\rVert_{\infty}:\pi\in\Pi\right\}
, that is the smallest gap between the gain of optimal policies and the gain of sub-optimal policies.
The second if the
worst diameter

D^{*}(M):=\max_{\pi\in\Pi}D(\pi;M)
where
D(\pi;M)
is the policy diameter of
\pi
(
Definition

D.1
), that is the largest diameter over all possible policies.

Proposition 12

(Fundamental properties of leveling)
.

For every communicating Markov decision process
M\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p,\nu)
, there exists a quantity
C(M)\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
, that is a rational function of
\Delta^{g}(M)
and
D^{*}(M)
, such that for all
\epsilon>0
and
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
satisfying

\epsilon+2C(M)\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}<\Delta^{g}(M)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptC(M)\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}<\epsilon,

(13)

we have

1.

[M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon}
and
M
have the same gain optimal policies;

2.

\mathcal{Z}^{**}([M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon})=\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
, i.e.,
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon}(M^{\prime})=\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
;

The proof of
Proposition

12
is deferred to
Section

B.1
.

4.3
The regularized lower bound
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)

With the properties of the leveling transform in hand, we can describe the regularized lower bound
K_{\epsilon}(M)
, see
Definition

15
.
We combine three regularization mechanisms.

1.

Approximating the confusing set by the
\epsilon
-leveled confusing set (
Definition

14
) via the leveling transform: While the confusing set cannot be estimated under noise because of discontinuity issues, its leveled version is continuous.

2.

Approximating invariant measures by
\epsilon
-uniform invariant measures (
Definition

13
): Optimizing over uniform invariant measures is encouraged by the formulation (
9
) of
K(M)
and is key to the smoothness properties of the regularized
K_{\epsilon}(M)
.
Also, using fully supported exploration policies guarantees that during exploration, the set of pairs is uniformly covered, which is helpful in the regret analysis.

3.

Adding a strongly convex regularizer to the objective function
\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\Delta^{*}(z)
of (
8
): This makes the optimizer unique.
This one is rather useful than necessary.

Uniform invariant measures and leveled confusing sets are defined as follows.

Definition 0

(Uniform invariant measure)
.

For
\epsilon>0
, an
\epsilon
-uniform invariant measure
of a model
M
is an element
\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
such that
\mu(s,a)\geq\epsilon\sum_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\mu(s,a^{\prime})
for all
s\in\mathcal{S}
.
The set of
\epsilon
-uniform invariant measures is denoted
\mathrm{Inv}(\epsilon;M)
.

Definition 0

(Leveled confusing set)
.

Fix
\mathcal{M}
a space of Markov decision processes and fix
\epsilon>0
.
The
\epsilon
-leveled confusing set
of a communicating model
M\in\mathcal{M}
is

\mathrm{Cnf}(\epsilon;M)\equiv\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M):=\left\{M^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{M}:{M^{\dagger}\gg M,M^{\dagger}=M\mathrm{\penalty\ on\penalty\ }\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon}(M)\atop\mathrm{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\Pi^{*}(M^{\dagger})\cap\Pi^{*}(M)=\emptyset}\right\}.

It can be shown that uniform invariant measures of communicating Markov decision processes are fully supported, see
Section

A.3
.
Also, note that only difference between the leveled confusing set
\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M)
and the vanilla version
\mathrm{Cnf}(M)
is that elements of
\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M)
are required to coincide with
M
on
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon}(M)
rather than
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
.
As one can show that
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon}(M)
satisfies
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon}(M)\supseteq\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
, we have
\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M)\subseteq\mathrm{Cnf}(M)
for all
\epsilon\geq 0
.
Moreover, by
Proposition

12
, for
\epsilon<\Delta^{g}(M)
, we have
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon}(M)=\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
so that
\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M)=\mathrm{Cnf}(M)
.

We have everything in hand to give a complete description of the regularized regret lower bound.
The regularized version (
Definition

15
) uses a
regularization hyperparameter

{\bar{\epsilon}}\equiv({\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}})\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{3}
to tune the different regularization components (I–III).
(I) The first
{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}
is the
leveling regularizer
and tunes the leveling threshold used to estimate the confusing set;
(II) The second
\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}
is the
uniformization regularizer
and tunes how uniform are the invariant measures that we are optimizing over; and
(III) The third
\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}
is the
convexification regularizer
that makes the objective function
\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}
-strongly convex.

Definition 0

(Regularized regret lower bound)
.

Fix
\mathcal{M}
a space of Markov decision processes and fix
{\bar{\epsilon}}\equiv({\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}})\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{3}
a regularization hyperparameter.
The
{\bar{\epsilon}}
-regularized regret lower bound
is the solution
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)\in[0,\infty]
of the optimization problem below:

\inf\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\Delta^{*}(z)+\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\lVert\mu\rVert_{2}^{2}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathrm{s.t.}\hskip 10.00002pt\begin{cases}&\displaystyle\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}};M),\quad\mathrm{and}\\
&\displaystyle\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}({\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}};M)}\left\{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\right\}\geq 1.\end{cases}\hskip-3.30002pt

Observe that
K_{{\bar{\epsilon}}}(M)
is the solution of a convex optimization problem, where the objective function is
\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}
-strongly convex and the constraints spawn a closed convex cone of
\mathbf{R}_{+}^{\mathcal{Z}}
.
Using these properties, we show in
Sections

B.3.1
and
B.3.2
that
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)<\infty
and that there exists an optimizer in
\mathrm{Inv}(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}};M)
.
When the convexification is strict with
\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}>0
, that optimizer is unique, called the
{\bar{\epsilon}}
-optimal exploration measure
and denoted
\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}
.

4.4
Properties of the regularized lower bound

In this paragraph, we overview the different properties of the regularized regret lower bound.
In
Proposition

16
, we state that
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
converges to
K(M)
as
{\bar{\epsilon}}\to 0
, meaning that the regularized lower bound is a good approximation of the true lower bound when the regularizer is small enough.
In
Proposition

17
, we state that the associated optimizer
\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}
converges to a special optimizer of
K(M)
(in the formulation of (
8
)) as the components of the regularizer
{\bar{\epsilon}}
vanish at the right speed; In particular, the uniformization regularizer must vanish faster than the convexification regularizer.

Proposition 16

(Approximation of
K(M)
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a space of Markov decision processes.
For all
M\in\mathcal{M}
that is communicating, we have

K(M)\leq K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)\leq K(M)+\OH\left(\lVert\epsilon\rVert\right)\hskip 10.00002pt\mathrm{as}\hskip 10.00002pt\lVert{\bar{\epsilon}}\rVert\to 0.

Proposition 17

(Convergence of
\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a space of Markov decision processes and let
M\in\mathcal{M}
be a communicating model.
Let
\mu^{*}(M)\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
be the (unique) optimizer of
K(M)
in the sense of (
8
) that further minimizes
\lVert\mu\rVert_{2}
among optimizers.
We have

\left\lVert\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)-\mu^{*}(M)\right\rVert_{2}\to 0\hskip 10.00002pt\mathrm{as}\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}}{\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}}+\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}+{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}\to 0.

More precise statements of
Proposition

16
and
Proposition

17
, together with proofs, can be bound in
Section

B.3
, see
Proposition

B.20
and
Proposition

B.18
respectively.

Regarding the continuity properties of
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
with respect to
M
, we add further assumptions on the structure of the ambient space of Markov decision processes
\mathcal{M}
.
Firstly, we assume that
\mathcal{M}
is a space of models with Bernoulli rewards (
3
).
Doing so, the simple stared norm
\lVert-\rVert^{*}
from
Definition

11
is adequate and we do not run into topological troubles—the result is probably easy to generalize to single parameter exponential families, but we have chosen to keep everything as simple as possible.
Secondly, we assume that
\mathcal{M}
is in product form
\mathcal{M}\equiv\prod_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{z}\times\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{z})
(
4
), where
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{z}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{z}=(0,1)
.
That is,
\mathcal{M}
is a product and all rewards are
(0,1)
.
Under these two assumptions, we show in
Proposition

18
that
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
and
\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
are both continuous relatively to the topology induced by
\lVert-\rVert^{*}
.

Assumption 3
.

For all
M\in\mathcal{M}
,
M
has Bernoulli rewards.

Assumption 4
.

The space
\mathcal{M}
is of the form
\mathcal{M}\equiv\prod_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{z}\times(0,1))
where
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{z}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
.

Proposition 18

(Continuity of
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
and
\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a space of Markov decision processes and let
M\in\mathcal{M}
be a communicating model.
Assume that rewards are Bernoulli (
3
) and that
\mathcal{M}
is in product form (
4
).
Let
{\bar{\epsilon}}\equiv({\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}})\in(\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*})^{3}
be a positive regularization hyperparameter.
If
\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}<\Delta^{g}(M)
, then, in
\mathcal{M}
, we have

K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)=\lim_{\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}\to 0}K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M^{\prime})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)=\lim_{\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}\to 0}\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M^{\prime})

A more precise statement can be found in
Section

B.3
, see
Proposition

B.21
.

Combining
Propositions

16
,
17
and
18
, it means that given an approximation
\hat{M}
of
M
and a regularizer
{\bar{\epsilon}}\ll 1
such that
\lVert\hat{M}-M\rVert^{*}\ll\lVert{\bar{\epsilon}}\rVert
, we have
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(\hat{M})\approx K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)\approx K(M)
and
\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(\hat{M})\approx\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)\approx\mu^{*}(M)
.
Accordingly, we can estimate the lower bound
K(M)
and a good exploration policy out of empirical data.

Now, we can provide a complete description of
ECoE
.

5
ECoE*
, an algorithm with asymptotically optimal regret

For the clarity of the exposition, we focus on an instance of
ECoE
that ignores the computational weight of its subroutines.
Ideally,
ECoE
eventually estimate the regret lower bound
K(M)
, an optimal exploration measure
\mu^{*}
and check dynamically when information is lacking.
All three problems are computationally difficult (NP-hard in some cases) and solving them requires a dedicate care that is too heavy to carry for a first regret analysis.
That is why, for this “oracle-based” version of
ECoE
called
ECOE*
, we shall assume that we are able to compute the regret lower bound
K(-)
, optimal exploration measures
\mu^{*}
and to run exploration tests (
12
) just by snapping fingers.

The pseudo-code of
ECoE*
is provided in
Algorithm

2
.

Parameters:

A family of regularization parameters functions
{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{tst}}
;

1:

Initialize
\mathcal{T}^{-},\mathcal{T}^{\pm},\mathcal{T}^{+},\mathcal{T}^{+}_{0}
and
\mathcal{T}^{!}
to
\emptyset
;

2:

Initialize
t\leftarrow 1
;

3:

for

k=1,2,\ldots

do

4:

Set
t_{k}\leftarrow t
;

5:

Update floored hyperparameters
{\bar{\epsilon}}^{\prime}(t)
, see (
17
);

6:

Update maximum likelihood estimator
\hat{M}_{t}
for
M
;

7:

Update co-exploration structure
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}^{\prime}(t)}(\hat{M}_{t})
, split the structure into components
\mathfrak{Z}_{t}:=(\mathcal{Z}_{t}^{1},\ldots,\mathcal{Z}_{t}^{c(t)})
, and let
i(t)
be the component containing
S_{t}
(current component);

8:

Update exploitation policy
\pi_{t_{k}}^{+}
as given by (
14
);

9:

Update exploration measure
\mu_{t_{k}}\leftarrow\mu_{{\bar{\epsilon}}^{\prime}(t)}^{*}(\hat{M}_{t})
by computing
K_{{\bar{\epsilon}}^{\prime}(t)}(\hat{M}_{t})
using an oracle;

10:

Update exploration policy by setting
\pi^{-}_{t_{k}}(a|s)\propto\mu_{t_{k}}(s,a)
;

11:

Update skeleton
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{skel}}_{t}\leftarrow\{z\in\mathcal{Z}:N_{t}(z)\geq\log^{2}(t)\}
;

12:

if

S_{t}
is not recurrent under
\pi_{t}^{+}
in
\hat{M}_{t}
or
\mathrm{GLR}(H_{t})
holds, see (
16
)
then

13:

Play
A_{t}\sim\pi_{t}^{-}(-|S_{t})
;

\triangleright

vanilla exploration

14:

Increment
t\leftarrow t+1
, add
t-1
in
\mathcal{T}^{-}
;

15:

else

if

(
ST
)

\penalty\ (\min\{N_{t}(z):z\in\mathcal{Z}_{t}^{i(t)}\})^{1/2}\geq\min\{N_{t}(z):z\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}^{\prime}(t)}(\hat{M}_{t})\}

then

16:

Play
A_{t}\sim\pi_{t}^{-}(-|S_{t})
;

\triangleright

co-exploration

17:

Increment
t\leftarrow t+1
, add
t-1
in
\mathcal{T}^{-}
and in
\mathcal{T}^{\pm}
;

18:

else

19:

Add
t
in
\mathcal{T}_{0}^{+}
;

\triangleright

exploitation until regeneration

20:

repeat

21:

Play
A_{t}\sim\pi_{t}^{+}(-|S_{t})
;

22:

Increment
t\leftarrow t+1
;

23:

if

S_{t}\notin\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{Z}_{t}^{i(t)})

then
add
t-1
in
\mathcal{T}^{!}
and
break
;

\triangleright

transition discovery

24:

else
add
t-1
in
\mathcal{T}^{+}
;

25:

until

S_{t}=S_{t_{k}}
;

\triangleright

regeneration

26:

end

if

27:

end

for

Algorithm 2

ECoE*
, an instantiation of
ECoE
(
Algorithm

1
)

5.1
Overview of
ECoE*

First,
ECoE*
requires a regularization hyperparameter
{\bar{\epsilon}}\equiv({\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{tst}}):\mathbf{N}\to\mathbf{R}_{+}^{4}
that is a slowly decreasing function.
This hyperparameter is essential to the regularization of the lower bound
K(M)
, that is discontinuous in
M
and that cannot be estimated under noise.
In comparison to the regularization of
K(M)
in
Definition

15
, note the presence of a fourth hyperparameter
\epsilon_{\mathrm{tst}}
, called the
test hyperparameter
, that controls the power of exploration tests (see
Section

3.1
).
The choice of
{\bar{\epsilon}}
is discussed in
Section

5.2
.

Algorithm

2
works in phases
k=1,2,3,\ldots
that are very short.

At the beginning of a phase, it first updates the regularization parameter to a floored regularization
\epsilon^{\prime}
described in
Section

5.2
and (
17
).
Then, it updates the maximum likelihood estimator for
M
, denoted
\hat{M}_{t}
, as described in
Section

5.3
and (
19
), and computes the leveled optimal pairs
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}^{\prime}}(\hat{M}_{t})
using the leveling threshold of
{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}^{\prime}(t)
and deduces an exploitation policy
\pi_{t}^{+}
and an exploration policy
\pi_{t}^{-}
.
As motivated in
Section

3.2
,
ECoE*
chooses its exploitation policy as the policy playing leveled optimal pairs uniformly at random, if any, and actions uniformly at random if no element of
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}^{\prime}}(\hat{M}_{t})
can be played right away, i.e.,

\pi_{t}^{+}(-|s):=\begin{cases}\mathrm{Unif}\left(\{a\in\mathcal{A}(s):(s,a)\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}^{\prime}(t)}(\hat{M}_{t})\}\right)&\text{if $s\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}^{\prime}(t)}(\hat{M}_{t}))$}\\
\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{A}(s))&\text{if $s\notin\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}^{\prime}(t)}(\hat{M}_{t}))$}\end{cases}

(14)

where we wrote
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}):=\{s\in\mathcal{S}:\exists a\in\mathcal{A}(s),(s,a)\in\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\}
for
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
.
The exploration policy of
ECoE*
is the policy induced by the measure achieving
K_{{\bar{\epsilon}}(t)}(\hat{M}_{t})
, i.e.,

\pi_{t}^{-}(a|s):=\frac{\mu_{{\bar{\epsilon}}^{\prime}(t)}^{*}(s,a;\hat{M}_{t})}{\sum_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\mu_{{\bar{\epsilon}}^{\prime}(t)}^{*}(s,a^{\prime};\hat{M}_{t})}

(15)

where
\mu^{*}_{{\bar{\epsilon}}^{\prime}(t)}
is the unique probability invariant measure of
\hat{M}_{t}
achieving
K_{{\bar{\epsilon}}^{\prime}(t)}(\hat{M}_{t})
.
Then, the algorithm decides whether

(1)
it
explores
, i.e., plays
\pi_{t}^{-}
once to gather information on presumed sub-optimal pairs; or

(2)
it
coexplores
, i.e., plays
\pi_{t}^{-}
to reach a sub-visited components of nearly optimal pairs; or

(3)
it
exploits
, i.e., plays
\pi_{t}^{+}
to score as much as possible.
More precisely:

(0)
By-default exploration
(t\in\mathcal{T}^{-}
)
:
If the algorithm is not on a component of the set of leveled optimal pairs
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}^{\prime}}(\hat{M}_{t})
, it iterates
\pi_{t}^{-}
and ends the phase.
Otherwise, the algorithm moves to the exploration GLR test.

(1)
Exploration
(t\in\mathcal{T}^{-}
)
:
Following the ideas in
Section

3.3
, the algorithm checks first if there is a lack of information on sub-optimal pairs by running a
generalized log-likelihood ratio test
(GLR) that is truncated to a
skeleton

\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{skel}}_{t}
, that contains the pairs visited more than
\log^{2}(t)
times.
The GLR test for exploration is
\mathrm{GLR}(H_{t})
given by

\mathrm{GLR}(H_{t}):\exists M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(\hat{M}_{t})\text{\penalty\ s.t}\begin{cases}M^{\dagger}=\hat{M}_{t}\text{\penalty\ on\penalty\ }\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{skel}}_{t}\cup\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}^{\prime}(t)}(\hat{M}_{t});\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\\
\displaystyle\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}N_{t}(z)\mathrm{KL}(\hat{M}_{t}(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\leq(1+\epsilon_{\mathrm{tst}}^{\prime}(t))\log(t)\end{cases}\!

(16)

where
\epsilon_{\mathrm{tst}}:\mathbf{N}\to\mathbf{R}_{+}
is a well-chosen vanishing function.
If (
16
) is true, the algorithm iterates
\pi_{t}^{-}
once and the phase ends.
Otherwise, the algorithm deals with co-exploration.

(2)
CoExploration
(t\in\mathcal{T}^{\pm}\cap\mathcal{T}^{-}
)
:
As motivated in
Section

3.2
, to avoid missing information on nearly optimal pairs, the algorithms synchronizes the visit counts on all the communicating components of leveled optimal pairs
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}^{\prime}}(\hat{M}_{t})
.
Following the square trick (
ST
), if one component is visited less than the square root of the current component, the algorithm decides that this sub-visited component should be reached and attempts to do so by explorating: It iterates
\pi_{t}^{-}
once and the phase ends.
The idea is to explore by the meantime and to reconsider the exploration-exploitation trade-off once another component of
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}^{\prime}}(\hat{M}_{t})
has been reached.
Otherwise, the amount of information on components of
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}^{\prime}}(\hat{M}_{t})
is considered to be balanced enough, and the algorithm moves to exploitation.

(3)
Exploitation

(t\in\mathcal{T}^{+})
:
The algorithm iterates
\pi_{t}^{+}
until regeneration.

(!)
Panic
(t\in\mathcal{T}^{!})
:
When the algorithm iterates the policy
\pi_{t}^{+}
until regeneration, the regeneration may never come if the algorithm is wrong about the support of transition kernels.
In that case, at some point, the algorithm will observe a transition
(S_{t},A_{t},S_{t+1})
that it has never seen before, with
S_{t+1}
outside of the current component of estimated optimal pairs.
We call these time-instants
panic times
.
The algorithm deals with them by aborting the current phase right away.

5.2
The regularization hyperparameter
{\bar{\epsilon}}

The regularization hyperparameter
{\bar{\epsilon}}\equiv({\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{tst}}):\mathbf{N}\to(\mathbf{R}_{+})^{4}
regularizes the estimation of optimal pairs, the computation of the exploration policy and the exploration GLR tests.
Every component of
{\bar{\epsilon}}
has a specific purpose and although they are all maps
\mathbf{N}\to\mathbf{R}_{+}
, they do not take the same input.

•

{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}
is the
leveling
parameter: It determines the threshold beyond which a sub-optimal state-action pair of
\hat{M}_{t}
is considered optimal because it is near-optimal enough;
It is a function of the global time
t
;

•

\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}
is the
uniformization
parameter: It makes sure that exploration is slightly uniform and is key bounding the probability that
ECoE*
blunders the estimation of the hidden model, or that
ECoE*
purposely avoids regions of the environment that should be explored, but that aren’t because the empirical data is completely off;
It is a function of the exploration time
\lvert\mathcal{T}^{-}\rvert
;

•

\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}
is the
convexification
parameter: It comes from the way the regret lower bound is being regularized (
Definition

15
) and makes sure that the estimated exploration measure converges;
It is a function of the exploration time
\lvert\mathcal{T}^{-}\rvert
;

•

\epsilon_{\mathrm{tst}}
is the
test power
parameter: It controls the power of the exploration test that regulates second order errors in exploration tests.
It is a function of the global time
t
;

Note that not all regularizers take the same input, as some are functions of the global time
t
in
Algorithm

2
and others are functions of the number of exploration phases
\lvert\mathcal{T}^{-}\rvert
.
Also, the regularization is
floored
into
{\bar{\epsilon}}^{\prime}
during play to stabilize the algorithm:

\begin{gathered}{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}^{\prime}(t):={\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}(t)\qquad\epsilon_{\mathrm{tst}}^{\prime}(t):=\epsilon_{\mathrm{tst}}(t)\\
\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}^{\prime}(t):=\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}\left(2^{\lfloor\log_{2}\lvert\mathcal{T}^{-}\rvert\rfloor}\right),\qquad\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}^{\prime}(t):=\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\left(2^{\lfloor\log_{2}\lvert\mathcal{T}^{-}\rvert\rfloor}\right).\end{gathered}

(17)

Moreover, for
ECoE*
to be asymptotically optimal, the regularization hyperparameter
{\bar{\epsilon}}
must be carefully be tuned.
We move the technical discussion on the set of assumptions (
6
) that
{\bar{\epsilon}}
must satisfy to the appendix.
For now, we will assume that the regularization hyperparameters are chosen as follows:

\begin{gathered}{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}(t):=\frac{1}{\max\left(1,\log\log(t)\right)},\qquad\epsilon_{\mathrm{tst}}(t):=\frac{1}{\max\left(1,\log\log(t)\right)},\\
\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}(m):=\frac{1}{\max\left(1,\log\log(m)\right)},\qquad\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}(m):=\frac{1}{\max\left(1,\log(m)\right)}.\end{gathered}

(18)

We can finally provide the regret guarantees of
ECoE*
.

5.3
Maximum likelihood estimator of
M
and structure of the ambient space

The maximum likelihood estimator of
M
under history
H_{t}
is the unique model
\hat{M}_{t}\equiv(\mathcal{Z},\hat{p}_{t},\hat{\nu}_{t})
that maximizing the likelihood of
H_{t}
.
Formally,

\hat{M}_{t}\in\argmax_{M^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{M})}\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\hat{p}_{t}(S_{i+1}|Z_{i})\hat{p}_{t}(R_{i}|Z_{i}).

(19)

It is preferable for the optimizer in (
19
) to be unique and easy to compute.
Also, the regret analysis of
ECoE*
requires
\hat{M}_{t}
to be well-behaved relatively to
M
, with
\support(\hat{p}_{t})\subseteq\support(p)
for instance.
For that purpose, we will strengthen our set of assumptions (
4
) on the structure of the ambient space of models
\mathcal{M}
, see
5
below.

Assumption 5
.

The space
\mathcal{M}
is of the form
\mathcal{M}\equiv\prod_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}(\mathcal{P}_{z}\times(0,1))
where
\mathcal{P}_{z}=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
or
\mathcal{P}_{z}=\{p(z)\}
for some
p(z)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
.
We write
\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{M}):=\prod_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}(\mathcal{P}_{z}\times[0,1])
.

Under
5
and for
M\in\mathcal{M}
, the maximum likelihood estimator
\hat{M}_{t}
is the model with reward function and transition kernel given by

\hat{r}_{t}(z)=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{t-1}R_{i}\mathbf{1}(Z_{i}=z)}{N_{t}(z)}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\hat{p}_{t}(s|z)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{t-1}\mathbf{1}(Z_{i}=z,S_{i+1}=s)}{N_{t-1}(z)}&\text{if $\mathcal{P}_{z}=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})$;}\\
\displaystyle p(z)&\text{if $\mathcal{P}_{z}=\{p(z)\}$.}\end{cases}

That is,
\hat{r}_{t}
is the empirical mean of the rewards observed when playing
z
and
\hat{p}_{t}(s|z)
is the number of transitions to
s
observed when playing
z
.
Note that
\hat{M}_{t}(z)\ll M(z)
for all
t\geq 1
a.s. as soon as
N_{t}(z)\geq 1
.

5.4
The regret guarantees of
ECoE*

ECoE*
tuned with
{\bar{\epsilon}}
chosen as in
Equation

18
is asymptotically optimal.

Theorem 19

(Asymptotic optimality)
.

Assume that the regularization hyperparameter
{\bar{\epsilon}}
is chosen as in (
18
), and that the ambient space
\mathcal{M}
has Bernoulli rewards (
3
) and strong product structure (
5
).
For all
M\in\mathcal{M}
communicating and all initial state
s_{0}\in\mathcal{S}
,
ECoE*
satisfies

\textrm{Reg}(T;M,\texttt{ECoE*},s_{0})\leq K(M)\log(T)+\oh(\log(T))

as
T\to\infty
.
Accordingly,
ECoE*
is asymptotically optimal.

The proof of
Theorem

6
is difficult and is deferred to
Appendix

C
.
We bound the expected regret
\textrm{Reg}(T;M,\Lambda,s_{0})
by upper-bounding the pseudo regret
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Delta^{*}(Z_{t})
in expectation.
As
\Delta^{*}(Z_{t})>0
only for
Z_{t}\notin\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
, this is about upper-bounding the number of visits
N_{T}(z)
in expectation for sub-optimal pairs
z\notin\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
.
We show that there exists a special optimizer
\mu^{*}\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
of (
8
) such that, for all sub-optimal pairs
z\notin\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
, we have

\mathbf{E}\left[N_{T}(z)\right]\leq\mu^{*}(z)\log(T)+\oh(\log(T))

when
T\to\infty
.
Because
\mu^{*}
is an optimizer of the optimization problem (
8
) associated to
K(M)
, it immediately follows that
ECoE*
is asymptotically optimal.

Therefore, the regret lower bound of
Theorem

6
is tight.

6
Open directions and future research

In this paper, we have shown that under reasonable assumptions on the space of ambient Markov decision processes
\mathcal{M}
, then

\min_{\Lambda\text{\penalty\ consistent}}\sup_{\scriptstyle M\in\mathcal{M}\atop\scriptstyle\text{communicating}}\limsup_{T\to\infty}\left\{\frac{\textrm{Reg}(T;M,\Lambda)}{\log(T)}-K(M)\right\}=0.

In other words, we have provided a learning algorithm
\Lambda
with
\textrm{Reg}(T;M,\Lambda)\leq K(M)\log(T)+\oh(\log(T))
for all communicating model
M\in\mathcal{M}
simultaneously, while every reasonable (i.e., consistent) learning algorithm
\Lambda^{\prime}
satisfies
\textrm{Reg}(T;M,\Lambda^{\prime})\geq K(M)\log(T)+\oh(\log(T))
.
Accordingly, our algorithm is universally asymptotically optimal with
\textrm{Reg}(T;M,\Lambda)=K(M)\log(T)+\oh(\log(T))
and the regret lower bound of
Boone and Maillard 2025
is tight.

From there, many directions open up.

Tractable algorithms.

Our algorithm
ECoE*
is not easy to implement, because the computation of
K(M)
is difficult.
In fact, even the exploration tests (
16
) are computationaly difficult in general, see
Boone and Maillard 2025
.
Nonetheless, we believe that this computational difficulties can be avoided and that an asymptotically optimal version of
ECoE
that runs in reasonable time is doable—if the size of the pair space stays small, at least.

More general settings.

To simplify the exposition, we have assumed that rewards are Bernoulli (
3
).
This assumption can be dropped for another.
Our proof is likely to hold for reward distributions among a single parameter exponential family, but this is yet to prove.
More interestingly, there is the question of dropping the communicating assumption (
2
) and of dropping the a priori knowledge on the structure of the finite pair space
\mathcal{Z}
(
1
).
Indeed, not only
ECoE
is only designed to work for communicating Markov decision processes, but some a priori knowledge on
\mathcal{Z}
is hard-coded in
\mathcal{M}
, because every element of
\mathcal{M}
has pair space
\mathcal{Z}
.
This raises the following question:

What can we say about the regret lower bound when
\mathcal{M}
is the space of all Markov decision processes with Bernoulli rewards and
arbitrary
finite state space?

We believe that the above is essentially the same problem as dealing with weakly communicating Markov decision processes with fixed finite pair space.

All this is left for future work.

Appendix A
Additional notions on Markov decision processes

In this appendix, we provide various complementary results on Markov decision processes.
In
Section

A.1
, we provide a refresher on Bellman optimality that comes back for the proof of
Proposition

12
.
In
Section

A.2
, we define the worst diameter and the gain gap.
The last consequent
Section

A.3
is dedicated to the analysis of invariant measures (
Definition

4
) of Markov decision processes.

Definition A.0

(Definite minimum of a model)
.

Let
M\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p,\nu)
be a Markov decision process with Bernoulli rewards.
Its
definite minimum
is
\mathrm{dmin}(M):=\min\{\mathrm{dmin}(p),\mathrm{dmin}(\nu)\}

A.1
Refresher on gain, Bellman and bias optimalities

At several places in this paper, we will use the notion of Bellman optimality.
Bellman optimality sits in-between gain and bias optimalities.
We recall its definition below.

Definition A.0

(Bellman optimal)
.

Let
M\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p,r)
be a communicating Markov decision process.
A policy is said
Bellman optimal
, written
\pi\in\Pi^{*}_{\mathrm{Bell}}(M)
, if
\mathrm{sp}(g^{\pi}(M))=0
and
\pi
satisfies the first order Bellman equations:

\forall s\in\mathcal{S},\hskip 10.00002ptg^{\pi}(s;M)+b^{\pi}(s;M)=\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\Big\{r(s,a)+p(s,a)b^{\pi}(M)\Big\}.

(A.1)

In other words, Bellman optimal policies are fixpoints of the Policy Iteration algorithm, see
(
Puterman 2014
, §8.6)
.
It is linked to gain and bias optimalities as follows.

Proposition A.3
.

In the space of communicating Markov decision processes, every Bellman optimal policy is gain optimal, and every bias optimal policy is Bellman optimal;
The converse statements are wrong in general.

A proof of
Proposition

A.3
can be found in
(
Boone 2024
, Theorem I.3)
.

A.2
Worst diameter and gain-gap of a communicating Markov decision process

At several places in this work, we rely on the
worst diameter
and the
gain-gap
of a communicating Markov decision process.
They are defined in this paragraph for further reference.
The worst diameter
D^{*}(M)
is involved in gain deviations inequalities (see
Appendix

D
) that are uniform over the space of policies.
The gain-gap is related to how small the leveling threshold must be so that the leveling transform
[M]_{\epsilon}
is well-behaved, see
Appendix

B
and
Proposition

12
more specifically.

Definition A.0

(Worst diameter)
.

The
worst diameter

D^{*}(M)\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
of a communicating Markov decision process
M
is the largest policy diameter (
Definition

D.1
