) of its deterministic stationary policies, visually,

D^{*}(M):=\max_{\pi\in\Pi}D(\pi;M).

Definition A.0

(Gain gap)
.

The
gain-gap

\Delta^{g}(M)\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
of a communicating Markov decision process
M
is the difference between the optimal gain and the gain of the best sub-optimal policy, visually,

\Delta^{g}(M):=\min_{\pi\notin\Pi^{*}(M)}\lVert g^{*}(M)-g^{\pi}(M)\rVert_{\infty}.

(A.2)

A.3
Invariant measures of Markov decision processes

In this section, we provide a collection of results on the invariant measures of a Markov decision process.
Recall that
\mu
is an
invariant measure
of
M\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p,\nu)
if

\forall s\in\mathcal{S},\hskip 10.00002pt\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\mu(s,a)=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}p(s|z)\mu(z).

We write
\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
.
The set of
probability invariant measures
is written

\mathcal{I}(M):=\mathrm{Inv}(M)\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z}).

(A.3)

An invariant measure
\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
is said
\epsilon
-uniform
(
Definition

13
) if it is the (unique) invariant measure of some
\epsilon
-uniform policy, i.e., if
\mu(s,a)\geq\epsilon\sum_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\mu(s,a^{\prime})
for all
(s,a)\in\mathcal{Z}
.
We write
\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(\epsilon;M)
their space and
\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M):=\mathrm{Inv}(\epsilon;M)\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z})
.

Outline.

In
Section

A.3.1
, we draw the link between invariant measures and the asymptotic behavior of learning algorithms by showing that the normalized visit vector
\frac{1}{t}N_{t}
converges to the space of invariant measures as
t\to\infty
.
Using this property, we show that there exists a stationary randomized policy that covers all pairs uniformly fast with respect to the diameter in
Section

A.3.2
.
In
Section

A.3.3
, we link fully supported randomized policies to fully supported probability invariant measures by describing the natural one-to-one mapping between
\Pi^{\mathrm{SFR}}
and
\bigcup_{\epsilon>0}\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M)
.
In
Section

A.3.4
, we show that elements of
\mathrm{Inv}(M)\equiv\mathrm{Inv}(0;M)
can be approximated with arbitrary precision by elements of
\mathrm{Inv}(\epsilon;M)
as
\epsilon\to 0
.
In
Section

A.3.5
, we provide properties on the minimum and the diameter of
\epsilon
-uniform invariant measures, then deduce a bound of the variations of
\mathcal{I}(M)
when
M
vary.
In the last
Section

A.3.6
, we show that every element of
\mathrm{Inv}(M)
can be written as a convex combination of invariant measures of unichain policies and provide several useful consequences of this result.

A.3.1
Links between invariant measures and learning algorithms

We begin this appendix by showing that the average visit counts
\frac{1}{t}N_{t}
converges to the space of invariant measures almost surely, whatever the learning algorithm is.

Proposition A.6

(Asymptotic flow property of
N_{t}
)
.

Let
M\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p,\nu)
be a communicating Markov decision process and let
\Lambda
be a learning algorithm.
Then
\frac{1}{t}N_{t}\to\mathrm{Inv}(M)
a.s. when
t\to\infty
, i.e., every limit point of
(\frac{1}{t}N_{t})_{t\geq 1}
is an invariant measure with probability one.

Proof.

This follows from a direct computation.
Denote
\hat{\mu}_{t}:=\frac{1}{t}N_{t}
and let
\mu^{*}
be a limit point of
\hat{\mu}_{t}
when
t\to\infty
.
There exists a sequence
t_{n}\to\infty
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\hat{\mu}_{t_{n}}=\mu^{*}
.
Let
s\in\mathcal{S}
.
We check that
\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\mu^{*}(s,a)=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu^{*}(z)p(s|z)
as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\mu^{*}(s,a)

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{=}\frac{1}{t_{n}}\left(\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}(s)}N_{t_{n}}(s,a)+\oh(t_{n})\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ddagger)}{=}\frac{1}{t_{n}}\left(\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}N_{t_{n}}(s|z)+\OH(1)+\oh(t_{n})\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(\lx@sectionsign)}{=}\frac{1}{t_{n}}\left(\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\left(\hat{p}_{t_{n}}(s|z)-p(s|z)\right)N_{t_{n}}(z)+\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}p(s|z)N_{t_{n}}(z)+\oh(t_{n})\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(\$)}{=}\frac{1}{t_{n}}\left(\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}p(s|z)N_{t_{n}}(z)+\oh(t_{n})\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(\#)}{=}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu^{*}(z)p(s|z)

where

(\dagger)
holds by assumption;

(\ddagger)
uses the notation
N_{t}(s|z):=\sum_{i=1}^{t-1}\mathbf{1}(Z_{i}=z,S_{i+1}=s)
and uses the quasi-flow property of
N_{t_{n}}
, which is
\lvert\sum_{a}N_{t_{n}}(s,a)-\sum_{z}N_{t_{n}}(s|z)\rvert\leq 2
;

(\lx@sectionsign)
uses that the empirical transition kernel satisfies
\hat{N}_{t_{n}}(s|z)N_{t_{n}}(z)=N_{t_{n}}(s|z)
by definition;

(\$)
follows from the strong law of large numbers; and

(\#)
holds by definition of
(t_{n})
.

\blacksquare

Corollary A.7
.

Let
M\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p,\nu)
be a communicating Markov decision process.
Let
\Lambda
a learning algorithm such that
\frac{1}{t}N_{t}(z)\to\mu(z)
a.s., for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
.
Then
\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
.

A.3.2
A convenient fast covering policy

In this paragraph, we show that there exists a policy
\pi
that covers all pairs uniformly fast, with
\min(\mu^{\pi})\geq\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert^{-1}D(M)^{-1}
.
This policy is obtained as the limit of average behavior of a non-stationary policy that navigates as fast as possible between pairs in a round-robin fashion, see
Lemma

A.8
below.
The existence of that limit average behavior is guaranteed by the flow properties of the visit vector shown upstream, see
Corollary

A.7
.

This covering policy plays an important part in the approximation of invariant measures by
\epsilon
-uniform invariant measures when
\epsilon\to 0
, see
Theorem

A.11
.
It also provides a simple bound on
K(M)
in terms of
D(M)
, see
Theorem

B.14
.

Lemma A.8

(Fast covering policy)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p,\nu)
a communicating Markov decision process.
There exists a stationary fully-randomized policy
\pi\in\Pi^{\mathrm{SR}}
such that its unique probability invariant measure
\mu^{\pi}
(
Proposition

A.10
) satisfies

\min(\mu^{\pi})\geq\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert D(M)}.

Proof.

Enumerate
\mathcal{Z}
as
\mathcal{Z}=\{z_{0},\ldots,z_{m-1}\}
.
Given
z_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}
, pick
\pi_{i}\in\Pi
a policy achieving

\min_{\pi\in\Pi}\max_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\mathbf{E}_{s}^{M,\pi}\left[\inf\left\{t\geq 1:Z_{t}=z_{i}\right\}\right].

By definition,
\mathbf{E}_{s}^{M,\pi_{i}}[\inf\{t\geq 1:Z_{t}=z_{i}\}]\leq D(M)
for
i=0,\ldots,m-1
.
Consider the non-stationary policy
\Lambda
given by the following pseudo-code:

1:

Set
t\leftarrow 1
;

2:

for

n=0,1,2,\ldots

do

3:

Set
t_{n}\leftarrow t
;

4:

Let
i\leftarrow n\penalty\ \mathrm{mod}\penalty\ m
and
\pi_{n}\leftarrow\pi_{i}
;

5:

Iterate
\pi_{i}
until
Z_{t}=z_{i}
;

6:

end

for

Assume throughout that we iterate
\Lambda
on
M
.

First, we argue that
\frac{1}{t}N_{t}(z)
converges almost-surely as
t\to\infty
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
.

Fix
z\in\mathcal{Z}
.
The idea is that, by design of
\Lambda
, the random states
S_{t_{km+i}}
and
S_{t_{k^{\prime}m+i}}
are identically distributed for
k,k^{\prime}\geq 1
and
i\in\{0,\ldots,m-1\}
.
Denote
\mu_{i}
their common distribution.
Then, for all
k\geq 1
and regardless of
s_{0}\in\mathcal{S}
, we have

\mathbf{E}^{M,\Lambda}_{s_{0}}[t_{km+i+1}-t_{km+i}]=\mathbf{E}^{M,\pi_{i}}_{\mu_{i}}\left[\inf\left\{t\geq 1:Z_{t}=z_{i}\right\}\right]=:T_{i}\in\mathbf{R}.

Denoting
T:=\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}T_{i}
, we deduce from the Strong Law that
t_{n}\sim\frac{nT}{m}
as
n\to\infty
.
Let

\alpha_{i}(z):=\mathbf{E}^{M,\Lambda}\left[\sum_{t=t_{km+i}}^{t_{km+1+i}-1}\mathbf{1}(Z_{t}=z)\right]

the expected number of times the pair
z
is played when
\Lambda
iterates
\pi_{i}
over the episode
n=km+i
for
k\geq 1
.
Denothing
\alpha(z):=\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\alpha_{i}(z)
, we deduce from the Strong Law that
N_{t_{n}}(z)\sim\frac{n\alpha(z)}{m}
as
n\to\infty
.
All together, we obtain

N_{t}(z)\sim\frac{tm}{T}\cdot\frac{\alpha(\alpha)}{m}=\frac{\alpha(z)t}{T}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{a.s.}

as
t\to\infty
.
Hence
\frac{1}{t}N_{t}(z)\sim\frac{\alpha(z)}{T}=:\mu(z)
.

Second, we argue that
(I)
\mu(z)\geq\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert^{-1}D(M)^{-1}
and that
(II)
\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z})
.
The assertion (I) is immediate from the observation that
\alpha_{i}(z_{i})\geq 1
and that
T_{i}\leq D(M)
.
The assertion (II) is a consequence of the flow property of the visit vector
N_{t}
, see
Corollary

A.7
.

Conclude using that the randomized policy induced by
\mu
, which is given by
\pi^{\mu}(a|s):=\mu(s,a)(\sum_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\mu(s,a^{\prime}))^{-1}
, is fully randomized (because
\mu
is fully supported) and has unique probability invariant measure
\mu
(
Proposition

A.10
).

\blacksquare

A.3.3
Links between fully supported policies and fully supported measures

In this paragraph, we show that fully supported invariant measures can be thought as fully supported policies, because
\bigcup_{\epsilon>0}\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M)\cong\Pi^{\mathrm{SFR}}
.
Indeed, in
Proposition

A.10
below, we state that from a fully randomized probability invariant measure
\mu
, we can construct a policy
\pi^{\mu}
and that by iterating that policy, expected visit rates converge to
T\mu(z)+\oh(T)
.
This further encourages the computation of
K(M)
via (
9
) in the first place, because a near optimal solution
\pi\in\Pi^{\mathrm{SFR}}
provides a policy that, if iterated, makes visit rates converge to the optimal rates in the sense of (
8
), hence
\pi
gathers information near optimally.

For later convenience, we provide a notation for the set of invariant measures of a policy.

Definition A.0

(Invariant measures of a policy)
.

Let
M
a Markov decision process.
The set of invariant measures of
\pi\in\Pi
in
M
, denoted
\mathrm{Inv}(\pi;M)
, is the collection of
\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
such that
\mu(s,a)=\pi(a|s)\sum_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\mu(s,a^{\prime})
for all
(s,a)\in\mathcal{Z}
.

It is to be noted that
\mathrm{Inv}(\pi;M)
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{Inv}(M_{\pi})
where
M_{\pi}\equiv(\mathcal{S},p_{\pi},r_{\pi})
is the single-action Markov decision process (hence is a Markov reward process) with kernel
p_{\pi}(s):=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\pi(a|s)p(s,a)
and reward function
r_{\pi}(s):=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\pi(a|s)r(s,a)
, where the isomorphism maps
\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(\pi;M)
to
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathbf{R}^{\mathcal{S}}
given by
\mu^{\prime}(s):=\mu(s,\pi(s))
.

Proposition A.10

(Randomized policies and invariant measures)
.

Assume that
M
is a communicating Markov chain.
There is a one-to-one correspondence between probability invariant measures of full support and fully supported randomized policies
\Pi^{\mathrm{SFR}}
, with

1.

A policy
\pi\in\Pi^{\mathrm{SFR}}
is associated to a unique probability invariant measure
\mu^{\pi}
, given by
\mu^{\pi}(z):=\lim\mathbf{E}_{s_{0}}^{M,\pi}[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}(Z_{t}=z)]
, regardless of the initial state
s_{0}\in\mathcal{S}
;
In other words,
\mathrm{Inv}(\pi;M)=\mathbf{R}_{+}\mu^{\pi}
;

2.

A fully supported invariant measure
\mu
is associated to a unique fully randomized policy
\pi^{\mu}
, given by
\pi^{\mu}(a|s):=\mu(s,a)(\sum_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\mu(s,a^{\prime}))^{-1}
;

3.

We have
\pi^{\mu^{\pi}}=\pi
for all
\pi\in\Pi^{\mathrm{SFR}}
.

The correspondence described in
Proposition

A.10
cannot be generalized easily to non-fully supported invariant measures.
If every probability invariant measure is a probability invariant measure of some policy, it is not the unique one when that policy is multi-chain; And as matter of fact, the iterates of a multi-chain policy
\pi
converge to a specific invariant measure of
\pi
that depend on the initial state.
When policies and invariant measures are of full support, we don’t need to take such considerations into account.

Proof of
Proposition

A.10
.

Assertion (1) is a result about Markov chains.
If
\pi
is fully randomized and
M
is communicating, then it is easy to see that the Markov chain that
\pi
induces on
\mathcal{Z}
is irreducible, so that it has a unique probability invariant measure from classical results, see
(
Levin and Peres 2017
, Corollary 1.17)
.
The fact that this measure is found as the asymptotic presence probability is also standard.

Assertion (2) is immediate, because
\pi^{\mu}
satisfies
\pi^{\mu}(a|s)\geq\epsilon
by definition of
\mathrm{Inv}(\epsilon;M)
.
Then, it is easy to check that
\mu
is an invariant measure of
\pi^{\mu}
; It is the unique one, because the Markov chain induced by
\pi
on
\mathcal{Z}
is irreducible since
\pi^{\mu}
is fully supported and
M
is communicating.

For Assertion (3), we have
\mu^{\pi}(s,a)\propto\pi(a|s)
so
\pi^{\mu^{\pi}}(a|s)=\pi(a|s)
is immediate.

\blacksquare

A.3.4
Approximation of invariant measures with uniform invariant measures

In this paragraph, we show that uniform invariant measures can approximate invariant measures with arbitrary precision.
In
Theorem

A.11
below, we show that provided that the required uniformization
\epsilon
is small enough, an invariant measure can be made
\epsilon
-uniform by blending it with the invariant measure of the fast covering policy of
Lemma

A.8
.

Theorem A.11

(Approximation of invariant measures)
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{I}(M)
be a probability invariant measure.
For all
\epsilon<\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert^{-1}D(M)^{-1}
, there exists
\mu_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M)
such that

\lVert\mu_{\epsilon}-\mu\rVert_{\infty}\leq\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert D(M)\epsilon.

Proof.

Fix
\mu\in\mathcal{I}(M)
a probability invariant measure.
Let
\mu_{c}
be the (unique) probability invariant measure of the fast covering policy given by
Lemma

A.8
, satisfying
\min(\mu_{c})\geq\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert^{-1}D(M)^{-1}
.
As
\mathcal{I}(M)
is obtained as the intersection of finitely many hyperplanes, it is convex.
In particular, for every
\lambda\in[0,1]
,
(1-\lambda)\mu+\lambda\mu_{c}\in\mathcal{I}(M)
and
(1-\lambda)\mu+\lambda\mu_{c}\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{\mathcal{Z}}
.
It follows that

\min\left((1-\lambda)\mu+\lambda\mu_{c}\right)\geq\lambda\min(\mu_{c}).

Let
\lambda_{\epsilon}:=\min(\mu_{c})^{-1}\epsilon
.
Since
\epsilon<\min(\mu_{c})
,
\mu_{\epsilon}:=(1-\lambda_{\epsilon})\mu+\lambda_{\epsilon}\mu_{c}
is a probability invariant measure.
Moreover, we see that for all
(s,a)\in\mu_{\epsilon}
, we have

\mu_{\epsilon}(s,a)\geq\lambda_{\epsilon}\min(\mu_{c})\geq\epsilon\geq\epsilon\sum_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\mu_{\epsilon}(s,a^{\prime})

so that
\mu_{\epsilon}
is
\epsilon
-uniform.
Moreover, we have
\lVert\mu_{\epsilon}-\mu\rVert_{\infty}=\lambda_{\epsilon}\lVert\mu-\mu_{c}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\lambda_{\epsilon}
.

\blacksquare

A.3.5
Properties of policies induced by uniform invariant measures

In this paragraph, we provide a few properties on uniform probability invariant measures.
In
Lemma

A.12
, we provide a lower bound of the minimums of
\epsilon
-uniform invariant measures as well as the diameters of their induced policies.
In
Lemma

A.13
, we show that the projection of
\mu\in\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M)
onto
\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M^{\prime})
is close to
\mu
, leading to a bound on the Hausdorff distance between
\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M)
and
\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M^{\prime})
in
Corollary

A.14
.
In turn,
Corollary

A.14
plays an important part in proving that
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M^{\prime})\to K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
when
\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}\to 0
, see
Proposition

B.21
.

Lemma A.12
.

Let
M\in\mathcal{M}
be a communicating Markov decision process and
\epsilon>0
.
For all
\epsilon
-uniform invariant measure
\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(\epsilon;M)
, the following holds:

1.

We have
\min(\mu)\geq\epsilon^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}\mathrm{dmin}(p)^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}\lVert\mu\rVert_{1}
;

2.

Its induced policy has small diameter with
D(\pi_{\mu};M)\leq\epsilon^{1-\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}\mathrm{dmin}(p)^{1-\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}(\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1)
.

Proof.

Up to normalizing
\mu
, we assume that
\lVert\mu\rVert_{1}=1
.

We start with assertion (1).
Pick
z\in\mathcal{Z}
and let
s\in\mathcal{S}
maximizing
\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\mu(s,a)
.
In particular, we have
\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\mu(s,a)\geq\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert^{-1}
.
Because
M
is communicating, there exists a path from
s
to
z
of length at most
\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert
, i.e.,
(s_{1},a_{1},\ldots,s_{n},a_{n})
such that
(I)
s_{1}=s
,
(II)
(s_{n},a_{n})=z
,
(III)
p(s_{i+1}|s_{i},a_{i})>0
for
i=1,\ldots,n-1
, and
(IV)
n\leq\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert
.
Then,

\displaystyle\mu(z)

\displaystyle\geq\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{p(s_{i+1}|s_{i},a_{i})\mu(s_{i},a_{i})}{\sum_{a^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{A}(s_{i})}\mu(s_{i},a^{\prime}_{i})}\right)\sum_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\mu(s,a^{\prime})

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{=}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}p(s_{i+1}|s_{i},a_{i})\pi_{\mu}(a_{i}|s_{i})\right)\sum_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\mu(s,a^{\prime})

\displaystyle\overset{(\ddagger)}{\geq}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}p(s_{i+1}|s_{i},a_{i})\epsilon\right)\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}\geq\frac{\mathrm{dmin}(p)^{n-1}\epsilon^{n-1}}{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}\geq\frac{\mathrm{dmin}(p)^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}\epsilon^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}}{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}

where

(\dagger)
follows by definition of the induced policy, and

(\ddagger)
uses that
\pi_{\mu}
is
\epsilon
-uniform and
\sum_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\mu(s,a^{\prime})\geq\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert^{-1}
.

We continue with assertion (2).
Let
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
and denote
T(s,s^{\prime}):=\mathbf{E}_{s}^{\pi_{\mu},M}[\inf\{t\geq 0:S_{t}=s^{\prime}\}]
the reaching time to
s^{\prime}
from
s
.
Since
\pi_{\mu}
is recurrent, by definition, we have
D(\pi_{\mu};M)=\max_{s\neq s^{\prime}}T(s,s^{\prime})
.
Let
(s,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}^{2}
achieving the maximum of
T
.
Using Markov’s property, we find that

\displaystyle T(s,s^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1+\left(1-\mathbf{P}_{s}^{\pi_{\mu},M}\left(\exists t\leq\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert:S_{t}=s^{\prime}\right)\right)T(s,s^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1+\left(1-\mathrm{dmin}(p)^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}\epsilon^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}\right)T(s,s^{\prime})

where
(\dagger)
follows with the same argument as for assertion (1).
Solve in
T(s,s^{\prime})
.

\blacksquare

Lemma A.13
.

Let
M,M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
be a pair of communicating Markov decision processes and fix
\epsilon>0
.
For all
\mu\in\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M)
, there is
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M^{\prime})
such that

\lVert\mu^{\prime}-\mu\rVert_{\infty}\leq\min\big\{D(\pi_{\mu};M),D(\pi_{\mu};M^{\prime})\big\}\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert_{\infty}.

Proof.

As
\mu
is a
\epsilon
-uniform invariant measure, its induced policy
\pi_{\mu}
is
\epsilon
-uniform.
Moreover,
M^{\prime}
is communicating, so
\pi_{\mu}
is recurrent on
M^{\prime}
.
Let
\mu^{\prime}
be the unique probability invariant measure of
\pi_{\mu}
in
M^{\prime}
.
By
Lemma

D.10
, we have

\lVert\mu^{\prime}-\mu\rVert_{\infty}\leq\min\big\{D(\pi_{\mu};M),D(\pi_{\mu};M^{\prime})\big\}\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert_{\infty}

hence the result.

\blacksquare

Combining
Lemma

A.12
and
Lemma

A.13
, we can express the variations of the set of
\epsilon
-uniform probability invariant measures
\mathcal{I}(\epsilon)
in Hausdorff distance as follows.

Corollary A.14
.

Let
M,M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
be a pair of communicating Markov decision processes and fix
\epsilon>0
.
The Hausdorff distance (in
\ell_{\infty}
-norm) between
\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M)
and
\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M^{\prime})
is bounded by

d_{\infty}(\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M^{\prime}),\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M))\leq\frac{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert\cdot\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert_{\infty}}{\epsilon^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}\mathrm{dmin}(p)^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}}.

A.3.6
Decomposition of invariant measures with unichain policies

In this paragraph, we show that every invariant measure can be expressed as a convex combination of invariant measures of unichain policies, see
Proposition

A.15
.
This result is analogous to the decomposition of flow into simple cycles in Graph Theory.

Proposition

A.15
has several interesting consequences.
The first that we present is a bound on the variations (in Hausdorff distance) of the space of probability invariant measures
\mathcal{I}(M):=\mathrm{Inv}(M)\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z})
as
M
varies, see
Lemma

A.16
downstream.
Combined with
Theorems

A.11
and
A.11
, we further obtain a bound on the variations (in Hausdorff distance) of the space of
\epsilon
-uniform probability invariant measures
\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M):=\mathrm{Inv}(\epsilon;M)\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z})
as
M
varies, see
Lemma

A.17
—A bound that can be compared with
Corollary

A.14
.

Proposition

A.15
is also used to prove that optimal exploration measures exist, see
Theorem

B.15
.

Proposition A.15
.

Let
M
be a communicating Markov decision process.
Let
\Pi_{u}
be the set of unichain policies in
M
.
We have
\mathrm{Inv}(M)=\convexhull(\{\mathrm{Inv}(\pi;M):\pi\in\Pi_{u}\})
, where
\convexhull(-)
denotes the convex hull.

Proof.

Because
\mathrm{Inv}(\pi;M)\subseteq\mathrm{Inv}(M)
and that
\mathrm{Inv}(M)
is convex, we have
\convexhull(\{\mathrm{Inv}(\pi;M):\pi\in\Pi_{u}\})\subseteq\mathrm{Inv}(M)
.

We show the reverse inclusion.

Let
\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
.
If
\mu=0
, there is nothing to prove, so we assume
\support(\mu)\neq\emptyset
.
Let
(s_{1},a_{1},s_{2},a_{2},\ldots,s_{n})\in\mathcal{Z}^{n-1}\times\mathcal{S}
a closed path in
M
such that
(I)
p(s_{i+1}|s_{i},a_{i})>0
,
(II)
\mu(s_{i},a_{i})>0
,
(III)
s_{1}=s_{n}
and
(IV)
n\geq 2
.
The condition (III) states that the path is closed and (IV) that the path is non-trivial.
Up to simplifying that path, we can assume that (V) it is simple in the sense that
s_{i}\neq s_{j}
for all
i\neq j<n
.
Then, the set of pairs
\mathcal{Z}_{0}:=\{(s_{i},a_{i}):i<n\}
is the set of recurrent pairs of the policy given by

\pi_{0}(a|s):=\begin{cases}1&\text{if\penalty\ $s=s_{i}$ and $a=a_{i}$;}\\
0&\text{if\penalty\ $s=s_{i}$ and $a\neq a_{i}$;}\\
\lvert\mathcal{A}(s)\rvert^{-1}&\text{if $s\neq s_{i}$.}\end{cases}

Note that
\pi_{0}
is unichain.
Let
\mu_{0}
the unique probability invariant measure of
\pi_{0}
.
We have
\support(\mu_{0})=\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.
By construction of the path, we have
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\subseteq\support(\mu)
, see (II).
Therefore, the set
\{\lambda>0:\lambda\mu_{0}<\mu\}
is non-empty; Let
\lambda_{0}>0
be its supremum.
Consider the measure
\mu^{\prime}
given by

\mu^{\prime}:=\mu-\lambda_{0}\mu_{0}.

By choice of
\lambda_{0}
, we see that
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{\mathcal{Z}}
.
Note that
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
.
Indeed,
\mathrm{Inv}(M)
is the intersection

\mathrm{Inv}(M)=\mathbf{R}_{+}^{\mathcal{Z}}\cap\mathcal{F}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{F}:=\bigcap_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\left\{\mu^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbf{R}^{\mathcal{Z}}:\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\mu^{\prime\prime}(s,a)=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu^{\prime\prime}(z)p(s|z)\right\}

and
\mathcal{F}
is a linear space, so that
\mu^{\prime}:=\mu-\lambda_{0}\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{F}
.
As
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{\mathcal{Z}}
as explained above, we have
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
.
But by choice of
\lambda_{0}
, the support of
\mu^{\prime}
is strictly smaller than the support of
\mu
, because
\support(\mu^{\prime})\subseteq\support(\mu)
and there must be at least one
z_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}_{0}
such that
z_{0}\notin\support(\mu_{0})
.

So, by induction on the size of the support of
\mu
, we find a sequence
(\lambda_{i},\mu_{i},\pi_{i})_{i\leq m}
with
\lambda_{i}>0
,
\mu_{i}\in\mathrm{Inv}(\pi_{u};M)
and
\pi_{i}\in\Pi_{u}
such that

\mu=\lambda_{0}\mu_{0}+\ldots+\lambda_{m}\mu_{m}.

Note that we can ensure
m+1\leq\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert
.
This concludes the proof.

\blacksquare

Follow
Proposition

A.15
, we show that
\mathcal{I}(M)
is locally
D^{*}(M)
-Lipschitz continuous where
D^{*}(M)
is the worst diameter (
Definition

A.4
).

Lemma A.16

(Variations of
\mathcal{I}(M)
)
.

Let
M\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p,r),M^{\prime}\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p^{\prime},r^{\prime})\in\mathcal{M}
be a pair of communicating Markov decision processes with the same kernel supports, i.e.,
p\sim p^{\prime}
.
The Hausdorff distance (in
\ell_{\infty}
-norm) between
\mathcal{I}(M)
and
\mathcal{I}(M^{\prime})
is bounded by

d_{\infty}(\mathcal{I}(M),\mathcal{I}(M^{\prime}))\leq D^{*}(M)\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert_{\infty}

where
D^{*}(M)
is the worst diameter (
Definition

A.4
).

Proof.

Because
M
and
M^{\prime}
have the same kernel supports, they have the same space of unichain policies
\Pi_{u}
.
Given
\pi\in\Pi_{u}
, we write
\mu_{\pi}
and
\mu^{\prime}_{\pi}
the unique invariant probability measure of
\pi
in
M
and
M^{\prime}
respectively.
For all
\pi\in\Pi_{u}
, we bound
\lVert\mu^{\prime}_{\pi}-\mu_{\pi}\rVert_{\infty}
using
Lemma

D.10
as

\lVert\mu^{\prime}_{\pi}-\mu_{\pi}\rVert_{\infty}\leq D(\pi;M)\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert_{\infty}\leq D^{*}(M)\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert_{\infty}

(A.4)

where the second inequality follows by definition of the worst diameter.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{I}(M)
.
By
Proposition

A.15
, there exists
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}(\Pi_{u})
such that
\mu=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{u}}\lambda_{\pi}\mu_{\pi}
.
Introduce
\mu^{\prime}:=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{u}}\lambda_{\pi}\mu^{\prime}_{\pi}
.
By
Proposition

A.15
, we have
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}(M^{\prime})
.
So, using (
A.4
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\inf_{\mu^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{I}(M^{\prime})}\lVert\mu^{\prime\prime}-\mu\rVert_{\infty}\leq\lVert\mu^{\prime}-\mu\rVert_{\infty}\leq\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{u}}\lambda_{\pi}\lVert\mu^{\prime}_{\pi}-\mu_{\pi}\rVert_{\infty}\leq D^{*}(M)\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert_{\infty}.

Similarly, we show that for all
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}(M)
, we have
\inf_{\mu^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{I}(M)}\lVert\mu^{\prime\prime}-\mu^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}\leq D^{*}(M)\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert_{\infty}
.
This concludes the proof.

\blacksquare

Lemma A.17

(Variations of
\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M)
)
.

Let
M\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p,r),M^{\prime}\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p^{\prime},r^{\prime})\in\mathcal{M}
be a pair of communicating Markov decision processes with the same kernel supports, i.e.,
p\sim p^{\prime}
.
Let
\epsilon<\frac{1}{2}\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert^{-1}D(M)^{-1}
be the required uniformization.
The Hausdorff distance (in
\ell_{\infty}
-norm) between
\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M)
and
\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M^{\prime})
is bounded by

d_{\infty}(\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M),\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M^{\prime}))\leq D^{*}(M)\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert_{\infty}+2\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert D(M)\epsilon

where
D^{*}(M)
is the worst diameter (
Definition

A.4
).

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;-)\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z})
, we have
d_{\infty}(\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M),\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M^{\prime}))\leq 1
trivially.
So, the bound of
Lemma

A.17
is only interesting when
\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert_{\infty}\leq D^{*}(M)^{-1}
.
By
Proposition

D.3
, we have
D(M)\leq D^{*}(M)
so that we can assume that
\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert_{\infty}\leq D(M)^{-1}
.
So, by
Lemma

D.8
, we have

D(M^{\prime})\leq 2D(M).

(A.5)

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M)
.
By
Lemma

A.16
and because
\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M^{\prime})
is compact, there exists
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M^{\prime})
such that
\lVert\mu^{\prime}-\mu\rVert_{\infty}\leq D^{*}(M)\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert_{\infty}
.
Following (
A.5
), we have
\epsilon<\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert^{-1}D(M^{\prime})^{-1}
.
So, by uniformizing
\mu^{\prime}
into
\mu^{\prime}_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M^{\prime})
via
Theorem

A.11
, we have
\lVert\mu^{\prime}-\mu_{\epsilon}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\epsilon\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert D(M^{\prime})
.
So, by triangular inequality, we get

\lVert\mu^{\prime}-\mu_{\epsilon}\rVert\leq D^{*}(M)\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert_{\infty}+\epsilon\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert D(M^{\prime})\overset{(\dagger)}{\leq}D^{*}(M)\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert_{\infty}+2\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert D(M)\epsilon

where
(\dagger)
follows from (
A.5
).

When starting with
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M^{\prime})
, we proceed similarly.

\blacksquare

Note that
Corollary

A.14
and
Lemma

A.17
bound the same quantity,
d_{\infty}(\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M),\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M^{\prime}))
.
Their respective bounds are

\frac{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert\cdot\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert_{\infty}}{\epsilon^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}\mathrm{dmin}(p)^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{V.S.}\hskip 10.00002ptD^{*}(M)\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert_{\infty}+2\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert D(M)\epsilon.

It is to be noted that the second is
not
better than the first; The two bounds are rather of a different nature.
Since
D(M),D^{*}(M)\leq\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert\mathrm{dmin}(p)^{1-\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}
, it is clear that the second bound is much better than the first when
\epsilon
and
\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert_{\infty}
are of the same order.
However, when
\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert_{\infty}\ll\epsilon^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}
as in the proof of
Proposition

B.20
, the first bound is better.
As a matter of fact, the second introduces a flat error
2\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert D(M)\epsilon
that does not vanish when
\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert_{\infty}\to 0
, although
d_{\infty}(\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M),\mathcal{I}(\epsilon;M^{\prime}))\to 0
.

Appendix B
Regularized lower bound and approximate optimalities

In this appendix, we provide proof details on approximate optimalities, leveled Bellman gaps and near optimal pairs, as well as the regularized regret lower bound.

Outline.

Section

B.1
is dedicated to the proof of
Proposition

12
, that establishes the most important properties of the leveling transform (
Definition

9
).
In the remaining
Sections

B.2
and
B.3
, we investigate the continuity properties of the regularized regret lower bound (
Definition

15
).
In
Section

B.2
, we introduce and study the properties of the concept of information value (
Definition

B.3
), that is central to the GLR exploration tests (
16
) and to the regret lower bound.
In the last
Section

B.3
, we focus on the properties of the regularized regret lower bound and establish
Propositions

16
,
17
and
18
.

B.1
Properties of leveled optimality and proof of
Proposition

12

The goal of this paragraph is to provide a proof of
Proposition

12
in the main text (the statement is recalled below for better readability).

Proposition

12
states that if
\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}
is small with respect to the leveling threshold
\epsilon>0
, then the
\epsilon
-leveled transform of
M^{\prime}
(
Definition

9
) has the same gain optimal policies and optimal pairs as
M
.
Moreover, the required ratio between
\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}
and
\epsilon
is shown to be rational in the gain-gap
\Delta^{g}(M)
(
Definition

A.5
) and the worst diameter
D^{*}(M)
(
Definition

A.4
).

Proposition

12
.

For every communicating Markov decision process
M\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p,\nu)
, there exists a quantity
C(M)<\infty
, that is a rational function of
\Delta^{g}(M)
and
D^{*}(M)
, for all
\epsilon>0
and
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
satisfying

\epsilon+2C(M)\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}<\Delta^{g}(M)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptC(M)\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}<\epsilon

(B.1)

we have

1.

[M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon}
and
M
have the same gain optimal policies;

2.

\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon}(M)=\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
;

Proof outline.

The remaining of this paragraph is dedicated to the proof of
Proposition

12
.
In
Lemma

B.1
, we show that bias optimal policies of
M^{\prime}
are Bellman optimal (
Definition

A.2
) in
[M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon}
.
In turn, we prove
Lemma

B.2
, that consists in the assertion (1) of
Proposition

12
: by leveling a noisy model, we recover the set of gain optimal policies.
Once (1) is established, the other assertion (2) is immediate by definition of
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
and
\epsilon>0
.
Every bias optimal policy of
M^{\prime}
is Bellman optimal (
Definition

A.2
) in
[M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon}
, i.e., for all
\pi\in\Pi
that is bias optimal policy in
M^{\prime}
, we have

1.

\mathrm{sp}(g^{\pi}([M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon}))=0
; and

2.

[r^{\prime}]_{\epsilon}(s,a)+[p^{\prime}]_{\epsilon}(s,a)b^{\pi}([M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon})\leq g^{\pi}(s;[M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon})+b^{\pi}(s;[M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon})
for all
(s,a)\in\mathcal{Z}
.

In particular,
\Pi^{*}(M^{\prime})\subseteq\Pi^{*}([M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon})
and
g^{*}(M^{\prime})=g^{*}([M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon})
.

Proof.

Let
\pi^{*}
be a bias optimal policy of
M^{\prime}
, so that
g^{\pi^{*}}(M^{\prime})=g^{*}(M^{\prime})
and
b^{\pi^{*}}(M^{\prime})=b^{*}(M^{\prime})
.
By construction, we have
[r^{\prime}]_{\epsilon}(z)=r^{\prime}(z)+\mathbf{1}(\Delta^{*}(z;M^{\prime})<\epsilon)\Delta^{*}(z;M^{\prime})
for
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, so

g^{\pi^{*}}([M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon})=g^{\pi^{*}}(M^{\prime})=g^{*}(M^{\prime})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptb^{\pi^{*}}([M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon})=b^{\pi^{*}}(M^{\prime})=b^{*}(M^{\prime}).

Therefore,
\mathrm{sp}(g^{\pi^{*}}([M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon}))=0
and
\pi^{*}
satisfies the Bellman equations (
A.1
) in
[M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon}
, i.e., it is Bellman optimal (
Definition

A.2
) in
[M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon}
;
Hence the assertions (1) and (2).

\blacksquare

In
Appendix

D
, we investigate the sensibility of the gain and bias functions to changes of reward function and transition kernel.
Following
Lemma

D.14
and
Lemma

D.16
, for every policy
\pi\in\Pi
and provided that
\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert^{*}_{\infty}\leq{D^{*}(M)}^{-1}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert g^{\pi}(M^{\prime})-g^{\pi}(M)\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq C_{g}(M)\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}

(B.2)

\displaystyle\left\lVert b^{\pi}(M^{\prime})-b^{\pi}(M)\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq C_{b}(M)\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Delta^{\pi}(M^{\prime})-\Delta^{\pi}(M)\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\Delta}(M)\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}

where
C_{g}(M),C_{b}(M),C_{\Delta}(M)
are polynomial in
D^{*}(M)
and of respective orders
D^{*}(M)
,
D^{*}(M)^{2}
,
D^{*}(M)^{2}
.

Lemma B.2
.

Fix
\epsilon>0
a leveling parameter.
For every pair of communicating Markov decision processes
M,M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
such that

\epsilon+2C_{g}(M)\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}<\Delta^{g}(M)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptC_{\Delta}(M)D^{*}(M)\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}<\epsilon

(B.3)

we have
\Pi^{*}([M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon})=\Pi^{*}(M)
.

Proof.

We proceed by double inclusion.

Assume that
\pi\in\Pi^{*}([M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon})
.
By
Lemma

B.1
, bias optimal policies of
M^{\prime}
are Bellman optimal (
Definition

A.2
) in
[M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon}
.
In particular, they are gain optimal.
In the mean time, bias optimal policies are supported in weakly optimal actions, so by construction of
[M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon}
, the gain of bias optimal policies of
M^{\prime}
is the same in
M^{\prime}
and
[M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon}
.
Combined, we conclude that
g^{*}([M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon})=g^{*}(M^{\prime})
.
Now, since
M^{\prime}
and
[M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon}
only differ in their reward function with
\lVert r^{\prime}-[r^{\prime}]_{\epsilon}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\epsilon
, it follows that

g^{\pi}(M^{\prime})\geq g^{\pi}([M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon})-\epsilon e=g^{*}(M^{\prime})-\epsilon e

where
e
is the unitary vector.
By (
B.2
), we further have
g^{\pi}(M)\geq g^{\pi}(M^{\prime})-C_{g}(M)\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}e
and
g^{*}(M^{\prime})\geq g^{*}(M)-C_{g}(M)\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}e
.
All together,

g^{\pi}(M)\geq g^{*}(M)-\big(\epsilon+2C_{g}(M)\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}\big)e

so
\pi\in\Pi^{*}(M)
if
\epsilon+2C_{g}(M)\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}<\Delta^{g}(M)
.
This is indeed the case by assumption.

Conversely, let
\pi\in\Pi^{*}(M)
.
Let
\mathcal{Z}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{Z}_{m}
be the recurrent components of
\pi
and let
\mu_{i}
be the unique probability invariant measure of
\pi
supported in
\mathcal{Z}_{i}
.
Fix
\pi^{\prime}
a bias optimal policy of
M^{\prime}
, so that
\Delta^{\pi^{\prime}}(M^{\prime})\geq 0
.
So,

\Delta^{\pi^{\prime}}(z;M)\geq-C_{\Delta}(M)\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}

(B.4)

for every pair
z\in\mathcal{Z}
.
We have already shown that
\Pi^{*}([M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon})\subseteq\Pi^{*}(M)
.
Since
\Pi^{*}(M^{\prime})\subseteq\Pi^{*}([M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon})
by
Lemma

B.1
, we have
\Pi^{*}(M^{\prime})\subseteq\Pi^{*}(M)
.
So,
\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{*}(M)
.

We deduce that
g^{\pi^{\prime}}(M)=g^{\pi}(M)
.
Now, for
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
, we have
g^{\pi}(M)=g^{\pi^{\prime}}(M)-\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu_{i}(z)\Delta^{\pi^{\prime}}(z;M)
, so together with
g^{\pi}(M)=g^{\pi^{\prime}}(M)
, we get
\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}[\mu_{i}(z)\Delta^{\pi^{\prime}}(z;M)]_{+}=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}[\mu_{i}(z)\Delta^{\pi^{\prime}}(z;M)]_{-}
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
.
Combined with (
B.4
), we obtain

\max_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\Delta^{\pi^{\prime}}(z;M)\leq C_{\Delta}(M)\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}.

(B.5)

By
Lemma

D.2
, we have
\min(\mu_{i})\geq D(\pi;M|_{\mathcal{Z}_{i}})^{-1}
.
Together with
D(\pi;M|_{\mathcal{Z}_{i}})\geq D(\pi;M)^{-1}
and (
B.5
), we conclude that

\Delta^{\pi^{\prime}}(z;M)\leq{C_{\Delta}(M)}{D(\pi;M)}\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}\leq{C_{\Delta}(M)}{D^{*}(M)}\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}

for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
that is recurrent under
\pi
.
So, provided that
C_{\Delta}(M)D^{*}(M)\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}<\epsilon
—which is indeed the case by assumption, we have
\Delta^{\pi^{\prime}}(z;[M]_{\epsilon})=0
for every
z\in\mathcal{Z}
that is recurrent under
\pi
in
M
.
Now, note that the recurrent pairs of
\pi
are the same on
M
,
M^{\prime}
,
[M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon}
.
So, again using the formula
g^{\pi}([M]_{\epsilon})=g^{\pi^{\prime}}([M]_{\epsilon})-\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\Delta^{\pi^{\prime}}(z;[M]_{\epsilon})
that holds for every probability invariant measure of
\pi
in
[M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon}
, we conclude that
g^{\pi}([M]_{\epsilon})=g^{\pi^{\prime}}([M]_{\epsilon})
.
3
3

3

To see this, note that this already holds for
\mu=\mu_{i}
with
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
and use that every probability invariant measure of
\pi
is a convex combination of
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{m}
.

By
Lemma

B.1
,
\pi^{\prime}
is Bellman optimal (
Definition

A.2
) in
[M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon}
hence is gain optimal in
[M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon}
in particular.
We conclude that
g^{\pi}([M]_{\epsilon})=g^{*}(M)
, i.e.,
\pi\in\Pi^{*}(M)
.

\blacksquare

With
Lemma

B.2
in hand, we can prove
Proposition

12
.

Proof of
Proposition

12
.

Assume that
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
satisfies
\epsilon+2C_{g}(M)\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}<\Delta^{g}(M)
.
By
Lemma

B.2
, we have
\Pi^{*}([M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon})=\Pi^{*}(M)
, hence proving (1).
Together with the fact that the transition kernels of
M
and
[M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon}
have the same support, it follows that
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)=\mathcal{Z}^{**}([M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon})
, hence proving (2).
This concludes the proof.

\blacksquare

B.2
Information value of measures and critical models

In this section, we discuss the notion of the
information value
(
Definition

B.3
), that quantifies how efficient is a measure on
\mathcal{Z}
to reject the leveled confusing set.
Information values provide an alternative view on information constraints, are key in the derivation of the continuity properties of the regularized lower bound.
In association with the information value, we introduce
critical models
(
Definition

B.4
) as the confusing Markov decision processes that are the hardest to reject under a measure on
\mathcal{Z}
.

Additional notations.

In this section, we write
\mathcal{P}_{\delta}(\mathcal{Z})
the set of probability distributions
\mu
on
\mathcal{Z}
such that
\min(\mu)\geq\delta
.
We write
e:=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}e_{z}
the unit measure on
\mathcal{Z}
and
(e_{z})_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}
the canonical basis of
\mathbf{R}^{\mathcal{Z}}
.
For
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
, we denote
\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})
the closure of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
for the topology induced by
\lVert-\rVert
.

Definition B.0

(Information value)
.

Fix
\epsilon>0
a leveling parameter.
The
\epsilon
-leveled information value
of a measure
\mu\in\mathbf{R}^{\mathcal{Z}}
in
M
is given by

C_{\epsilon}(\mu,M):=\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M)}\left\{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\right\}\in[0,\infty].

Note that the regret lower bound can be written as

K(M)=\inf_{\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)}\left\{\frac{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\Delta^{*}(z;M)}{C_{0}(\mu,M)}\right\}.

As a matter of fact, given
\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
, the quantity
C_{0}(\mu,M)^{-1}
is the scalar
\lambda\in[0,\infty]
with which
\mu
must be normalized so that it properly rejects all confusing models, in the sense of
\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\lambda\mu(z)\cdot\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\geq 1
.

Definition B.0

(Critical models)
.

Fix
\epsilon>0
a leveling parameter and
\mu\in\mathbf{R}^{\mathcal{Z}}
a measure.
A Markov decision process
M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{cl}(\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M))
is said
(\mu,\epsilon,M)
-critical
if
M^{\dagger}
achieves the
\epsilon
-leveled information value of
\mu
in
M
, i.e.,

C_{\epsilon}(\mu,M)=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z)).

For
\epsilon=0
, we talk more simply of information value and
(\mu,M)
-critical models.
Unfortunately, the case
\epsilon=0
is not well-behaved relatively to
M
in general.
It is among the reasons why the regret lower bound is discontinuous with respect to
M
.
This is quite inconvenient when one has only access to noisy copies of
M
, as it is the case in learning.
Thankfully, when the leveling parameter satisfies
\epsilon>0
and when
\mu
is fully supported, the leveled information value is continuous in both
M
(
Lemma

B.13
) and
\mu
(
Lemma

B.12
).
Accordingly, it can be correctly estimated even in the presence of noise.

B.2.1
Properties of the confusing set and of critical models

In this paragraph, we provide a few useful properties of leveled confusing sets and critical models.
In
Lemma

B.5
, we rephrase the definition of the
\epsilon
-confusing set in terms of the gain function.
In
Corollary

B.7
, we show that all critical models have small bias span and that their gain optimal policies are unichain.

Lemma B.5

(Characterization of confusing models)
.

Let
M\in\mathcal{M}
be a communicating model and
\epsilon>0
.
Fix
M^{\prime}\gg M
such that
M^{\prime}(z)=M(z)
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon}(M)
.
The following assertions are equivalent:

1.

M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M)
, i.e.,
\Pi^{*}(M^{\dagger})\cap\Pi^{*}(M)=\emptyset
;

2.

g^{*}(M^{\dagger})>g^{\pi}(M^{\dagger})
for all
\pi\in\Pi^{*}(M)
;

3.

g^{*}(M^{\dagger})>g^{*}(M)
.

Proof.

Let
M^{\prime}\gg M
be such that
M^{\prime}(z)=M(z)
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon}(M)
.
Because
M^{\prime}\gg M
and
M
is communicating,
M^{\prime}
is communicating as well.

Let
\pi\in\Pi^{*}(M)
.
If
\mathcal{Z}_{\pi}
denotes the recurrent pairs of
\pi
in
M
, then since
p^{\prime}\gg p
, we have
\mathbf{P}_{s}^{\pi,M^{\prime}}(\forall m,\exists n\geq m:Z_{n}\in\mathcal{Z}_{\pi})=1
regardless of the initial state
s\in\mathcal{S}
.
In other words, the iterates of
\pi
hit
\mathcal{Z}_{\pi}
infinitely often in
M^{\prime}
.
But
M^{\prime}=M
on
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
and
\mathcal{Z}_{\pi}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
since
\pi\in\Pi^{*}(M)
; So, every recurrent pair of
\pi
in
M^{\prime}
belongs to
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
and in particular,

g^{\pi}(M^{\prime})=g^{\pi}(M)=g^{*}(M).

(B.6)

Following (
B.6
), the equivalence between the assertions (2) and (3) in
Lemma

B.5
is immediate; and
\Pi^{*}(M^{\dagger})\cap\Pi^{*}(M)=\emptyset
as soon as
\pi\notin\Pi^{*}(M^{\dagger})
, i.e., as soon as
g^{*}(M^{\dagger})>g^{\pi}(M)
.

\blacksquare

Lemma B.6

(Simplification of confusing models)
.

Assume that
\mathcal{M}
is in product form, i.e., that
\mathcal{M}=\prod_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}(\mathcal{R}_{z}\times\mathcal{P}_{z})
.
Let
M\in\mathcal{M}
a communicating model and fix
\epsilon\geq 0
.
For every
M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M)
, there exists
M^{\ddagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M)
such that

1.

M^{\ddagger}
is communicating;

2.

\mathrm{sp}(b^{*}(M^{\ddagger}))\leq D(M)
;

3.

M^{\ddagger}(z)=M(z)
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon}(M)\cup\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M^{\ddagger})^{c}
;

4.

For all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
,
\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\ddagger}(z))\leq\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))
;

5.

\lVert M^{\ddagger}-M\rVert\leq\lVert M^{\dagger}-M\rVert
.

Proof.

We consider the extended Markov decision process
M^{\prime}=M^{\dagger}\cup M
where, from the state
s\in\mathcal{S}
, a choice of action consists in choosing whether the transition is done in
M
or in
M^{\dagger}
, i.e., choosing
a\in\mathcal{A}(s)
, then a reward among
\{\nu(s,a),\nu^{\dagger}(s,a)\}
and a transition kernel among
\{p(s,a),p^{\dagger}(s,a)\}
.
Note that
M^{\prime}
is communicating with
D(M^{\prime})\leq D(M)
.
Moreover,

g^{*}(M^{\prime})\geq g^{*}(M)\text{\hskip 10.00002ptand \hskip 10.00002pt}g^{*}(M^{\prime})\geq g^{*}(M^{\dagger}).

An (extended) policy achieving optimal bias in
M^{\prime}
defines a policy
\pi^{\prime}
on
M
together with a model
M^{\ddagger}
such that
g^{*}(M^{\ddagger})=g^{*}(M^{\prime})=g^{\pi^{\prime}}(M^{\ddagger})
and
\mathrm{sp}(b^{*}(M^{\dagger}))=\mathrm{sp}(b^{*}(M^{\prime}))\leq D(M^{\prime})\leq D(M)
, see
Lemma

B.23
.
So
M^{\ddagger}
satisfies (1).
By construction, we also have

\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\ddagger}(z))\leq\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z)).

(B.7)

To conclude, we show that
M^{\ddagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M)
as follows.
Because
M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M)
, it follows that
M^{\ddagger}
coincides with
M
on
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon}(M)
.
In particular, every gain optimal policy
\pi\in\Pi^{*}(M)
has at least one recurrent component within
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)\subseteq\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon}(M)
hence satisfies
g^{\pi}(s;M^{\ddagger})=g^{*}(s;M)
for some
s\in\mathcal{S}
.
But
g^{*}(M^{\ddagger})\geq g^{*}(M^{\dagger})>g^{*}(M)
by
Lemma

B.5
, so by
Lemma

B.5
again, we have
M^{\ddagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M)
.

\blacksquare

Following
Lemma

B.6
, we obtain that critical models have small bias span, see
Corollary

B.7
below.

Corollary B.7

(Structure of critical models)
.

Assume that
\mathcal{M}
is in product form, i.e., that
\mathcal{M}=\prod_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}(\mathcal{R}_{z}\times\mathcal{P}_{z})
.
Let
M\in\mathcal{M}
a communicating model,
\epsilon>0
and fix
\mu\in(\mathbf{R}^{*}_{+})^{\mathcal{Z}}
a fully supported measure.
Every
(\mu,\epsilon,M)
-critical model
M^{\dagger}
is such that

1.

\mathrm{sp}(b^{*}(M^{\dagger}))\leq D(M)
;

2.

M^{\dagger}(z)=M(z)
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon}(M)\cup\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M^{\dagger})^{c}
.

Another consequence of
Lemma

B.6
is that every model is bounded away (in norm) from its confusing set relatively to its gain-gap and its diameter, see
Lemma

B.8
below.

Lemma B.8

(Distance to confusing set)
.

Every communicating model
M\in\mathcal{M}
is bounded away from its confusing set (in norm), i.e.,

\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)}\lVert M^{\dagger}-M\rVert\geq\frac{\Delta^{g}(M)}{4D(M)}>0

where
\Delta^{g}(M)
is the gain-gap of
M
, see
Definition

A.5
.

Proof.

Let
M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)
.
By
Lemma

B.6
, there exists
M^{\ddagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)
such that
\lVert M^{\ddagger}-M\rVert\leq\lVert M^{\dagger}-M\rVert
and
\mathrm{sp}(b^{*}(M^{\dagger}))\leq D(M)
.
By
Lemma

B.23
, we also have
\mathrm{sp}(b^{*}(M))\leq D(M)
.

Both
M
and
M^{\ddagger}
are communicating, so
\mathrm{sp}(g^{*}(M))=\mathrm{sp}(g^{*}(M^{\ddagger}))=0
, so by
Lemma

D.9
,

\begin{gathered}\lVert g^{\pi}(M^{\ddagger})-g^{\pi}(M)\rVert_{\infty}\leq\lVert r^{\ddagger}-r\rVert_{\infty}+\frac{1}{2}D(M)\lVert p^{\ddagger}-p\rVert_{\infty};\\
\lVert g^{\pi^{\ddagger}}(M^{\ddagger})-g^{\pi^{\ddagger}}(M)\rVert_{\infty}\leq\lVert r^{\ddagger}-r\rVert_{\infty}+\frac{1}{2}D(M)\lVert p^{\ddagger}-p\rVert_{\infty}\end{gathered}

(B.8)

where
\pi
and
\pi^{\ddagger}
are bias optimal policies of
M
and
M^{\ddagger}
respectively.

By assumption, we have
\pi^{\ddagger}\notin\Pi^{*}(M)
, so

\displaystyle\Delta^{g}(M)

\displaystyle\leq\lVert g^{\pi^{\ddagger}}(M)-g^{\pi}(M)\rVert_{\infty}=\max\left(g^{\pi}(M)-g^{\pi^{\ddagger}}(M)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\max\left(g^{\pi}(M)-g^{\pi}(M^{\ddagger})\right)+\max\left(g^{\pi}(M^{\ddagger})-g^{\pi^{\ddagger}}(M^{\ddagger})\right)+\max\left(g^{\pi}(M^{\ddagger})-g^{\pi^{\ddagger}}(M)\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{\leq}\max\left(g^{\pi}(M)-g^{\pi}(M^{\ddagger})\right)+\max\left(g^{\pi}(M^{\ddagger})-g^{\pi^{\ddagger}}(M)\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ddagger)}{\leq}2\left(\lVert r^{\ddagger}-r\rVert_{\infty}+D(M)\lVert p^{\ddagger}-p\rVert_{\infty}\right)\leq 4\max\{1,D(M)\}\lVert M^{\ddagger}-M\rVert

where

(\dagger)
follows from
\pi^{\ddagger}\in\Pi^{*}(M^{\ddagger})
and

(\ddagger)
follows from (
B.9
).
Note that
D(M)\geq 1
by definition.
We conclude by solving the above in
\lVert M^{\ddagger}-M\rVert
and using that
\lVert M^{\dagger}-M\rVert\geq\lVert M^{\ddagger}-M\rVert
.

\blacksquare

For the last result of this paragraph, we provide a variant of
Lemma

B.8
, see
Lemma

B.9
.
This result generalized the lower bound of
Lemma

B.8
from confusing set to a weak version of the
alternative set
.
The alternative set of
M
is the collection of
M^{\dagger}\gg M
such that
\Pi^{*}(M^{\dagger})\cap\Pi^{*}(M)=\emptyset
.
In
Lemma

B.9
below, the absolute continuity condition “
M^{\dagger}\gg M
” is strenghtened to a mutual absolute continuity condition on supports, i.e., to “
p^{\dagger}\sim p
”.

Lemma B.9

(Distance to alternative set, weak version)
.

Given a communicating model
M\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p,r)\in\mathcal{M}
, if
M^{\dagger}\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p^{\dagger},r^{\dagger})
is such that
(1)
p^{\dagger}\sim p
and
(2)
\Pi^{*}(M^{\dagger})\setminus\Pi^{*}(M)\neq\emptyset
, then

\lVert M^{\dagger}-M\rVert\geq\frac{\Delta^{g}(M)}{4D(M)}

where
\Delta^{g}(M)
is the gain-gap of
M
, see
Definition

A.5
.

Proof.

By (2),
p^{\dagger}
and
p
have the same support, so
M^{\dagger}
is communicating as well.
Assume that
\lVert p^{\dagger}-p\rVert^{*}_{\infty}\leq D(M)^{-1}
.
Then, by
Lemma

D.8
, we have
D(M^{\dagger})\leq 2D(M)
so
\mathrm{sp}(b^{*}(M^{\dagger}))\leq 2D(M)
.
By
Lemma

B.23
, we also have
\mathrm{sp}(b^{*}(M))\leq D(M)
.

Let
\pi^{\dagger}\in\Pi^{*}(M^{\dagger})\setminus\Pi^{*}(M)
.
In particular,
g^{\pi^{\dagger}}(M)<g^{*}(M)
so
\pi^{\dagger}
has a recurrent class of pairs
\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
(in
M
) on which it has sub-optimal gain (in
M
).
By (2),
p^{\dagger}
and
p
have the same support, so the recurrent classes of policies are the same under
M
and
M^{\dagger}
.
In particular,
\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}
is also a recurrent class of
\pi^{\dagger}
in
M^{\dagger}
.
Now, up to an infinitesimal perturbation of
r^{\dagger}
of the form
r^{\dagger}(z)+\epsilon\mathbf{1}(z\in\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger})
with
\epsilon>0
, we can assume that
\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}
is the unique optimal recurrent class of
M^{\dagger}
; In other words, that all gain optimal policies of
M^{\dagger}
are unichain with recurrent class
\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}
.
In particular, all bias optimal policies of
M^{\dagger}
with unique recurrent class
\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}
, so are sub-optimal in
M
.
Let
\pi^{\dagger}
a bias optimal policy of
M^{\dagger}
.

From then, the proof is mostly similar to the one of
Lemma

B.8
.

Both
M
and
M^{\dagger}
are communicating, so
\mathrm{sp}(g^{*}(M))=\mathrm{sp}(g^{*}(M^{\dagger}))=0
, so by
Lemma

D.9
,

\begin{gathered}\lVert g^{\pi}(M^{\dagger})-g^{\pi}(M)\rVert_{\infty}\leq\lVert r^{\dagger}-r\rVert_{\infty}+\frac{1}{2}D(M)\lVert p^{\dagger}-p\rVert_{\infty};\\
\lVert g^{\pi^{\dagger}}(M^{\dagger})-g^{\pi^{\dagger}}(M)\rVert_{\infty}\leq\lVert r^{\dagger}-r\rVert_{\infty}+D(M)\lVert p^{\dagger}-p\rVert_{\infty}\end{gathered}

(B.9)

where
\pi
and
\pi^{\dagger}
are bias optimal policies of
M
and
M^{\dagger}
respectively.

As argued upstream, we have
\pi^{\dagger}
has sub-optimal gain in
M
, so

\displaystyle\Delta^{g}(M)

\displaystyle\leq\lVert g^{\pi^{\dagger}}(M)-g^{\pi}(M)\rVert_{\infty}=\max\left(g^{\pi}(M)-g^{\pi^{\dagger}}(M)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\max\left(g^{\pi}(M)-g^{\pi}(M^{\dagger})\right)+\max\left(g^{\pi}(M^{\dagger})-g^{\pi^{\dagger}}(M^{\dagger})\right)+\max\left(g^{\pi}(M^{\dagger})-g^{\pi^{\dagger}}(M)\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{\leq}\max\left(g^{\pi}(M)-g^{\pi}(M^{\dagger})\right)+\max\left(g^{\pi}(M^{\dagger})-g^{\pi^{\dagger}}(M)\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{\leq}2\left(\lVert r^{\dagger}-r\rVert_{\infty}+D(M)\lVert p^{\dagger}-p\rVert_{\infty}\right)\leq 4\max\{1,D(M)\}\lVert M^{\dagger}-M\rVert

where

(\dagger)
follows from
\pi^{\dagger}\in\Pi^{*}(M^{\dagger})
and

(\dagger)
follows from (
B.9
).
Conclude using
D(M)\geq 1
.

\blacksquare

B.2.2
Regularity properties of the information value

In this paragraph, we show that for
\epsilon>0
and when working away from the boundary of
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z})
, the information value
C_{\epsilon}(\mu,M)
is bounded relatively to the reference value
C_{\epsilon}(e,M)
(
Lemma

B.10
) that itself is bounded below (
Lemma

B.11
), and is continuous relatively to
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{\delta}(\mathcal{Z})
(
Lemma

B.12
) and
M\in\mathcal{M}
(
Lemma

B.13
).

Lemma B.10
.

Fix
M\in\mathcal{M}
, a leveling parameter
\epsilon\geq 0
and
\delta>0
.
The leveled information value of
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{\delta}(\mathcal{Z})
is lower and upper bounded as

\delta\penalty\ C_{\epsilon}(e,M)\leq C_{\epsilon}(\mu,M)\leq C_{\epsilon}(e,M).

Proof.

By definition,
\min(\mu)\geq\delta
for all
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{\delta}(\mathcal{Z})
.
So,

\displaystyle C_{\epsilon}(\mu,M)

\displaystyle:=\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M)}\left\{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\right\}

\displaystyle\geq\delta\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M)}\left\{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}1\cdot\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\right\}=\delta\penalty\ C_{\epsilon}(e,M).

Using that
\max(\mu)\leq 1
, the upper bound follows from a similar computation.

\blacksquare

Lemma B.11

(Gap bound of information value)
.

For all
M\in\mathcal{M}
and whatever the leveling parameter
\epsilon\geq 0
, we have

C_{\epsilon}(e,M)\geq\left(\frac{\Delta^{g}(M)}{4D(M)}\right)^{2}.

Proof.

The result follows from a straight forward computation.
We have

\displaystyle C_{\epsilon}(M)

\displaystyle=\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M)}\left\{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{\geq}\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)}\left\{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\right\}

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)}\max_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\left\{\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{(\ddagger)}{\geq}\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)}\max_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\left\{\left(\lVert r^{\dagger}(z)-r(z)\rVert_{\infty}+\lVert p^{\dagger}(z)-p(z)\rVert_{1}\right)^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle=\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)}\lVert M^{\dagger}-M\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(\lx@sectionsign)}{\geq}\left(\frac{\Delta^{g}(M)}{4D(M)}\right)^{2}

where

(\dagger)
follows from
\mathrm{Cnf}(\epsilon;M)\subseteq\mathrm{Cnf}(M)
;

(\ddagger)
follows from Pinsker’s inequality; and

(\lx@sectionsign)
follows from
Lemma

B.8
.

\blacksquare

Lemma B.12

(Continuity in
\mu
)
.

Fix
M\in\mathcal{M}
, a leveling parameter
\epsilon\geq 0
and
\delta>0
.
For all
\mu,\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{\delta}(\mathcal{Z})
such that
\lVert\mu^{\prime}-\mu\rVert_{\infty}<\frac{1}{2}\delta
, we have

\left\lvert C_{\epsilon}(\mu^{\prime},M)-C_{\epsilon}(\mu^{\prime},M)\right\rvert\leq\frac{2C_{\epsilon}(\mu,M)}{\delta}\cdot\lVert\mu^{\prime}-\mu\rVert_{\infty}.

Proof.

Let
\mu,\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{\delta}(\mathcal{Z})
.
By
Lemma

B.10
,
C_{\epsilon}(\mu,M)
and
C_{\epsilon}(\mu^{\prime},M)
are either both finite or infinite.
If
\mathrm{Cnf}(M)=\emptyset
, then these quantities are infinite and there is nothing to be said; Let us assume that
C_{\epsilon}(\mu,M),C_{\epsilon}(\mu^{\prime},M)<\infty
.
Accordingly, there exist
M^{\dagger}_{\mu}
and
M^{\dagger}_{\mu^{\prime}}
that are a
(\mu,\epsilon,M)
-critical and a
(\mu^{\prime},\epsilon,M)
-critical model respectively.
Note that since
\lVert\mu^{\prime}-\mu\rVert_{\infty}<\frac{1}{2}\delta
with
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{\delta}(\mathcal{Z})
, we can write
\mu^{\prime}=\mu+u\mu
with
\lVert u\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{2}\delta^{-1}\lVert\mu^{\prime}-\mu\rVert_{\infty}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle C_{\epsilon}(\mu^{\prime},M)

\displaystyle:=\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M)}\left\{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu^{\prime}(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu^{\prime}(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}_{\mu}(z))

\displaystyle\leq(1+\lVert u\rVert_{\infty})\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}_{\mu}(z))\leq\left(1+\frac{\lVert\mu^{\prime}-\mu\rVert_{\infty}}{\delta}\right)C_{\epsilon}(\mu,M).

As
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
play a symmetric role in the proof, they can be interchanged.
This immediately provides a lower bound of
C_{\epsilon}(\mu^{\prime},M)
with respect to
C_{\epsilon}(\mu,M)
.
We conclude by using
(1+\frac{\lVert\mu^{\prime}-\mu\rVert_{\infty}}{\delta})^{-1}\geq 1-\frac{2\lVert\mu^{\prime}-\mu\rVert_{\infty}}{\delta}
, that holds because
\lVert\mu^{\prime}-\mu\rVert_{\infty}<\frac{1}{2}\delta
.

\blacksquare

Lemma B.13

(Continuity in
M
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an ambient space with Bernoulli rewards (
3
) in product form (
4
).
Fix
M\in\mathcal{M}
a communicating model.
There exist functions
\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*}
that are polynomial in
\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert,D,\mathrm{dmin}(M)
and
C(e,M)
, such that, for all leveling parameter
\epsilon>0
with
\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M)=\mathrm{Cnf}(M)
and all
\delta>0
, if
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
satisfies

\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}\leq\min\left\{\frac{\epsilon}{\alpha(M)},\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\delta}\beta_{1}(M)-\beta_{2}(M)\right)\right\}

(B.10)

then for all
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{\delta}(M)
, we have

\left\lvert C_{\epsilon}(\mu,M^{\prime})-C_{\epsilon}(\mu,M)\right\rvert\leq\exp\left(\beta(M)\right)\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}.

Proof idea.

The proof is difficult.
We start by introducing
\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}
, a well-behaved neighborhood of
M
for the support-aware norm
\lVert-\rVert^{*}
, given by

\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}:=\left\{M^{\prime}:\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}<\min\left\{\frac{1}{D(M)},\frac{\Delta^{g}(M)}{4\max\{1,D(\pi_{u};M)\}},\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{dmin}(p),\frac{\epsilon}{\alpha(M)}\right\}\right\}

(B.11)

where
(I) the condition with
D(M)^{-1}
is to make sure that
D(M^{\prime})\leq 2D(M)
, see
Lemma

D.8
;
(II) the condition with
\frac{1}{4}D(M)^{-1}\Delta^{g}(M)
is to make sure that
M^{\prime}\notin\mathrm{Cnf}(M)
, see
Lemma

B.9
;
(III) the condition with
\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{dmin}(p)
is a bit technical, and is to obtain uniform bounds on escaping probabilities; and
(IV) the condition with
\frac{\epsilon}{\alpha(M)}
follows from
Proposition

12
, stating that there exists
\alpha(M)<\infty
such that if the leveling parameter
\epsilon>0
is small enough, every model
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
that satisfies

\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{\alpha(M)}

(B.12)

is such that
\mathcal{Z}^{**}([M^{\prime}]_{\epsilon})=\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
.
Note that by
Lemma

B.5
, such a model
M^{\prime}
satisfies

\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M^{\prime})=\left\{M^{\prime\dagger}\gg M:M^{\prime\dagger}=M^{\prime}\text{\penalty\ on\penalty\ }\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }g^{*}(M^{\prime\dagger})>g^{*}(M^{\prime})\right\}.

(B.13)

Fix
\hat{M},\widetilde{M}\in\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}
generic Markov decision processes in the neighborhood of
M
.
The goal is to upper-bound the information value of
\hat{M}
relatively to the information value of
\widetilde{M}
.
We will conclude by instantiating
\hat{M}
and
\widetilde{M}
as
M=\hat{M},M^{\prime}=\widetilde{M}
and
M=\widetilde{M},M^{\prime}=\hat{M}
.

Let
\widetilde{M}^{\dagger}
be a
(\mu,\epsilon,\widetilde{M})
-critical model.
We introduce
\hat{M}^{\dagger}
the model given by

\hat{M}^{\dagger}(z):=\begin{cases}\hat{M}(z)&\text{if\penalty\ }z\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M);\\
\widetilde{M}^{\dagger}(z)&\text{if\penalty\ }z\notin\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M).\end{cases}

(B.14)

In general, we have
\hat{M}^{\dagger}\notin\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(\hat{M})
because the condition
g^{*}(\hat{M}^{\dagger})>g^{*}(\hat{M})
is false—barely false, but false nonetheless.
But if it is false, then because
g^{*}(\widetilde{M}^{\dagger})>g^{*}(\widetilde{M})
and
\hat{M}\approx\widetilde{M}
, there is a slight modification of
\hat{M}^{\dagger}
that belongs to
\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(\hat{M})
.
In the first place, the condition “
g^{*}(\hat{M}^{\dagger})>g^{*}(\hat{M})
” may be false because by going from
\widetilde{M}^{\dagger}
to
\hat{M}^{\dagger}
, the optimal pairs of
\widetilde{M}^{\dagger}
may be degraded while the (leveled) optimal pairs of
\widetilde{M}
may be improved.
Naturally, the idea of the proof is to
repair

\hat{M}^{\dagger}
into
\hat{M}^{\ddagger}\approx\hat{M}^{\dagger}
with
\hat{M}^{\ddagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M)
by increasing the reward function at well-chosen pairs.

Given
\eta>0
, we introduce the repaired model as

\hat{M}^{\ddagger}_{\eta}\equiv\hat{M}^{\ddagger\eta}\equiv(\mathcal{Z},\hat{r}^{\dagger}+\eta\penalty\ e_{\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}},\hat{p}^{\dagger})\text{\hskip 10.00002ptwhere \hskip 10.00002pt}\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}:=\mathcal{Z}^{**}(\widetilde{M}^{\dagger})\setminus\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M),

(B.15)

i.e., as the copy of
\hat{M}^{\dagger}
where the reward function is increased by
\eta
at every pair of
\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}
.
The difficulty of the proof consists in choosing
\eta>0
correctly.

Proof of
Lemma

B.13
.

We proceed with the Markov decision processes
\hat{M},\widetilde{M},\widetilde{M}^{\dagger},\hat{M}^{\dagger}
and
\hat{M}^{\ddagger}_{\eta}\equiv\hat{M}^{\ddagger\eta}
defined upstream.
The proof is organized as follows.

We begin by the end.
We show in (
S
TEP
1
) that if
\hat{M}^{\ddagger\eta}
is an
\epsilon
-confusing model and if
\eta
is small enough, then
C_{\epsilon}(\mu,\hat{M})
is upper bounded with respect to
C_{\epsilon}(\mu,M)
; and that is the desired conclusion.
The remaining steps consist in tuning
\eta>0
.
In (
S
TEP
2
), we show that
\hat{M}^{\ddagger\eta}
is well-defined if
\eta
is small enough relatively to
M
and
\epsilon
.
In (
S
TEP
3
) and (
S
TEP
4
), we detail how the optimal gain of
\hat{M}^{\ddagger\eta}
is influenced by the choice of
\eta
.
The point is to derive, in (
S
TEP
5
), a lower bound
c(M,\epsilon)>0
on
\eta
that is sufficient to guarantee that
\hat{M}^{\ddagger\eta}\in\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M)
.
The final result is obtained by choosing
\eta=c(M,\epsilon)
, that makes sure that
\hat{M}^{\ddagger\eta}
is well-defined, and to inject this expression in (
S
TEP
1
).

(
S
TEP
1
)

For every
\eta>0
with
\eta<\min\{1-\max(\widetilde{r}^{\dagger}),\frac{1}{2}\max(\widetilde{r}^{\dagger})\}
and
\hat{M}^{\ddagger}_{\eta}\in\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M)
, we have

C_{\epsilon}(\mu,\hat{M})\leq C_{\epsilon}(\mu,\widetilde{M})+2\beta_{0}\left(C_{\epsilon}(\mu,\widetilde{M})+2\log(2e\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert)\right)

(B.16)

where
\beta_{0}:=\max\{\max(\widetilde{r}^{\dagger})^{-1}\eta,\mathrm{dmin}(M)^{-1}\lVert\hat{M}-\widetilde{M}\rVert^{*}\}
.

Proof.

Unfolding the definition of
C_{\epsilon}(\mu,\hat{M})
, we have

\displaystyle C_{\epsilon}(\mu,\hat{M})

\displaystyle:=\inf_{\hat{M}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(\hat{M})}\left\{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(\hat{M}(z)||\hat{M}^{\prime}(z))\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{\leq}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(\hat{M}(z)||\hat{M}^{\ddagger}_{\eta}(z))

\displaystyle\overset{(\ddagger)}{=}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(\hat{p}(z)||\hat{p}^{\ddagger}_{\eta}(z))+\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(\hat{\nu}(z)||\hat{\nu}^{\ddagger}_{\eta}(z))

where

(\dagger)
uses that
\hat{M}_{\eta}^{\ddagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(\hat{M})
.
Both sums in
(\ddagger)
are reworked using
Lemma

B.24
.

We begin with the sum involving kernels.
Note that for
\gamma:=\mathrm{dmin}(\hat{p})^{-1}\lVert\widetilde{p}-\hat{p}\rVert^{*}_{\infty}
, we have
(1-\gamma)\hat{p}(s|z)\leq\widetilde{p}(s|z)\leq(1+\gamma)\hat{p}(s|z)
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
and
s\in\support(\widetilde{p}(z))
.
By assumption, we further have
\mathrm{dmin}(M)^{-1}\lVert\widetilde{M}-\hat{M}\rVert^{*}<\frac{1}{2}
, so we can bound the varaitions of
\mathrm{KL}(-||-)
by invoking
Lemma

B.24
to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(\hat{p}(z)||\hat{p}_{\eta}^{\ddagger}(z))

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{=}\sum_{z\notin\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(\hat{p}(z)||\hat{p}^{\dagger}(z))

\displaystyle\overset{(\ddagger)}{=}\sum_{z\notin\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(\hat{p}(z)||\widetilde{p}^{\dagger}(z))

\displaystyle\overset{(\lx@sectionsign)}{\leq}\left(1+\beta\right)\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(\widetilde{p}(z)||\widetilde{p}^{\dagger}(z))+\beta\log\left(e\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert\right)

where

(\dagger)
uses that
\hat{p}^{\ddagger\eta}=\hat{p}^{\dagger}
by definition and that
\hat{M}
and
\hat{M}^{\ddagger\eta}
only differ outside of
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
;

(\ddagger)
uses that
\hat{p}^{\dagger}(z)=\widetilde{p}^{\dagger}(z)
for
z\notin\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
; and

(\lx@sectionsign)
introduces
\beta:=2\penalty\ \mathrm{dmin}(M)^{-1}\lVert\widetilde{M}-\hat{M}\rVert^{*}
and invokes
Lemma

B.24
.

Continuing with the sum involving rewards, we note that for
\gamma:=\mathrm{dmin}(r)^{-1}\lVert\widetilde{r}-r\rVert^{*}_{\infty}
, we have
(1-\gamma)\hat{\nu}(i|z)\leq\widetilde{\nu}(i|z)\leq(1+\gamma)\hat{\nu}(i|z)
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
and
i=0,1
.
Introduce the shorthand
\alpha:=\max\{\max(\widetilde{r}^{\dagger})^{-1}\eta,\mathrm{dmin}(M)^{-1}\lVert\widetilde{M}-\hat{M}\rVert^{*}\}
.
Note that
\alpha<\frac{1}{2}
.
We have

\displaystyle\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(\hat{\nu}(z)||\hat{\nu}^{\ddagger}_{\eta}(z))

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{kl}\left(\hat{r}(z)\middle\|\hat{r}^{\dagger}(z)+\eta\mathbf{1}(z\in\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger})\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{=}\sum_{z\notin\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)}\mu(z)\mathrm{kl}\left(\hat{r}(z)\middle\|\hat{r}^{\dagger}(z)+\eta\mathbf{1}(z\in\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger})\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ddagger)}{=}\sum_{z\notin\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)}\mu(z)\mathrm{kl}\left(\hat{r}(z)\middle\|\widetilde{r}^{\dagger}(z)+\eta\mathbf{1}(z\in\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger})\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(\lx@sectionsign)}{\leq}\sum_{z\notin\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)}\mu(z)\mathrm{kl}\left(\hat{r}(z)\middle\|\left(1+\frac{\eta}{\max(\widetilde{r}^{\dagger})}\right)\widetilde{r}^{\dagger}(z)\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(\$)}{\leq}(1+2\alpha)\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(\widetilde{\nu}(z)||\widetilde{\nu}^{\dagger}(z))+2\alpha\log(2e)

where

(\dagger)
follows from
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)\cap\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}=\emptyset
;

(\ddagger)
follows by construction of
\hat{M}^{\dagger}
;

(\lx@sectionsign)
follows by monotonicity of
v\mapsto\mathrm{kl}(\hat{r}(z)||v)
for
v\geq\hat{r}(z)
; and

(\$)
follows by
Lemma

B.24
.

Conclude by combining everything together, with
\beta\leq\alpha
and
1\leq\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert
.

\blacksquare

(
S
TEP
2
)

We have

\max(\widetilde{r}^{\dagger})\leq 1-\exp\left(-\frac{\frac{C_{\epsilon}(e,\widetilde{M})}{\delta}+\log(2)}{1-\max({r})-\lVert\widetilde{r}-r\rVert_{\infty}}\right).

In particular,
\hat{M}_{\eta}^{\ddagger}
is well-defined for all
\eta
satisfying

0<\eta<\exp\left(-\frac{\frac{C_{\epsilon}(e,\widetilde{M})}{\delta}+\log(2)}{1-\max({r})-\lVert\widetilde{r}-r\rVert_{\infty}}\right)-\lVert\hat{r}-\widetilde{r}\rVert_{\infty}.

Proof.

Using the bound on
C_{\epsilon}(e,\widetilde{M})
from
Lemma

B.10
, we obtain

\displaystyle C_{\epsilon}(\mu,\widetilde{M})

\displaystyle\geq\delta\penalty\ C_{\epsilon}(e,\widetilde{M})

\displaystyle\geq\delta\max_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\left\{\mathrm{KL}(\widetilde{M}(z)||\widetilde{M}^{\dagger}(z))\right\}

\displaystyle\geq\delta\max_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\left\{\mathrm{KL}(\widetilde{\nu}(z)||\widetilde{\nu}^{\dagger}(z))\right\}

\displaystyle\geq\delta\max_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\left\{-\mathrm{Ent}(\widetilde{r}(z))+\widetilde{r}(z)\log\left(\frac{1}{\widetilde{r}^{\dagger}(z)}\right)+(1-\widetilde{r}(z))\log\left(\frac{1}{1-\widetilde{r}^{\dagger}(z)}\right)\right\}.

In particular, for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
,
(1-\widetilde{r}(z))\log\left(\frac{1}{1-\widetilde{r}^{\dagger}(z)}\right)\leq\mathrm{Ent}(\widetilde{r}(z))+\frac{1}{\delta}C_{\epsilon}(e,M)
.
We obtain the desired result by solving in
\hat{r}^{\dagger}(z)
and using
\max(\widetilde{r})\leq\max(r)+\lVert\widetilde{r}-r\rVert_{\infty}
.

\blacksquare

(
S
TEP
3
)

Assume that
\lVert\widetilde{M}-M\rVert^{*}<D(M)^{-1}
.
Let
\widetilde{\pi}^{\dagger}
be a bias optimal policy of
\widetilde{M}^{\dagger}
.
For all
\pi\in\Pi^{*}({M})
, we have

\displaystyle g^{\widetilde{\pi}^{\dagger}}(\hat{M}_{\eta}^{\ddagger})

\displaystyle\geq g^{*}(\widetilde{M}^{\dagger})-(1+2D({M}))\lVert\hat{M}-\widetilde{M}\rVert^{*}e_{\mathcal{Z}}+\eta\cdot g^{\widetilde{\pi}^{\dagger}}(e_{\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}},\hat{p}^{\dagger});

(B.17)

\displaystyle g^{\pi}(\hat{M}_{\eta}^{\ddagger})

\displaystyle\leq g^{*}(\widetilde{M}^{\dagger})+(1+2D({M}))\lVert\hat{M}-\widetilde{M}\rVert^{*}.

(B.18)

Proof.

Because
\lVert\widetilde{M}-M\rVert^{*}<D(M)^{-1}
, we have
D(\widetilde{M})\leq 2D(M)
by
Lemma

D.8
.

For
\pi\in\Pi
, the Cesàro-limit of
p_{\pi}^{t}
is denoted
\mu_{\pi}:=\lim T^{-1}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}p_{\pi}^{t}
.
We further denote
\widetilde{r}_{\pi}^{\dagger},\hat{r}_{\pi}^{\dagger}
and
\hat{r}_{\pi}^{\ddagger\eta}
the reward functions of
\pi
in
\widetilde{M}^{\dagger},\hat{M}^{\dagger}
and
\hat{M}^{\ddagger}_{\eta}
respectively.

Starting with the first inequality, writing
\pi\equiv\widetilde{\pi}^{\dagger}
for short, we have

\displaystyle g^{\pi}(\hat{M}_{\eta}^{\ddagger})=\hat{\mu}_{\pi}^{\ddagger\eta}\cdot\hat{r}_{\pi}^{\ddagger\eta}

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{=}\hat{\mu}_{\pi}^{\dagger}\cdot\left(\hat{r}_{\pi}^{\dagger}+\eta\penalty\ e_{\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ddagger)}{\geq}g^{\pi}(\widetilde{M})-\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{sp}(b^{\pi}(\widetilde{M}^{\dagger}))\right)\lVert\hat{M}^{\dagger}-\widetilde{M}^{\dagger}\rVert^{*}+\eta\penalty\ g^{\pi}(e_{\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}},\hat{p}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\geq g^{\pi}(\widetilde{M})-\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{sp}(b^{\pi}(\widetilde{M}^{\dagger}))\right)\lVert\hat{M}-\widetilde{M}\rVert^{*}+\eta\penalty\ g^{\pi}(e_{\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}},\hat{p}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\overset{(\lx@sectionsign)}{\geq}g^{\pi}(\widetilde{M})-\left(1+D(\widetilde{M})\right)\lVert\hat{M}-\widetilde{M}\rVert^{*}+\eta\penalty\ g^{\pi}(e_{\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}},\hat{p}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\overset{(\$)}{\geq}g^{\pi}(\widetilde{M})-\left(1+2D({M})\right)\lVert\hat{M}-\widetilde{M}\rVert^{*}+\eta\penalty\ g^{\pi}(e_{\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}},\hat{p}^{\dagger})

where
in
(\dagger)
we wrote
e_{\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}}:=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}}e_{z}
;

(\ddagger)
follows from
Lemma

D.9
;

(\lx@sectionsign)
follows from
\mathrm{sp}(b^{\pi}(\widetilde{M}^{\dagger}))\leq D(\widetilde{M})
, because
\pi\equiv\widetilde{\pi}^{\dagger}
is a bias optimal in
\widetilde{M}^{\dagger}
which is a critical model, hence
\mathrm{sp}(b^{\pi}(\widetilde{M}^{\dagger}))=\mathrm{sp}(b^{*}(\widetilde{M}^{\dagger}))\leq D(\widetilde{M})
by
Corollary

B.7
, assertion (1);
and
(\$)
follows from
D(\widetilde{M})\leq 2D(M)
.

For the second inequality, first note that since
\lVert\widetilde{M}-M\rVert<\frac{1}{4}(\max\{1,D(\pi_{u};M)\})^{-1}\Delta^{g}(M)
where
\pi_{u}
is the uniform policy, all gain optimal policies of
\widetilde{M}
are gain optimal in
M
by
Lemma

B.8
.
As
\lVert\widetilde{M}-M\rVert^{*}<\infty
, we further have
\widetilde{M}\sim M
so
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(\widetilde{M})\subseteq\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
.
So, for all
\pi\in\Pi^{*}({M})
, we have

\displaystyle g^{\pi}(\hat{M}_{\eta}^{\ddagger})=\hat{\mu}_{\pi}^{\ddagger\eta}\cdot\hat{r}_{\pi}^{\ddagger\eta}

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{=}\hat{\mu}_{\pi}\cdot\hat{r}_{\pi}

\displaystyle\overset{(\ddagger)}{\geq}g^{\pi}(\widetilde{M})-\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{sp}(b^{*}(\widetilde{M}))\right)\lVert\hat{M}-\widetilde{M}\rVert^{*}

\displaystyle\overset{(\lx@sectionsign)}{\geq}g^{\pi}(\widetilde{M})-\left(1+D(\widetilde{M})\right)\lVert\hat{M}-\widetilde{M}\rVert^{*}

\displaystyle\overset{(\$)}{\geq}g^{\pi}(\widetilde{M})-\left(1+2D({M})\right)\lVert\hat{M}-\widetilde{M}\rVert^{*},

where

(\dagger)
follows from
\hat{M}^{\ddagger\eta}\sim\widetilde{M}
and
\hat{M}^{\ddagger\eta}(z)=\hat{M}(z)
for
z\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}(\widetilde{M})
;

(\ddagger)
follows from the gain deviation inequality of
Lemma

D.9
;

(\lx@sectionsign)
follows from
Lemma

B.23
and

(\$)
follows from
D(\widetilde{M})\leq 2D(M)
.

\blacksquare

(
S
TEP
4
)

Assume that
\hat{p}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\geq\frac{1}{2}p(s^{\prime}|s,a)
for all
s,a,s^{\prime}
.
Writing
e:=\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}e_{s}
, we have

g^{\widetilde{\pi}^{\dagger}}(e_{\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}},\hat{p}^{\dagger})\geq\frac{\mathrm{dmin}(\hat{p})^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}}{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}e\geq\frac{\mathrm{dmin}(p)^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}}{2^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}e.

Proof.

For conciseness, we write
\pi\equiv\widetilde{\pi}^{\dagger}
.

Recall that
(1)
\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}=\mathcal{Z}^{**}(\widetilde{M}^{\dagger})\setminus\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
,
(2)
\pi\equiv\widetilde{\pi}^{\dagger}
is a bias optimal policy of
\widetilde{M}^{\dagger}
;
and that (3)
\widetilde{M}
and
\widetilde{M}^{\dagger}
are identical on
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
by (
B.13
) and
\widetilde{M}^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(\widetilde{M})
.
By mutual absolute continuity of
\widetilde{M}^{\dagger}
and
\hat{M}^{\dagger}
, recurrent components of
\pi
are the same in
\widetilde{M}^{\dagger}
and
\hat{M}^{\dagger}
.
They are denoted
\mathcal{Z}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{Z}_{m}
.
Let
\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{i}
be the unique probability invariant measure of
\pi
in
\hat{M}^{\dagger}
that is supported in
\mathcal{Z}_{i}
.
Given
s\in\mathcal{S}
, note that
g^{\widetilde{\pi}^{\dagger}}(s;e_{\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}},\hat{p}^{\dagger})
is a convex combination of
(\hat{\mu}_{i}^{\dagger}\cdot e_{\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}})_{1\leq i\leq m}
.
Accordingly, it is enough to show that for all
i=1,\ldots,m
, we have

\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}}\hat{\mu}_{i}^{\dagger}(z)\geq\frac{\mathrm{dmin}(\hat{p})^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}}{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}.

(B.19)

We focus on showing (
B.19
) for an arbitrary
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
.

Let
(s_{0},a_{0})\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}
maximizing
\hat{\mu}_{i}^{\dagger}(s_{0})
, where we use the writing shorthand
\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{i}(s):=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\hat{\mu}_{i}^{\dagger}(s,a)
;
In particular,
\hat{\mu}_{i}(s_{0})\geq\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert^{-1}
.

If
(s_{0},a_{0})\in\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}
, there is nothing left to prove.

Let us assume otherwise, i.e.,
(s_{0},a_{0})\notin\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}
.
Because
\pi
is gain optimal in
\widetilde{M}^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(\widetilde{M})
, it is not gain optimal in
M
, so
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\cap\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}\neq\emptyset
.
By considering all the paths in
\mathcal{Z}_{i}
of length
n=1,\ldots,\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert
starting from
s_{0}
, at least one must hit
\mathcal{Z}^{i}\cap\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}
.
So, there exist
(s^{\prime}_{0},a^{\prime}_{0})\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}\cap\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}
together with a path
(s_{1},a_{1},\ldots,s_{n})
of length
n\leq\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert
such that
s_{1}=s_{0}
,
s_{n}=s^{\prime}_{0}
,
p(s_{j+1}|s_{j},a_{j})>0
and
(s_{j},a_{j})\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}\setminus\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}
.
In particular,
(s_{j},a_{j})\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}(\widetilde{M})
for
j\leq n-1
, hence
\hat{p}^{\dagger}(s_{j},a_{j})=\hat{p}(s_{j},a_{j})
by definition of
\hat{M}^{\dagger}
.
We get

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{i}^{\dagger}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}_{0})

\displaystyle\geq\pi(a^{\prime}_{0}|s^{\prime}_{0})\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\pi(a_{j}|s_{j})\hat{p}^{\dagger}(s_{j+1}|s_{j},a_{j})\right)\hat{\mu}_{i}^{\dagger}(s_{0})

\displaystyle\geq\pi(a^{\prime}_{0}|s^{\prime}_{0})\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\pi(a_{j}|s_{j})\hat{p}(s_{j+1}|s_{j},a_{j})\right)\hat{\mu}_{i}^{\dagger}(s_{0})\overset{(\dagger)}{\geq}\frac{\mathrm{dmin}(\hat{p})^{n-1}}{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}\overset{(\ddagger)}{\geq}\frac{\mathrm{dmin}({p})^{n-1}}{2^{n-1}\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}

where

(\dagger)
follows from the observation that
\pi
is deterministic and

(\ddagger)
that
\hat{p}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\geq\frac{1}{2}p(s^{\prime}|s,a)
for all
s,a,s^{\prime}
.
Using that
n\leq\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert
, this shows (
B.19
) and concludes the proof.

\blacksquare

(
S
TEP
5
)

Assume that
\hat{p}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\geq\frac{1}{2}p(s^{\prime}|s,a)
for all
s,a,s^{\prime}
and that
\lVert\widetilde{M}-M\rVert^{*}<D(M)^{-1}
.
We have
\hat{M}_{\eta}^{\ddagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(\hat{M})
for every

\eta>\frac{2^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}(1+2D(M))\lVert\hat{M}-\widetilde{M}\rVert^{*}}{\mathrm{dmin}(p)^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}.

Proof.

By definition,
\hat{M}^{\ddagger\eta}\in\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(\hat{M})
if, and only if
(I)
\hat{M}^{\ddagger\eta}=\hat{M}
on
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon}(M)
and
(II)
\hat{M}^{\ddagger\eta}
belongs to the confusing set of
\hat{M}
, i.e.,
\Pi^{*}(\hat{M}^{\ddagger\eta})\cap\Pi^{*}(\hat{M})=\emptyset
.

For (I), note that
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon}(\hat{M})=\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
because
\lVert\hat{M}-M\rVert^{*}\leq\alpha(M)^{-1}\epsilon
, see (
B.12
);
And
\hat{M}^{\ddagger\eta}=\hat{M}
on
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
by construction, see (
B.14
) and (
B.15
).

We derive a sufficient condition for (II) following (
S
TEP
3
) and (
S
TEP
4
).
By
Lemma

B.5
,
\hat{M}^{\ddagger\eta}\in\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M)
if, and only if
g^{*}(\hat{M}^{\ddagger\eta})>g^{*}(\hat{M})
.

Because
\lVert\hat{M}-M\rVert^{*}<\frac{1}{4}D(M)^{-1}\Delta^{g}(M)
, all gain optimal policies of
\hat{M}
are gain optimal in
M
by
Lemma

B.9
.
In (
S
TEP
3
)
Equation

B.18
, we provide a bound of
g^{\pi}(\hat{M}^{\ddagger\eta})
for all
\pi\in\Pi^{*}(M)
and as a matter of fact, we have
g^{\pi}(\hat{M}^{\dagger})=g^{\pi}(\hat{M})
for all
\pi\in\Pi^{*}(M)
.
Indeed,
\hat{p}\sim p
so the recurrent classes of policies are the same on
\hat{M}
and
M
; And
\hat{p}^{\dagger}\gg\hat{p}
so gain optimal policies of
M
have their recurrent classes contained in
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
in
\hat{M}^{\ddagger\eta}
, on which
\hat{M}^{\ddagger\eta}=\hat{M}
.
Together with (
B.18
), we find:

g^{*}(\hat{M})\leq g^{*}(\widetilde{M}^{\dagger})+(1+2D(M))\lVert\hat{M}-\widetilde{M}\rVert^{*}.

So, by (
S
TEP
3
)
Equation

B.17
,
\widetilde{\pi}^{\dagger}
satisfies
g^{\widetilde{\pi}^{\dagger}}(\hat{M}^{\ddagger\eta})>g^{*}(\hat{M})
when

\eta>\frac{2(1+2D(M))\lVert\hat{M}-\widetilde{M}\rVert^{*}}{g^{\widetilde{\pi}^{\dagger}}(e_{\mathcal{Z}^{\dagger}},\hat{p}^{\dagger})}\overset{(\dagger)}{\geq}\frac{2^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}(1+2D(M))\lVert\hat{M}-\widetilde{M}\rVert^{*}}{\mathrm{dmin}(p)^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}

in particular, where
(\dagger)
follows from (
S
TEP
4
).
This concludes the proof.

\blacksquare

We may now conclude.
We proceed by upper bounding the information value of
\hat{M}
with respect to the information value of
\widetilde{M}
.
First, we pick some
\eta>0
