such that (I)
\hat{M}^{\ddagger\eta}
is well-defined and (II)
\hat{M}^{\ddagger\eta}\in\mathrm{Cnf}_{\epsilon}(M)
.
(
S
TEP
2
) and (
S
TEP
5
) provide sufficient conditions so that (I) and (II) are satisfied respectively.
For the conditions given by (
S
TEP
2
) and (
S
TEP
5
) to be compatible, we need

\frac{2^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}(1+2D(M))\lVert\hat{M}-\widetilde{M}\rVert^{*}}{\mathrm{dmin}(p)^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}<\exp\left(-\frac{\frac{C_{\epsilon}(e,\widetilde{M})}{\delta}+\log(2)}{1-\max({r})-\lVert\widetilde{r}-r\rVert_{\infty}}\right)-\lVert\hat{r}-\widetilde{r}\rVert_{\infty}.

Provided that
\lVert\widetilde{r}-r\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{2}(1-\max(r))
and using the upper bound
\lVert\hat{r}-\widetilde{r}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\lVert\hat{M}-\widetilde{M}\rVert^{*}
, we solve the above in
\lVert\hat{M}-\widetilde{M}\rVert^{*}
and find the sufficient condition:

\lVert\hat{M}-\widetilde{M}\rVert^{*}<\exp\left(-\frac{2\left(\frac{C_{\epsilon}(e,\widetilde{M})}{\delta}+\log(2)\right)}{1-\max({r})}+\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{dmin}(p)^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}{\mathrm{dmin}(p)^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert+2^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}(1+2D(M))}\right)\right).

Together with the condition
\hat{M},\widetilde{M}\in\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}
as given by (
B.11
), we retrieve indeed a condition in the form as given by (
B.10
) in
Lemma

B.13
.
Conclude with (
S
TEP
1
) by taking
\eta=\mathrm{dmin}(p)^{1-\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert^{-1}2^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}(1+2D(M))\lVert\hat{M}-\widetilde{M}\rVert^{*}
as allowed by (
S
TEP
5
), to obtain an bound of the form

C_{\epsilon}(\mu,\hat{M})\leq C_{\epsilon}(\mu,\widetilde{M})+\exp(\beta(M))\lVert\hat{M}-\widetilde{M}\rVert^{*}

(B.20)

where
\beta(M)
is polynomial in
\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert,D(M),\mathrm{dmin}(M),\Delta^{g}(M)
and
C(e,M)
.

Take
M=\hat{M},M^{\prime}=\widetilde{M}
to upper bound the information value of
M
relatively to
M^{\prime}
, and
M=\widetilde{M},M^{\prime}=\hat{M}
to lower bound the information value of
M
relatively to
M^{\prime}
.

\blacksquare

B.3
Properties of the regularized lower bound

In this section, we study the properties of the regularized lower bound
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
(
Definition

15
) which is given as the solution of the optimization problem below:

\min\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\Delta^{*}(z;M)+\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\lVert\mu\rVert_{2}^{2}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{s.t.}\hskip 10.00002pt\begin{cases}\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}({\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}};M)\\
\displaystyle\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}({\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}};M)}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\geq 1\end{cases}

where
{\bar{\epsilon}}\equiv({\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}})\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{3}
is the regularization hyperparameter.
The goal of this section is to show that the above has a solution
\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
called an
{\bar{\epsilon}}
-optimal exploration measures, and study the continuity and convergence properties of
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
and
\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
as
{\bar{\epsilon}}
and
M
vary.

Outline.

We begin by providing a simple bound on
K(M)
and
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
in
Section

B.3.1
.
In
Section

B.3.2
, we show that
{\bar{\epsilon}}
-optimal exploration measures exist, even when
{\bar{\epsilon}}=0
, and that
\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}
is unique when the convexification
\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}
is strict.
The remaining of the appendix is dedicated to the continuity properties of
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
and
\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
.
In
Section

B.3.3
, we present an alternative way of writing
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
that is adequate for that purpose.
In
Section

B.3.4
, we show that the sequence of
{\bar{\epsilon}}
-optimal exploration measures
(\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}})
converges to a special optimal exploration
\mu^{*}
when
{\bar{\epsilon}}\to 0
at the adequate rate (
Proposition

B.18
).
In
Section

B.3.5
, we show that as
{\bar{\epsilon}}\to 0
, we have
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)\downarrow K(M)
(
Proposition

B.20
), meaning that the regularized lower bound can approximates the true lower bound with arbitrary precision as the regularization vanishes.
Then, in
Section

B.3.6
, we show that if
\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}
is small relatively to
{\bar{\epsilon}}
, then
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M^{\prime})
is close to
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
.
Qualitatively, it means that

K(M)=\lim_{{\bar{\epsilon}}\to 0}\lim_{\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}\to 0}K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M^{\prime}).

(B.21)

The precise rates of convergence in (
B.21
) are given in
Proposition

B.21
.

B.3.1
A simple bound on the regret lower bound

In this paragraph, we provide an upper-bound of
K(M)
of order
\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert D(M)^{3}\Delta^{g}(M)^{-2}
, see
Theorem

B.14
where
D(M)
is the diameter and
\Delta^{g}(M)
is the gain-gap (
Definition

A.5
).
Our bound is of the same kind as
(
Auer et al. 2009
, Theorem 4)
, that indirectly provide a bound of order
\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert D(M)^{2}\Delta^{g}(M)^{-1}
by showing that
UCRL2
has regret growing as
\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert D(M)^{2}\Delta^{g}(M)^{-1}\log(T)
in the asymptotic.
Their bound is better than our own in most cases—as soon as
\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert\leq D^{2}(M)\Delta^{g}(M)^{-1}
in fact, but requires the construction and the analysis of a complete learning algorithm.

Overall, we believe that bounds on
K(M)
in terms of
D(M)
and
\Delta^{g}(M)
can be improved up to
\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert D(M)^{2}\Delta^{g}(M)^{-1}
.
This is left for future work.

Theorem B.14

(Simple upper bound of
K(M)
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an ambient space with Bernoulli rewards (
3
).
For every communicating model
M\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p,\nu)\in\mathcal{M}
, the regret lower bound is upper-bounded as

K(M)\leq\frac{16\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert D(M)^{3}}{\Delta^{g}(M)^{2}}.

where
D(M)
is the diameter of
M
(
2
) and
\Delta^{g}(M)
its gain-gap (
Definition

A.5
).
In particular,
K(M)<\infty
.

Proof.

Let
\pi\in\Pi^{\mathrm{SR}}
the fast covering policy provided by
Lemma

A.8
, of which the unique probability invariant measure satisfies
\min(\mu^{\pi})\geq\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert^{-1}D(M)^{-1}
.
By combining
Lemma

B.11
and
Lemma

B.10
, the information value of
\mu
is lower bounded as

C(\mu^{\pi},M)\geq\frac{C(e,M)}{\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert D(M)}\geq\frac{\Delta^{g}(M)^{2}}{16\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert D(M)^{3}}.

(B.22)

Using the formulation of the regret lower bound in terms of policies (
Proposition

7
, see (
9
)), we deduce that

K(M)\leq\frac{g^{*}(M)-g^{\pi}(M)}{C(\mu^{\pi},M)}\overset{(\dagger)}{\leq}\frac{16\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert D(M)^{3}}{\Delta^{g}(M)^{2}}

where

(\dagger)
invokes (
B.22
) and uses that rewards are
[0,1]
, so that
g^{*}(M)-g^{\pi}(M)\leq 1
.

\blacksquare

B.3.2
Existence of optimal and
{\bar{\epsilon}}
-optimal exploration measures

In this paragraph, we show that
K(M)
and
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
are achieved by invariant measures.
More specifically, we show that there exists
\mu^{*}\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
with
\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\geq 1
for all
M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)
and
\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu^{*}(z)\Delta^{*}(z)=K(M)
—with a similar result for the regularized lower bound.
This shows that the concepts of optimal exploration measure (
Section

2.2.2
) and
{\bar{\epsilon}}
-optimal exploration measure (
Section

4.3
) are well-defined.

Theorem B.15

(Existence of optimal exploration measures)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an arbitrary ambient space of Markov decision processes.
Let
M\in\mathcal{M}
be a communicating model.
Then the infemum of (
8
) is a minimum, i.e.,
K(M)
is equal to

\min\left\{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\Delta^{*}(z):\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)\mathrm{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)}\left\{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\right\}\geq 1\right\}.

Proof.

The idea of the proof is to write
K(M)
as the solution of an optimization problem over a compact region.
This task is not that easy because invariant measures contain irrelevant information; For instance, it can be shown that if
\mu^{*}\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
is an optimizer of (
8
) achieving
K(M)
, then for every gain optimal unichain policy
\pi^{*}
and every of its invariant measure
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Inv}(\pi^{*};M)
, the invariant measure
\mu^{*}+\mu^{\prime}
is also an optimizer of (
8
).
This makes the set of optimizers of (
8
) unbounded.

To remove the irrelevant information contained by invariant measures, we write
K(M)
in an alternative form.

Let
\Pi_{u}
be the set of policies that are unichain in
M
and denote
\Pi_{u}^{-}:=\Pi_{u}\setminus\Pi^{*}(M)
.
We denote
\mu_{\pi}
the unique probability invariant measure of
\pi\in\Pi_{u}
in
M
.
By
Proposition

A.15
, every invariant measure
\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
can be written as
\mu=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{u}}\lambda_{\pi}\mu_{\pi}
for some
(\lambda_{\pi})\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{\Pi_{u}}
.
Now, note the information value of
\mu=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{u}}\lambda_{\pi}\mu_{\pi}
satisfies

\displaystyle C(\mu,M)

\displaystyle:=\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)}\left\{\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{u}}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\lambda_{\pi}\mu_{\pi}(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{=}\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)}\left\{\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{u}^{-}}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\lambda_{\pi}\mu_{\pi}(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\right\}

where
(\dagger)
holds following from the observation that
\support(\mu_{\pi})\subseteq\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
for all
\pi\in\Pi_{u}\cap\Pi^{*}(M)
and that every confusing model coincide with
M
on
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
by definition.
Accordingly, in the optimization problem of (
8
), the optimization region
\mathrm{Inv}(M)
can be changed as

\displaystyle K(M)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\lambda\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{\Pi_{u}^{-}}}\left\{\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{u}^{-}}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\lambda_{\pi}\mu_{\pi}(z)\Delta^{*}(z):\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)}\left\{\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{u}^{-}}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\lambda_{\pi}\mu_{\pi}(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\right\}\geq 1\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{=}\inf_{\lambda\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{\Pi_{u}^{-}}}\left\{\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{u}^{-}}\lambda_{\pi}\left(g^{*}-g^{\pi}\right):\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)}\left\{\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{u}^{-}}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\lambda_{\pi}\mu_{\pi}(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\right\}\geq 1\right\}

where
(\dagger)
follows from the observvation that
\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu_{\pi}(z)\Delta^{*}(z)=g^{*}-g^{\pi}
for every unichain policy
\pi\in\Pi_{u}
, which can be shown using that
\mathbf{E}_{s}^{\pi}[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{1}(Z_{t}=z)]=T\mu_{\pi}(z)+\OH(1)
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
and initial state
s\in\mathcal{S}
.

We now restrict
(\mathbf{R}_{+})^{\Pi_{u}^{-}}
to a compact sub-region as follows.
By
Theorem

B.14
, we know that
K(M)\leq 16\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert D(M)^{3}\Delta^{g}(M)^{-2}
.
As every policy
\pi\in\Pi_{u}^{-}
is sub-optimal, we have
g^{*}-g^{\pi}\geq\Delta^{g}(M)
.
So, if
(\lambda_{\pi})\in(\mathbf{R}_{+})^{\Pi_{u}^{-}}
is such that
\lVert\lambda\rVert_{\infty}>16\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert D(M)^{3}\Delta^{g}(M)^{-3}
, we know that the invariant measure
\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{u}^{-}}\lambda_{\pi}\mu_{\pi}
does not achieve
K(M)
.
In other words, setting

\mathcal{F}:=\left\{\lambda\in(\mathbf{R}_{+})^{\Pi_{u}^{-}}:{\lVert\lambda\rVert_{\infty}\leq 16\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert D(M)^{3}\Delta^{g}(M)^{-3}\mathrm{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\atop\forall M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M),\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{u}^{-}}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\lambda_{\pi}\mu_{\pi}(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\geq 1}\right\},

we have

K(M)=\inf_{\lambda\in\mathcal{F}}\left\{\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{u}^{-}}\lambda_{\pi}\left(g^{*}-g^{\pi}\right)\right\}.

(B.23)

Because
\mathcal{F}
is compact and that the objective function in (
B.23
) is continuous, the infemum of (
B.23
) is a minimum.
Let
\lambda^{*}\in\mathcal{F}
achieving that minimum.
The invariant measure
\mu^{*}:=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{u}^{-}}\lambda^{*}_{\pi}\mu_{\pi}
is an optimal invariant measure of
M
, concluding the proof.

\blacksquare

With the same proof, the result is generalized to
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
.

Theorem B.16

(Existence of
{\bar{\epsilon}}
-optimal exploration measures)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an arbitrary ambient space of Markov decision processes.
Let
M\in\mathcal{M}
be a communicating model and
{\bar{\epsilon}}\equiv({\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}})\in\mathbf{R}_{+}^{3}
be a regularization hyperparameter.
Then the infemum of the optimization problem associated to
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
(see
Definition

15
) is a minimum, i.e.,
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
is equal to

\min\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\Delta^{*}(z)+\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\lVert\mu\rVert_{2}^{2}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathrm{s.t.}\hskip 10.00002pt\begin{cases}&\displaystyle\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}};M),\quad\mathrm{and}\\
&\displaystyle\inf_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}({\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}};M)}\left\{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\right\}\geq 1.\end{cases}\hskip-3.30002pt

Moreover, if the convexification is strict with
\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}>0
, then the minimum
\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}
is unique.

Proof.

The existence of the minimum is shown similarly to
Theorem

B.15
.
The uniqueness of
\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}
is guaranteed by strong convexity of the objective function.

\blacksquare

B.3.3
A convenient form for the
{\bar{\epsilon}}
-regularized lower bound

To prepare the analysis of the continuity properties of
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
and
\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
, we start by rewriting the
{\bar{\epsilon}}
-regularized lower bound with respect to information values as follows:

K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)=\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{I}(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}};M)}\left\{\frac{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\Delta^{*}(z;M)}{C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\mu,M)}+\frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\lVert\mu\rVert_{2}^{2}}{C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\mu,M)^{2}}\right\}

(B.24)

where
\mathcal{I}(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}};M):=\mathrm{Inv}(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}};M)\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z})
is the space of
\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}
-uniform probability invariant measures of
M
.
The issue in (
B.24
) is that Bellman gaps
\Delta^{*}(z;M)
are discontinuous in
M
even regarding the support-aware norm
\lVert-\rVert^{*}
.
A simple example of discontinuity point of
\Delta^{*}(-)
is provided in
Figure

4
.

model
M_{\theta}
1
2
0
0
0.5+\theta
0.5
\Delta^{*}
,
\theta>0
1
2
1+2\theta
\mathbf{0}
0
\theta
\Delta^{*}
,
\theta=0
1
2
1
\mathbf{1}
0
0

Figure 4:

A discontinuity of
\Delta^{*}(M)
.
A class of Bernoulli reward models with deterministic transitions parameterized by
\theta\in\Theta\equiv[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
.
Arrows are choices of actions that deterministically lead to the pointed state and labels are mean rewards.

This discontinuity is actually artificial.
If
\Delta^{*}(-)
is discontinuous indeed, the inner product between an invariant measure and the Bellman gaps
\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\Delta^{*}(z;M)
is not, because it can be rewritten as
\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)(g^{*}(M)-r(z))
using
Lemma

B.17
below, and
g^{*}-r
can be shown to be a continuous function of
M
.

Lemma B.17
.

For every communicating Markov decision process
M\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p,r)
and all invariant measure
\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
, we have

\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\Delta^{*}(z;M)=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\left(g^{*}(M)-r(z)\right).

Proof.

Let
\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
.
This follows from the direct computation:

\displaystyle\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\left(g^{*}(M)-r(z)\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{=}\sum_{z\equiv(s,a)\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\big(\Delta^{*}(z;M)+\left(e_{s}-p(z)\right)b^{*}(M)\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\Delta^{*}(z;M)+\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}b^{*}(s;M)\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\mu(s,a)-\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}b^{*}(s^{\prime};M)\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)p(s^{\prime}|z)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ddagger)}{=}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\Delta^{*}(z;M)+\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}b^{*}(s;M)\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\mu(s,a)-\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}b^{*}(s^{\prime};M)\sum_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}(s^{\prime})}\mu(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\Delta^{*}(z;M)

where

(\dagger)
follows by definition of
\Delta^{*}(z;M)
and

(\ddagger)
holds because
\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
.

\blacksquare

B.3.4
Convergence of regularized optimal exploration measures

In this section, we provide convergence guarantees for the unique solution
\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}
of the regularized lower bound
K_{{\bar{\epsilon}}}(M)
.
Informally, we show that the optimal
{\bar{\epsilon}}
-regularized exploration measure
\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}
converges to the optimal exploration measure
\mu^{*}
that further minimized the
\ell_{2}
-norm, provided that
\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}/\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}+\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\to 0
, see
Proposition

B.18
below.
Under the same conditions, this shows that the optimal
{\bar{\epsilon}}
-regularized exploration policy converges to a unique randomized policy when
\mu^{*}
puts positive mass on every state
\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}(s)}\mu^{*}(s,a)>0
for all
s\in\mathcal{S}
—or, equivalently, if there exists an optimal exploration policy that is state-recurrent.

Proposition B.18
.

Let
M\in\mathcal{M}
be a communicating Markov decision process.
Given a regularization hyperparameter
{\bar{\epsilon}}\equiv({\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}})
, we write
\mu^{*}_{{\bar{\epsilon}}}(M)
the optimal
{\bar{\epsilon}}
-regularized exploration measure.
Let
\mu^{*}\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
be the (unique) optimizer of
K(M)
as in (
8
) that further minimizes
\lVert\mu\rVert_{2}^{2}
among optimizers.
There exists a function
\psi:\mathbf{R}_{+}\to\mathbf{R}_{+}
such that

\forall{\bar{\epsilon}},\hskip 10.00002pt\lVert\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}-\mu^{*}\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\psi\left(\frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}}{\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}}+\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}+{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}\right)

(B.25)

with
\psi(x)=\OH(x)
as
x\to 0
.

Therefore,
Proposition

B.18
shows that one optimal exploration measure of
M
is a bit more special than the others regarding our regularization scheme, that we call the
central
optimal exploration measure, see
Definition

B.19
.
We use the terminology “central” for that measure maximizes the support over optimal exploration measures.

Definition B.0

(Central measure)
.

The
central
optimal exploration probability measure of
M
is the unique optimal exploration probability measure
\mu^{*}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z})
that achieves
K(M)
and that further minimizes
\lVert\mu\rVert_{2}^{2}
among optimizers of
K(M)
.

Proof.

For
{\bar{\epsilon}}\geq 0
, we write
f_{\bar{\epsilon}}(\mu):=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\Delta^{*}(z;M)+\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\lVert\mu\rVert_{2}^{2}
the objective function and
\mathcal{F}_{\bar{\epsilon}}
the feasible set associated to
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
seen as an optimization problem, i.e.,

\mathcal{F}_{\bar{\epsilon}}:=\left\{\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}};M):\forall M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}({\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}};M),\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\geq 1\right\}.

Assume throughout that
{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}
is small enough so that
\mathrm{Cnf}({\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}};M)=\mathrm{Cnf}(M)
, say
{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}<\Delta^{g}(M)
for e.g., see
Proposition

12
.

Let
\tilde{\mu}_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*}
be the optimizer of
f_{\bar{\epsilon}}
in
\mathcal{F}_{0}
and let
\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}_{\bar{\epsilon}}
be its projection on
\mathcal{F}_{\bar{\epsilon}}
.
By
Theorem

A.11
and provided that
\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}<\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert^{-1}D(M)^{-1}
, we have

\left\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}_{\bar{\epsilon}}-\tilde{\mu}_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert D(M)\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}\rVert_{1}.

(B.26)

For conciseness, denote
\alpha:=\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert D(M)
.
Now, we have

\displaystyle f_{\bar{\epsilon}}(\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*})

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{\geq}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\tilde{\mu}_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*}(z)\Delta^{*}(z)+\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(\ddagger)}{\geq}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(z)\Delta^{*}(z)+\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}_{\bar{\epsilon}}\rVert_{2}^{2}-\alpha{\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}_{\bar{\epsilon}}\rVert_{1}}\Big(\lVert\Delta^{*}\rVert_{1}+\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\left(2\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}\rVert_{1}+\alpha{\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}\rVert_{1}}\right)\Big)

\displaystyle\overset{(\lx@sectionsign)}{\geq}f_{\bar{\epsilon}}(\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*})+\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\lVert\tilde{\mu}_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{\prime}-\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*}\rVert_{2}^{2}-\alpha{\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}_{\bar{\epsilon}}\rVert_{1}}\Big(\lVert\Delta^{*}\rVert_{1}+\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\left(2\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}\rVert_{1}+\alpha{\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}\rVert_{1}}\right)\Big)

where

(\dagger)
follows by definition of
\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}
and
\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*}\in\mathcal{F}_{{\bar{\epsilon}}}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{0}
;

(\ddagger)
follows from (
B.26
) and a bit of algebra; and

(\lx@sectionsign)
holds because
f_{\bar{\epsilon}}(\mu)\geq f_{\bar{\epsilon}}(\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*})+\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\lVert\mu-\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*}\rVert_{2}^{2}
for all
\mu\in\mathcal{F}_{\bar{\epsilon}}
.
Together with (
B.26
), we conclude that

\lVert\tilde{\mu}_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*}-\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*}\rVert\leq\frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}\rVert_{1}}{1/\alpha}+\frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}_{\bar{\epsilon}}\rVert_{1}}{\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}/\alpha}\left(\lVert\Delta^{*}\rVert_{1}+\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\left(2\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}\rVert_{1}+\frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}\rVert_{1}}{1/\alpha}\right)\right)

(B.27)

Note that
\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}_{\bar{\epsilon}}\rVert_{1}=\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}\rVert_{1}+\OH(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})
by (
B.26
).

We finish the proof by showing that
\mu^{*}=\tilde{\mu}_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*}
when
\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}
is small enough.

In the remaining of the proof, we more simply write
\epsilon
in place of
\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}
.
Consider the relaxation of the optimization problem
\min_{\mu\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\lVert\mu\rVert_{2}^{2}
s.t.
f_{0}(\mu)\leq K(M)
given by

\min_{\mu\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\lVert\mu\rVert_{2}^{2}+\lambda(f_{0}(\mu)-K(M))

for
\lambda\geq 0
.
Note that Slater’s condition is satisfied.
Indeed, the constraints “
\mu\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
” and “
f_{0}(\mu)=K(M)
” are polytopes, while the constraint “
\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\geq 1
for all
M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)
” is equivalently written in the form of “
\psi(\mu)\leq 0
” where
\psi(\mu):=1-\sup_{M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))
that is convex.
By taking
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z})
the unique probability invariant measure of the uniform policy
\pi_{0}(a|s):=\lvert\mathcal{A}(s)\rvert^{-1}
and considering
\lambda_{0}\mu_{0}\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
for
\lambda_{0}>0
large enough such that
\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\lambda_{0}\mu_{0}(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\geq 2
for all
M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)
, we obtain a point in the relative interior of the feasible region
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.
So strong duality holds, see
(
Boyd et al. 2004
, §5.2.3)
.
We deduce that

\max_{\lambda\geq 0}\min_{\mu\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\left(\lVert\mu\rVert_{2}^{2}+\lambda(f_{0}(\mu)-K(M))\right)=\min_{\mu\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\max_{\lambda\geq 0}\left(\lVert\mu\rVert_{2}^{2}+\lambda(f_{0}(\mu)-K(M))\right).

(B.28)

By definition,
\mu^{*}
is the unique solution of
\min_{\mu\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\lVert\mu\rVert_{2}^{2}
s.t.
f_{0}(\mu)\leq K(M)
.
Let
\lambda^{*}\equiv\frac{1}{\epsilon^{*}}
be the associated Lagrange multiplier.
By definition again,
\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{\epsilon}
is the unique solution of
\min_{\mu\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}f_{0}(\mu)+\epsilon\lVert\mu\rVert_{2}^{2}
, so
\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{\epsilon}
is the unique solution of
\min_{\mu\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\lVert\mu\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}(f_{0}(\mu)-K(M))
.
Let
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon^{*}]
.
Note that for
\mu\in\mathcal{F}_{0}
, we have
\lVert\mu\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}(f_{0}(\mu)-K(M))\geq\lVert\mu\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{\epsilon^{*}}(f_{0}(\mu)-K(M))
, so that taking the minimum for
\mu\in\mathcal{F}_{0}
, we obtain

\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{\epsilon}\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\left(f_{0}(\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{\epsilon})-K(M)\right)\geq\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{\epsilon^{*}}\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{\epsilon^{*}}\left(f_{0}(\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{\epsilon^{*}})-K(M)\right)=\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{\epsilon^{*}}\rVert_{2}^{2}=\lVert\mu^{*}\rVert_{2}^{2}.

It follows that
(\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{\epsilon},\frac{1}{\epsilon})
is a solution of (
B.28
), so
\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{\epsilon}=\mu^{*}
by uniqueness of
\mu^{*}
.

Accordingly, we have
\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}=\mu^{*}
for
\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\leq\epsilon^{*}
.
It further follows that
\lVert\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}_{\bar{\epsilon}}\rVert_{1}=\lVert\mu^{*}\rVert_{1}+\OH(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})+\OH(\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}})
.
Combined with (
B.27
), we conclude.

\blacksquare

B.3.5
The regularized lower bound approximates the lower bound

In this paragraph, we prove that the regularized lower bound
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
converges to the true lower bound
K(M)
as
{\bar{\epsilon}}\to 0
and provide the rate of convergence at first order, see
Proposition

B.20
below.
This result is a straight forward consequence of the approximation properties of the leveling operation
[-]_{\epsilon}
described by
Proposition

12
and of the possibility of approximating invariant measures by uniform invariant measures, see
Theorem

A.11
.

Proposition B.20
.

Let
M\in\mathcal{M}
be a communicating Markov decision process.
Then, as the regularization hyperparameter
{\bar{\epsilon}}\equiv({\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}})
vanishes, we have

K(M)\leq K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)\leq K(M)+\OH(\lVert{\bar{\epsilon}}\rVert_{\infty}).

More precisely, if
{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}<\Delta^{g}(M)
and
\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}<\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{dmin}(\mu^{*})\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert D(M)\lVert\mu^{*}\rVert_{1}^{-1}
where
\mu^{*}
is an arbitrary optimizer of
K(M)
, then

\displaystyle K(M)

\displaystyle\leq K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)

\displaystyle\leq K(M)+\OH\left(\lVert\mu^{*}\rVert_{1}\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert D(M)\left(\frac{K(M)}{\mathrm{dmin}(\mu^{*})}+\lVert\Delta^{*}(M)\rVert_{\infty}\right)\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}+\lVert\mu^{*}\rVert_{2}^{2}\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}+{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}\right).

Proof.

We begin with the upper bound.
Following
Proposition

12
, if
{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}<\Delta^{g}(M)
, then
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(M)=\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
and in particular
\mathrm{Cnf}({\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}};M)=\mathrm{Cnf}(M)
.
Moreover, we have
\mathrm{Inv}(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}};M)\subseteq\mathrm{Inv}(M)
.
So, if
\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*}
is the optimum of
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
, then
\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*}\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)
and
\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*}(z)\mathrm{KL}(M(z)||M^{\dagger}(z))\geq 1
for all
M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(M)
.
Accordingly,
\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*}
is within the feasible region of the optimization problem associated to
K(M)
, so

\displaystyle K(M)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*}(z)\Delta^{*}(z;M)\leq\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*}(z)\Delta^{*}(z;M)+\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\lVert\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*}\rVert_{2}^{2}=K_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*}(M).

We continue with the lower bound.
Let
\mu^{*}
be an optimum of
K(M)
.
As
\mu^{*}\lVert\mu^{*}\rVert_{1}^{-1}\in\mathrm{Inv}(M)\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z})
, by
Theorem

A.11
, there exists
\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}\in\mathrm{Inv}(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}};M)
such that

\lVert\mu^{*}-\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert D(M){\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}\lVert\mu^{*}\rVert_{1}}.

(B.29)

By
Proposition

12
again, if
{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}<\Delta^{g}(M)
, then
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(M)=\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
, so
\mathrm{Cnf}({\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}};M)=\mathrm{Cnf}(M)
.
Introduce the quantity
\alpha:=\mathrm{dmin}(\mu^{*})^{-1}\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert D(M)\lVert\mu^{*}\rVert_{1}>0
and note that
\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}\geq(1-\alpha\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})\mu^{*}
.
So, if
\alpha\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}<1
, we have
(1-\alpha\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})^{-1}\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}\geq\mu^{*}
.
Following
\mathrm{Cnf}({\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}};M)=\mathrm{Cnf}(M)
, we find that
(1-\alpha\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})^{-1}\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}
is within the feasible region of the optimization problem associated to
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
.
So, writing
\beta:=\min(\mu_{0})^{-1}\lVert\mu^{*}\rVert_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1-\alpha\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}(z)\Delta^{*}(z;M)+\frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\lVert\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}\rVert_{2}^{2}}{(1-\alpha\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})^{2}}

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{\leq}\frac{1}{1-\alpha\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\left(\mu^{*}(z)+\beta\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}\right)\Delta^{*}(z;M)+\frac{2\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\left(\lVert\mu^{*}\rVert_{2}^{2}+\beta^{2}\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}^{2}\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert^{2}\right)}{(1-\alpha\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})^{2}}

\displaystyle=K(M)+\OH\left(\lVert{\bar{\epsilon}}\rVert_{\infty}\right)

where

(\dagger)
invokes (
B.29
) together with
\lVert u+v\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq 2(\lVert u\rVert_{2}^{2}+\lVert v\rVert_{2}^{2})
.

\blacksquare

B.3.6
Properties of the regularized lower bound

The most difficult part are continuity properties.
In this paragraph, we show that for a fixed regularization hyperparameter
{\bar{\epsilon}}\in(\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*})^{3}
, we have
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M^{\prime})\to K(M)
and
\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M^{\prime})\to\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
as
\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}\to 0
.
In other words, we show that both the regularized lower bound
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(-)
and its optimizer
\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(-)
are continuous with respect to the support-aware norm
\lVert-\rVert^{*}
.

M_{\theta}
:
1
2
3
4
\theta_{1}
\theta_{2}
\theta_{3}
\theta_{4}
\theta_{5}
M^{x}
:
1
2
3
4
0.1
0.1
0.5
0.55-x
0.55-x

Figure 5:

A discontinuity of
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
when the product form (
4
) is dropped.
A set of Bernoulli reward models with deterministic transitions parameterized by
\theta\in\Theta\equiv[0,1]^{5}
(to the left).
Arrows are choices of actions that deterministically lead to the pointed state and labels are mean rewards.

Our proof requires a few technical assumptions on the structure of the ambient space
\mathcal{M}
.
The first is that
\mathcal{M}
is a space of Bernoulli reward Markov decision processes (
3
).
This assumption can probably be generalized to Gaussian rewards or, more generally, to rewards among a single parameter exponential family.
The second is that
\mathcal{M}
is in product form (
4
).
Without this assumption, the set of leveled confusing models
\mathrm{Cnf}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
can be discontinuous in
M
and information values (
Definition

B.3
) can be discontinuous, provoking discontinuities of
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
that cannot be addressed easily.

A simple example of discontinuity is provided in
Figure

5
.
Consider the ambient spaces
\mathcal{M}:=\{M_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta\}
(in product form) and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}:=\{M_{\theta}:\theta_{1}=\theta_{2}\mathrm{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\theta_{4}=\theta_{5}\}
(not in product form) and the model
M^{x}\in\mathcal{M}\cap\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
as given to the right of
Figure

5
.
Fix
{\bar{\epsilon}}\geq 0
a small enough regularizer (possibly
0
).
As soon as
\lvert x\rvert
is small enough, we always have
\mathrm{Cnf}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M^{x};\mathcal{M})\neq\emptyset
by considering the confusing model with
\theta_{2}\approx\theta_{3}\approx 1
, so that
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M^{x};\mathcal{M})>0
—In fact,
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M^{x};\mathcal{M})
is a continuous function of
x
as shown by
Proposition

B.21
below.
When the ambient space is
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
however, this confusing model doesn’t exist in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and we find

\mathrm{Cnf}_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M^{x};\mathcal{M}^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\emptyset&\text{if $x\geq 0$};\\
\Big\{M_{\theta}:\theta_{1}=\theta_{2}=0.1,\theta_{4}=\theta_{5}=0.55-x\mathrm{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\theta_{3}\geq 1-2x\Big\}&\text{if $x<0$}.\end{cases}

So, we have
\inf_{x<0}K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M^{x};\mathcal{M}^{\prime})>0
while
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M^{x};\mathcal{M}^{\prime})=0
if
x\geq 0
, so
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M^{x};\mathcal{M}^{\prime})
is not continuous at
x=0
.
Accordingly, the product form assumption (
4
) on the ambient space cannot be dropped in general.

Proposition B.21
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an ambient space with Bernoulli rewards (
3
) in product form (
4
).
Let
M\in\mathcal{M}
be a communicating model.
The regularized lower bound is continuous in the follow sense: There exist functions
\psi_{K},\psi_{\mu}:\mathbf{R}_{+}\to[0,\infty]
such that for all regularization hyperparameter
{\bar{\epsilon}}\equiv({\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}})\in(\mathbf{R}_{+}^{*})^{3}
and all model
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
, the errors
\lvert K_{{\bar{\epsilon}}}(M^{\prime})-K_{{\bar{\epsilon}}}(M)\rvert
and
\lVert\mu^{*}_{{\bar{\epsilon}}}(M^{\prime})-\mu^{*}_{{\bar{\epsilon}}}(M)\rVert_{\infty}
are respectively bounded by

\begin{gathered}\psi_{K}\left(\frac{\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}}{(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})^{3\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}}+\mathbf{1}\left(\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert>\beta_{1}\min\left\{{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}},\exp\left(-\frac{\beta_{2}}{(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}}\right)\right\}\right)\right)\\
\mathrm{and}\\
\psi_{\mu}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert^{*}}{(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})^{3\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}}}+\mathbf{1}\left(\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert>\beta_{1}\min\left\{{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}},\exp\left(-\frac{\beta_{2}}{(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}}\right)\right\}\right)\right)\end{gathered}

with
\psi_{K}(x)=\OH(x)
and
\psi_{\mu}(x)=\OH(x)
when
x\to 0
, and
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in\mathbf{R}_{+}
depend on
M
.

Proof.

Let
M_{1},M_{2}
be in the neighborhood of
M
, denoting
M_{i}=(\mathcal{Z},p_{i},r_{i})
for
i=1,2
, with
M\in\{M_{1},M_{2}\}
.
The result will follow by upper-bounding
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M_{2})
relatively to
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M_{1})
.
Using
Lemma

B.17
and
Equation

B.24
, for
i=1,2
, the
{\bar{\epsilon}}
-regularized regret lower bound of
M_{i}
is rewritten with respect to information values as follows:

K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M_{i})=\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{I}(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}};M_{i})}\left\{\frac{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)(g^{*}(M)-r_{i}(z))}{C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\mu,M)}+\frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\lVert\mu\rVert_{2}^{2}}{C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\mu,M)^{2}}\right\}.

The dot product
\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)(g^{*}(M)-r(z))
will be more concisely written
\mu(g^{*}-r)
.
Let
\mu_{1}^{*}\in\mathcal{I}(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}};M)
the optimum of
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M_{1})
and let
\mu_{2}
be its projection onto
\mathcal{I}(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}};M_{2})
as given by
Theorem

A.11
.
We introduce the following short-hand notations:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mu

\displaystyle:=\mu_{2}-\mu^{*}_{1}

(B.30)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\Delta

\displaystyle:=g^{*}(M_{2})+r(M_{1})-g^{*}(M_{1})-r(M_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}C

\displaystyle:=C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\mu_{2},M_{2})-C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\mu_{1},M_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}M

\displaystyle:=\lVert M_{2}-M_{1}\rVert^{*}

Using these notations, we have

\displaystyle K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mu_{2}(g^{*}_{2}-r_{2})}{C_{{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}}(\mu_{2},M_{2})}+\frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\lVert\mu_{2}\rVert_{2}^{2}}{C_{{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}}(\mu_{2},M_{2})^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\mu_{1}+\mathrm{d}\mu)(g^{*}_{1}-r_{1}+\mathrm{d}\Delta)}{C_{{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}}(\mu_{1},M_{1})+\mathrm{d}C}+\frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\lVert\mu_{1}+\mathrm{d}\mu\rVert_{2}^{2}}{(C_{{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}}(\mu_{1},M_{1})+\mathrm{d}C)^{2}}.

(B.31)

Roughly speaking, when
\mathrm{d}M\ll\mathrm{dmin}({\bar{\epsilon}})
, then all the variational quantities
\mathrm{d}\mu
,
\mathrm{d}\Delta
and
\mathrm{d}C
are of order
\mathrm{d}M
.
By injecting that in (
B.31
), we find
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M_{2})\leq K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M_{1})+\OH(\mathrm{d}M)
and conclude accordingly.
The point is that to make this argument precise.

(
S
TEP
1
)

Bounding the variational quantities

All variational quantities of (
B.30
) are bounded relatively to
\mathrm{d}M
.
We find:

\displaystyle\lVert\mathrm{d}\mu\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}{(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}\mathrm{dmin}(p)^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}}\mathrm{d}M

(B.32)

\displaystyle\lVert\mathrm{d}\Delta\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq(1+2D(M))\mathrm{d}M

\displaystyle\lvert\mathrm{d}C\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{2(C_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}(e,M)+{e^{\beta(M)}}\mathrm{d}M)\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}{(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}\mathrm{dmin}(p)^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}}+e^{\beta(M)}\right)\mathrm{d}M.

The various upper-bounds in (
B.32
) require conditions on
\mathrm{d}M
and
{\bar{\epsilon}}
.
The bound on
\lVert\mathrm{d}\mu\rVert_{\infty}
is obtained directly using
Corollary

A.14
and holds uniformly for
\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}
and
\mathrm{d}M
.
The bound on
\lVert\mathrm{d}\Delta\rVert_{\infty}
is obtained with a combination of
Lemmas

B.22
and
B.23
; Indeed, by
Lemma

B.22
,

\displaystyle\lVert\mathrm{d}\Delta\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq 2\lVert r^{\prime}-r\rVert_{\infty}+\frac{1}{2}\min\left\{\mathrm{sp}(b^{*}(M_{1})),\mathrm{sp}(b^{*}(M_{2}))\right\}\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{\leq}2\lVert r^{\prime}-r\rVert_{\infty}+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{sp}(b^{*}(M))\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert

\displaystyle\overset{(\ddagger)}{\leq}2\lVert r^{\prime}-r\rVert_{\infty}+\frac{1}{2}D(M)\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert\leq\left(2+D(M)\right)\mathrm{d}M

where

(\dagger)
follows from
M\in\{M_{1},M_{2}\}
and

(\ddagger)
follows from
Lemma

B.23
.
The resulting bound holds uniformly for
\mathrm{d}M
and
{\bar{\epsilon}}
.

Lastly, the bound on
\mathrm{d}C
in (
B.32
) is obtained with a combination of
Lemmas

B.10
,
B.13
and
B.12
as follows:

\displaystyle\lvert\mathrm{d}C\rvert

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{=}\left\lvert C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\mu^{\prime},M^{\prime})-C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\mu,M)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\mu^{\prime},M^{\prime})-C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\mu^{\prime},M)\right\rvert+\left\lvert C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\mu^{\prime},M)-C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\mu,M)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\overset{(\ddagger)}{\leq}e^{\beta(M)}\mathrm{d}M+\frac{2C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\mu,M)\mathrm{d}\mu}{\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}}

\displaystyle\overset{(\lx@sectionsign)}{\leq}e^{\beta(M)}\mathrm{d}M+\frac{2C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(e,M)\mathrm{d}\mu}{\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}}

\displaystyle\overset{(\$)}{\leq}\left(e^{\beta(M)}+\frac{2C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(e,M)\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}{(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}\mathrm{dmin}(p)^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}}\right)\mathrm{d}M

where

(\dagger)
rewrite
C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\mu_{2},M_{2})-C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\mu_{1},M_{1})
by using that
M\in\{M_{1},M_{2}\}
and introducing
M^{\prime}\in\{M_{1},M_{2}\}\setminus\{M\}
;

(\ddagger)
bound the first term using
Lemma

B.13
and the second using
Lemma

B.12
;

(\lx@sectionsign)
bounds
C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\mu,M)\leq C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(e,M)
using
Lemma

B.10
; and

(\$)
unfolds the upper bound of
\mathrm{d}\mu
.
Note that for
Lemmas

B.12
and
B.13
to be applicable in
(\ddagger)
, we need
\mathrm{d}\mu\leq\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}
,
\mathrm{Cnf}({\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}};M)=\mathrm{Cnf}(M)
and
\mathrm{d}M\leq\min\{{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}\alpha(M)^{-1},\exp(-(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})^{-1}\beta_{1}(M)-\beta_{2}(M)\}
where
\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
are polynomial functions
\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert
,
D
,
\mathrm{dmin}(M)
and
C(e,M)
as given in
Lemma

B.13
.
We obtain the conditions

\begin{gathered}{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}+\alpha(M)\mathrm{d}M<\Delta^{g}(M)\\
\mathrm{d}M\leq\min\left\{\frac{{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}}{\alpha(M)},\exp\left(-\frac{\beta_{1}(M)}{(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}}-\beta_{2}(M)\right),\frac{(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}\mathrm{dmin}(p)^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}}{2\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}\right\}\end{gathered}

(B.33)

Under (
B.33
), all the bounds of (
B.32
) hold and in particular, the variational quantities
\mathrm{d}\mu
,
\mathrm{d}\Delta
and
\mathrm{d}C
are of order
\OH(\mathrm{d}M)
; Hence are vanishing with
\mathrm{d}M
.

(
S
TEP
2
)

Bounding the variations of
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}

When
\mathrm{d}M
is vanishing, only keeping the terms of first order, we find

\displaystyle K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1+\frac{\mathrm{d}C}{C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\mu_{1},M_{1})}}\frac{(\mu_{1}+\mathrm{d}\mu)(g^{*}_{1}-r_{1}+\mathrm{d}\Delta)}{C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\mu_{1},M_{1})}+\left(\frac{1}{1+\frac{\mathrm{d}C}{{C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}}(\mu_{1},M_{1})}}\right)^{2}\frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\lVert\mu_{1}+\mathrm{d}\mu\rVert_{2}^{2}}{C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\mu_{1},M_{1})^{2}}

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{\lesssim}K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M_{1})+\frac{(g^{*}_{1}-r_{1})\mathrm{d}\mu+\mu_{1}\mathrm{d}\Delta+(1+2\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}})\mathrm{d}C}{C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\mu_{1},M_{1})}+\frac{2\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\mu_{1}\mathrm{d}\mu}{C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\mu_{1},M_{1})^{2}}

\displaystyle\overset{(\ddagger)}{\leq}K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M_{1})+\frac{(g^{*}_{1}-r_{1})\mathrm{d}\mu+\mu_{1}\mathrm{d}\Delta+(1+2\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}})\mathrm{d}C}{f(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(e,M_{1})}+\frac{2\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\mu_{1}\mathrm{d}\mu}{f(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})^{2}C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(e,M_{1})^{2}}

\displaystyle\overset{(\lx@sectionsign)}{\lesssim}K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M_{1})+\frac{\lVert\mathrm{d}\mu\rVert_{1}+\lVert\mathrm{d}\Delta\rVert_{\infty}+3\lvert\mathrm{d}C\rvert}{f(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(e,M)}+\frac{2\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\lVert\mathrm{d}\mu\rVert_{\infty}}{f(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})^{2}C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(e,M)^{2}}

\displaystyle\overset{(\$)}{\leq}K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M_{1})+\OH\left(\frac{1}{f(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})}\left(\frac{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert\left(1+\frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}}{C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(e,M)f(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})}\right)}{C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(e,M)f(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})}+\frac{1}{f(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})}+e^{\beta(M)}\right)\mathrm{d}M\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(\#)}{=}K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M_{1})+\OH\left(\frac{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}{f(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})}\left(\frac{1+f(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(e,M)^{2}}{C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(e,M)^{2}f(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})^{2}}+e^{\beta(M)}\right)\mathrm{d}M\right)

where

(\dagger)
is obtained by seeing every variational quantity
\mathrm{d}\mu
,
\mathrm{d}\Delta
and
\mathrm{d}C
as an infinitesimal quantity and developping at first order—Note that to do so, we need
C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\mu_{i},M)
to be bounded below in a neighborhood of
M
, which is indeed the case by combining
Lemmas

B.10
and
B.11
;

(\ddagger)
follows by combining
Lemma

A.12
assertion (1) and
Lemma

B.10
while introducing the shorthand
f(\epsilon):=\epsilon^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}\mathrm{dmin}(p)^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert-1}
;

(\lx@sectionsign)
follows by relating
C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(e,M)
and
C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(e,M_{1})
using
Lemma

B.13
and expanding at first order; and

(\$
) follows by expanding everything using (
B.32
) and
D(M)\leq\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert f(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})^{-1}
; and

(\#)
follows by rearranging terms.

In the end, by keeping only the dominant terms, we find a bound of the form

K_{{\bar{\epsilon}}}(M_{2})\leq K_{{\bar{\epsilon}}}(M_{1})+\OH\left(\frac{\lVert M_{2}-M_{1}\rVert^{*}}{(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})^{3\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}}\right)

under a condition of the form

\lVert M^{\prime}-M\rVert\leq\beta_{1}\min\left\{{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}},\exp\left(-\frac{\beta_{2}}{(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}}\right)\right\}

(B.34)

which is obtained by keeping the dominant terms in (
B.33
).
Hence the bound on the variations of
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}
described in
Proposition

B.21
.

(
S
TEP
3
)

Deducing the variations of
\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}

By symmetry of the previous argument,
K_{{\bar{\epsilon}}}(M_{2})
is also upper-bounded by
K_{{\bar{\epsilon}}}(M_{1})
.
For short, introduce
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(\mu;M):=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\Delta^{*}(z;M)+\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\lVert\mu\rVert_{2}^{2}
the objective function in the optimization problem associated with
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M)
.
Setting
\mu_{2}^{\prime}=C_{\bar{\epsilon}}(\mu_{2},M_{2})\mu_{2}
the re-scaled version of
\mu_{2}
, we have shown that
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M_{2})\leq K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(\mu_{2}^{\prime};M_{2})\leq K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M_{2})+\mathrm{B}
where
\mu_{2}^{\prime}
is a feasible point of
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M_{2})
and
\mathrm{B}
is of order

\mathrm{B}=\OH\left(\frac{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}{f(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})}\left(\frac{1+f(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(e,M)^{2}}{C_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(e,M)^{2}f(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})^{2}}+e^{\beta(M)}\right)\mathrm{d}M\right)

where
f(\epsilon):=\mathrm{dmin}(p)^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}\epsilon^{\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}
.

A direct computation shows
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(\mu^{\prime};M)=K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(\mu;M)+\nabla_{\mu}K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(\mu;M)(\mu^{\prime}-\mu)+\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}\lVert\mu^{\prime}-\mu\rVert_{2}^{2}
for all
\mu,\mu^{\prime}\in\mathbf{R}^{\mathcal{Z}}
.
As
\mu_{2}^{\prime}
is in the feasible region of
K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M_{2})
and
\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*}(M_{2})
is the associated minimizer, we have
\nabla_{\mu}K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*}(M_{2});M_{2})(\mu_{2}^{\prime}-\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M_{2}))\geq 0
, so

\lVert\mu_{2}^{\prime}-\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*}(M_{2})\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(\mu^{\prime}_{2};M_{2})-K_{\bar{\epsilon}}(M_{2})}{\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}}\leq(\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}})^{-1}\mathrm{B}.

(B.35)

Now, at first order, we have

\displaystyle\lVert\mu^{\prime}_{2}-\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*}(M_{1})\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\lVert C_{\bar{\epsilon}}(\mu_{2},M_{2})(\mu_{1}^{*}+\mathrm{d}\mu)-C_{\bar{\epsilon}}(\mu_{1}^{*},M_{1})\mu_{1}^{*}\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{\bar{\epsilon}}(e,M_{1})\lVert\mathrm{d}\mu\rVert_{\infty}+\lvert\mathrm{d}C\rvert.

(B.36)

We conclude by combining (
B.35
) and (
B.36
) and by invoking the bounds of (
B.32
).
Then, this bound can be simplified as

\left\lVert\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*}(M_{2})-\mu_{\bar{\epsilon}}^{*}(M_{1})\right\rVert_{\infty}=\OH\left(\sqrt{\frac{\lVert M_{2}-M_{1}\rVert^{*}}{(\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}})^{3\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert}\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}}}\right)

under same condition (
B.33
) that we simplify as (
B.34
) as well.
Hence the bound on the variations of
\mu^{*}_{\bar{\epsilon}}
described in
Proposition

B.21
.

\blacksquare

B.3.7
Auxiliary results

In this paragraph, we provide a few instrumental results, starting with the sensibility of the optimal gain function to perturbation of the reward vector and of the transition kernel.

Lemma B.22

(Variations of the optimal gain)
.

Let
M\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p,r)
and
M^{\prime}\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p^{\prime},r^{\prime})
be two Markov decision processes (possibly of infinite diameter).
If
\mathrm{sp}(g^{*}(M))=0
, then

\lVert g^{*}(M^{\prime})-g^{*}(M)\rVert_{\infty}\leq\lVert r^{\prime}-r\rVert_{\infty}+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{sp}(b^{*}(M))\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert_{\infty}.

Proof.

Let
\pi^{*}
be a bias optimal policy of
M
.
We have
\mathrm{sp}(g^{\pi^{*}}(M))=0
, so by
Lemma

D.9
,

\lVert g^{\pi^{*}}(M^{\prime})-g^{*}(M)\rVert_{\infty}=\lVert g^{\pi^{*}}(M^{\prime})-g^{\pi^{*}}(M)\rVert_{\infty}\leq\lVert r^{\prime}-r\rVert_{\infty}+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{sp}(b^{*}(M))\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert.

(B.37)

Now, consider
\pi\in\Pi
an arbitrary policy and fix
s\in\mathcal{S}
.

\displaystyle g^{\pi}(s;M^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\mathbf{E}_{s}^{\pi,M^{\prime}}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}r^{\prime}(Z_{t})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\mathbf{E}_{s}^{\pi,M^{\prime}}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}r(Z_{t})\right]+\lVert r^{\prime}-r\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{=}\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\mathbf{E}_{s}^{\pi,M^{\prime}}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\big(g^{*}(M)+\left(e_{S_{t}}-p(Z_{t})\right)b^{*}(M)-\Delta^{*}(Z_{t};M)\big)\right]+\lVert r^{\prime}-r\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\overset{(\ddagger)}{\leq}\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\mathbf{E}_{s}^{\pi,M^{\prime}}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\big(e_{S_{t}}-p(Z_{t})\big)b^{*}(M)\right]+g^{*}(M)+\lVert r^{\prime}-r\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\overset{(\lx@sectionsign)}{=}\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\mathbf{E}_{s}^{\pi,M^{\prime}}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\big(p^{\prime}(Z_{t})-p(Z_{t})\big)b^{*}(M)\right]+g^{*}(M)+\lVert r^{\prime}-r\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\overset{(\$)}{\leq}g^{*}(M)+\lVert r^{\prime}-r\rVert_{\infty}+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{sp}(b^{*}(M))\lVert p^{\prime}-p\rVert_{\infty}

where

(\dagger)
is obtained by definition of
\Delta^{*}(M)
and Doob’s optional stopping theorem;

(\ddagger)
uses that since
\mathrm{sp}(g^{*}(M))=0
, we have
\Delta^{*}(M)\geq 0
;

(\lx@sectionsign)
uses that
\mathbf{E}_{s}^{\pi,M^{\prime}}[\sum_{t=1}^{T}(e_{S_{t}}-p^{\prime}(Z_{t}))b^{*}(M)]=\mathbf{E}_{s}^{\pi,M^{\prime}}[b^{*}(s;M)-b^{*}(S_{T+1};M)]\leq\mathrm{sp}(b^{*}(M))
; and

(\$)
follows from
\lvert(p^{\prime}(z)-p(z))b^{*}(M)\rvert\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{sp}(b^{*}(M))\lVert p^{\prime}(z)-p(z)\rVert_{1}
for
z\in\mathcal{Z}
.

We conclude by combining the above with (
B.37
).

\blacksquare

In
Lemma

B.23
below, we recall a classical result linking the bias span to the diameter.

Lemma B.23

(
Bartlett and Tewari 2009
;
Fruit 2019
)
.

Let
M\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p,r)
be a communicating Markov decision process.
Then
\mathrm{sp}(b^{*}(M))\leq\mathrm{sp}(r)D(M)

Proof.

This result is well-known.
We provide a proof for self-containedness.
Let
s\neq s^{\prime}
and let
\pi\in\Pi
be a policy such that
\mathbf{E}_{s}^{M,\pi}[\tau_{s^{\prime}}]<\infty
, where
\tau_{s^{\prime}}:=\inf\{t\geq 1:S_{t}=s^{\prime}\}
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{s}^{M,\pi}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{\tau_{s^{\prime}}-1}(g^{*}-R_{t})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{=}\mathbf{E}_{s}^{M,\pi}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{\tau_{s^{\prime}}-1}\left(p(Z_{t})b^{*}-b^{*}(S_{t})+\Delta^{*}(Z_{t})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(\ddagger)}{=}\mathbf{E}_{s}^{M,\pi}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{\tau_{s^{\prime}}-1}\left(b^{*}(S_{t+1})-b^{*}(S_{t})\right)\right]+\mathbf{E}_{s}^{M,\pi}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{\tau_{s^{\prime}}-1}\Delta^{*}(Z_{t})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(\lx@sectionsign)}{\geq}b^{*}(s^{\prime})-b^{*}(s)

where

(\dagger)
follows by definition of
\Delta^{*}
;

(\ddagger)
uses that
\mathbf{E}[e_{S_{t+1}}|Z_{t}]=p(Z_{t})
; and

(\lx@sectionsign)
follows from the Bellman equation
\Delta^{*}(Z_{t})\geq 0
.
On the other hand, we see that
\mathbf{E}_{s}^{M,\pi}[\sum_{t=1}^{\tau_{s^{\prime}}-1}(g^{*}-R_{t})]\leq\mathrm{sp}(r)\mathbf{E}[\tau_{s^{\prime}}]
.
Taking the infimum for
\pi\in\Pi
, we conclude.

\blacksquare

In
Lemma

B.24
below, we bound the sensibility of
\mathrm{KL}(p||q)
to perturbations of
p
and
q
.

Lemma B.24
.

Let
p\ll q\in\mathcal{P}[m]
and
p^{\prime}\ll q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}[m]
be two pairs of categorical distributions over
m
elements.
Assume that
\support(p)=\support(p^{\prime})
and that, for all
i\in\support(p)
, we have
(1-\epsilon)p^{\prime}_{i}\leq p_{i}\leq(1+\epsilon)p^{\prime}_{i}
and
(1-\epsilon)q^{\prime}_{i}\leq q_{i}\leq(1+\epsilon)q^{\prime}_{i}
for some
\epsilon\leq\frac{1}{2}
.
Then

\displaystyle(1-\epsilon)\mathrm{KL}(p^{\prime}||q^{\prime})-2\epsilon\log(em)\leq\mathrm{KL}(p||q)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\epsilon)\mathrm{KL}(p^{\prime}||q^{\prime})+2\epsilon\log(em)

\displaystyle(1-\epsilon)\mathrm{KL}(p||q)-2\epsilon\log(em)\leq\mathrm{KL}(p^{\prime}||q^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq(1+2\epsilon)\mathrm{KL}(p||q)+2\epsilon\log(em)

Proof.

Using that
\log(1+x)\leq x
and
\log(1-x)>-x
for
x\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
, we get on the one hand:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}\log\left(\frac{p_{i}}{q_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}\log\left(\frac{1}{p_{i}}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}\log\left(\frac{1}{q_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq-(1-\epsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{m}p^{\prime}_{i}\log\left(\frac{1}{(1+\epsilon)p^{\prime}_{i}}\right)+(1+\epsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{m}p^{\prime}_{i}\log\left(\frac{1}{(1-\epsilon)q^{\prime}_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=(1+\epsilon)\mathrm{KL}(p^{\prime}||q^{\prime})+2\epsilon\mathrm{Ent}(p^{\prime})+(1-\epsilon^{\prime})\log(1+\epsilon)-(1+\epsilon)\log(1-\epsilon)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\epsilon)\mathrm{KL}(p^{\prime}||q^{\prime})+2\epsilon\log(em).

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}\log\left(\frac{p_{i}}{q_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}\log\left(\frac{1}{p_{i}}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}\log\left(\frac{1}{q_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq-(1+\epsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{m}p^{\prime}_{i}\log\left(\frac{1}{(1-\epsilon)p^{\prime}_{i}}\right)+(1-\epsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{m}p^{\prime}_{i}\log\left(\frac{1}{(1+\epsilon)q^{\prime}_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq(1-\epsilon)\mathrm{KL}(p^{\prime}||q^{\prime})-2\epsilon\mathrm{Ent}(p^{\prime})+(1+\epsilon)\log(1-\epsilon)-(1-\epsilon)\log(1+\epsilon)

\displaystyle\geq(1-\epsilon)\mathrm{KL}(p^{\prime}||q^{\prime})-2\epsilon\log(em).

This concludes the proof.
The second pair of inequalities are dervied from the first two.

\blacksquare

Appendix C
Regret analysis of
ECoE*

In this section, we provide a regret analysis of
ECoE*
(
Algorithm

2
) and we prove
Theorem

19
, that shows that
\textrm{Reg}(T;M,\texttt{ECoE*})\leq K(M)\log(T)+\oh(\log(T))
.

As
ECoE*
is the only algorithm of interest in the sequel, we drop the dependence in
\Lambda\equiv\texttt{ECoE*}
in notations.
Similarly, the underlying Markov decision process will be always equal to
M
throughout, so that we will drop the dependent in
M
as well.
We end up writing
\mathbf{E}[-]
and
\mathbf{P}(-)
in place of
\mathbf{E}^{M,\texttt{ECoE*}}[-]
and
\mathbf{P}^{M,\texttt{ECoE*}}(-)

The starting point of the regret analysis is to write the expected regret as

\textrm{Reg}(T)=\mathbf{E}\left[\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}N_{T}(z)\Delta^{*}(z)\right]+\OH(1)

As
\Delta^{*}(z)\geq 0
, the natural strategy is to upper-bound
\mathbf{E}^{M,\Lambda}[N_{T}(z)]
for pairs with
\Delta^{*}(z)>0
.
Such pairs corresponds in particulars to non-optimal pairs
z\notin\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
, see
Section

2.1.4
.
In
Theorem

C.1
below, we upper-bound
\mathbf{E}[N_{T}(z)]
for a large panel of choices for the regularization hyperparameter of
ECoE*
.
The set of assumptions that the regularization must satisfy is technical and detailed in
Section

C.1
, see
6
.

Theorem C.1

(Visit rates of
ECoE*
, general statement)
.

Assume that the regularization hyperparameter
{\bar{\epsilon}}
satisfies
6
, and that the ambient space
\mathcal{M}
has Bernoulli rewards (
3
) and strong product structure (
5
).
Fix
M\in\mathcal{M}
a communicating Markov decision process.
Let
\mu^{*}
the central optimal exploration probability measure of
M
as given by
Definition

B.19
.
For every non-optimal pair
z\notin\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
, its expected number of visits under
ECoE*
satisfies

\mathbf{E}\left[N_{T}(z)\right]\leq\frac{\mu^{*}(z)\log(T)}{C(\mu^{*},M)}+\OH\left(\frac{1}{{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}(T)^{6}}+\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\exp\left(-\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\mathrm{tst}}(t)\right)\log(t)\right)\right)+\oh(\log(T))

when
T\to\infty
.

Theorem

19
is an immediate corollary of
Theorem

C.1
for the choice of regularizer described in (
18
), because the second order term grows as
{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}(T)^{-6}+\int^{T}(\frac{1}{t})^{1+\epsilon_{\mathrm{tst}}(t)}\mathrm{d}t=\oh(\log(T))
.

Outline.

The proof is tedious.
The idea is to decompose the number of visits of
z\notin\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
along with the decomposition of time instants that is inherent to
Algorithm

2
: There are exploration times
t\in\mathcal{T}^{-}
, co-exploration times
t\in\mathcal{T}^{\pm}
, exploitation times
\mathcal{T}^{+}
and panic times
\mathcal{T}^{!}
.
As
\mathcal{T}^{\pm}\subseteq\mathcal{T}^{-}
, we decompose
N_{T}(z)
as

\displaystyle N_{T}(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\mathbf{1}(Z_{t}=z,t\in\mathcal{T}^{-})+\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\mathbf{1}(Z_{t}=z,t\in\mathcal{T}^{+})+\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\mathbf{1}(Z_{t}=z,t\in\mathcal{T}^{!}).

(C.1)

That is, we distinguish three cases in (
C.1
).
Either
Z_{t}=z
is played because
(I) the algorithm is explorating to collect information on presumably sub-optimal pairs; or
(II) because it is exploiting the (wrongly) presumed optimal policy;
(III) the algorithm just panicked by discovering a transition unseen so far.

We begin by (II) in
Section

C.2
, i.e., by bounding the number of times a sub-optimal pair may be played during exploitation and show that it is of order
{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}(T)^{-6}+\int^{T}\exp(-(1+\epsilon_{\mathrm{tst}}(t))\log(t))\mathrm{d}t
.
From there, simple algebra provides conditions on how small
{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}
and
\epsilon_{\mathrm{tst}}
must be so that this term is sub-logarithmic, leading to
6
.
In
Section

C.3
, we show that the dominant is due to exploration (I), for a total number of visits of order
\mu^{*}(z)C(\mu^{*},M)^{-1}\log(T)
, where
\mu^{*}
is central optimal exploration measure of
M
.
Lastly, the number of panic times (III) is upper-bounded by
\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert
in
Section

C.4
.

C.1
Required assumptions on the regularization hyperparameter

In the regret analysis, we assume that the regularization hyparameter
{\bar{\epsilon}}
satisfies
6
.
These assumptions are technical and we invite the reader to only take them into consideration when necessary.
It is easy to check that
6
is satisfied for
{\bar{\epsilon}}
chosen as in (
18
) in the main text.

Assumption 6

(Properties of regularization hyperparameter)
.

The regularization hyperparameter
{\bar{\epsilon}}\equiv({\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{tst}}):\mathbf{N}\to(0,1]
satisfies

(
A0
)

The map
{\bar{\epsilon}}
is vanishing, i.e.,
{\bar{\epsilon}}(m)\to 0
as
m\to\infty
;

(
A1
)

The map
{\bar{\epsilon}}
is non-increasing for the product order
\leq
, i.e.,
{\bar{\epsilon}}(n)\leq{\bar{\epsilon}}(m)
for
n\geq m
;

(
A2
)

For all
\alpha>0
,
{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}(T)=\omega(\log(T)^{-\alpha})
when
T\to\infty
;

(
A3
)

For all
\alpha>0
,
\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}(m)=\omega(m^{-\alpha})
when
m\to\infty
;

(
A4
)

For all
\alpha>0
,
\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}(m)=\omega(\log(m)^{-\alpha})
when
m\to\infty
;

(
A5
)

We have
\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}(m)=\oh(\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}(m))
when
m\to\infty
;

(
A6
)

For all
(\alpha_{i})\geq 0
, the function
\varphi_{\alpha}(m):=\epsilon_{\mathrm{conv}}(m)^{\alpha_{1}}\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}(m)^{\alpha_{2}}\exp(-\alpha_{3}\epsilon_{\mathrm{unif}}(m)^{-\alpha_{4}})m
is non-decreasing asymptotically, i.e.,
\exists m_{\alpha},\forall\ell^{\prime}\geq\ell\geq m_{\alpha},\varphi_{\alpha}(\ell)\leq\varphi_{\alpha}(\ell^{\prime})
;

(
A7
)

\epsilon_{\mathrm{tst}}(T)=\omega\left(\log\log(T)\log(T)^{-1}\right)
when
T\to\infty
;

C.2
Amount of wrong exploitation

In this section, we upper-bound the number of “wrong” exploitation times, i.e., the amount of time spent playing the approximately optimal empirical policy
\pi_{t}^{+}
while
\pi_{t}^{+}
is a sub-optimal policy of the true hidden model
M
.

Lemma C.2
.

For all
z\notin\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
, we have

\mathbf{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\mathbf{1}(Z_{t}=z,t\in\mathcal{T}^{+})\right]=\OH\left(\frac{1}{{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}(T)^{6}}+\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\exp\left(-\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\mathrm{tst}}(t)\right)\log(t)\right)\right)

(C.2)

All throughout, we fix a non-optimal pair
z_{0}\notin\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
.

C.2.1
Idea of the proof

To begin with, note that the algorithm only takes decisions at
initial
exploitation times.
Therefore, it is only relevant to decompose
t\in\mathcal{T}^{+}
according to good events if
t\in\mathcal{T}^{+}_{0}
.
Thankfully, the number of exploitation times
\lvert\mathcal{T}^{+}\rvert
and the number of initial exploitation times
\lvert\mathcal{T}^{+}_{0}\rvert
are proportional to one another, because exploitation phases are either done up to regeneration or killed by the discovery of an unseen transition; Both happen quickly in average and exploitation phases are short.
This is formalized in
Lemma

C.3
.

Lemma C.3
.

For all
z_{0}\notin\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
, we have

\mathbf{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\mathbf{1}(Z_{t}=z_{0},t\in\mathcal{T}^{+})\right]=\Theta\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\mathbf{1}(z_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}(t)}(\hat{M}_{t}),t\in\mathcal{T}^{+}_{0})\right]\right)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{as\penalty\ $T\to\infty$.}

The proof of
Lemma

C.3
relies on the notion of
co-exploration structure
and is postponed to
Section

C.2.2
.

Introduce the good events

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{t}

\displaystyle:=\left(\forall z\in\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{skel}}_{t},\kappa(M)\lVert\hat{M}_{t}(z)-M(z)\rVert^{*}<{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}(t)<\frac{1}{3}\Delta^{g}(M)\right)\text{;\penalty\ and}

(C.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{t}

\displaystyle:=\left(\forall z\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}(t)}(\hat{M}_{t}),\kappa(M)\lVert\hat{M}_{t}(z)-M(z)\rVert^{*}<{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}(t)<\frac{1}{3}\Delta^{g}(M)\right)

where
\kappa(M):=\max\{C(M),1+D^{*}(M)\}<\infty
is the maximum between the constant
C(M)
given by
Proposition

12
and the worst diameter (
Definition

A.4
); and
\Delta^{g}(M)
is the gain-gap of
M
(
Definition

A.5
).
The first event
\mathcal{E}_{t}
states that the data is approximately correct on the skeleton and the second
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{t}
that it is approximately correct on leveled optimal pairs.
Note that under
\mathcal{E}_{t}\cap\mathcal{E}_{t}^{\prime}
, we have
\lVert\hat{M}_{t}(z)-M(z)\rVert^{*}<\infty
for every pair
z\in\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{skel}}_{t}\cup\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t})
, so that
\hat{M}_{t}(z)\sim M(z)
.

Decomposition in good events.

Following
Lemma

C.3
, we are left to bound the number of initial exploitation times with
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t})\setminus\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)\neq\emptyset
.
Under the event
(\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t})\setminus\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)\neq\emptyset)
, leveled optimal pairs are wrong and the algorithm may play sub-optimal pairs during exploitation.
Yet, if the algorithm exploits at time
t\in\mathcal{T}^{+}_{0}
, then it has passed the exploration GLR test (
16
), meaning that the algorithm considers that every leveling-aware confusing model has been rejected with satisfying certainty.
It means that the GLR exploration test has failed to detect a lack of information, and we account for three different scenarios for that failure, as given by the decomposition of
\mathbf{1}(z_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t}),t\in\mathcal{T}^{+}_{0})
below:

\mathbf{1}\left(z_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t})\atop t\in\mathcal{T}^{+}_{0}\right)\leq\mathbf{1}\left(z_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t})\atop t\in\mathcal{T}^{+}_{0},\mathcal{E}_{t},\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{t}\right)+\mathbf{1}\left(z_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t})\atop t\in\mathcal{T}^{+}_{0},(\mathcal{E}_{t}^{\prime})^{c}\right)+\mathbf{1}(\mathcal{E}_{t}^{c}).

(C.4)

where
z_{0}
is some fixed pair
z_{0}\notin\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
.
The first term
\mathbf{1}(z_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t}),t\in\mathcal{T}^{+}_{0},\mathcal{E}_{t},\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{t})
accounts for exploitation times where the empirical data is approximately correct on both the skeleton
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{skel}}_{t}
and leveled optimal pairs
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t})
; Such cases boil down to the power of the GLR exploration test (
16
) and the tuning of
\epsilon_{\mathrm{tst}}(t)
, for a total expected aggregate error of order
\int(\frac{1}{t})^{1+\epsilon_{\mathrm{tst}}(t)}\mathrm{d}t
, see
Lemma

C.5
.
The third term
\mathbf{1}(\mathcal{E}_{t}^{c})
quantifies how likely it is for the empirical data to be wrong on the skeleton.
Because visit counts are high on the skeleton by definition, the aggregate error due to this term is bounded in expectation, see
Lemma

C.9
.
The dominant term is the second one,
\mathbf{1}(z_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t}),t\in\mathcal{T}^{+},(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})^{c}_{t})
, and accounts for the speed at which information is gathered on leveled optimal pairs
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t})
during exploitation.
This term can be made sub-logarithmic thanks to the co-exploration mechanism of
ECoE*
.
In expectation, the associated expected aggregate error term is shown to be of order
{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}(T)^{-6}
, see
Lemmas

C.7
and
C.8
.

The co-exploration structure
\mathfrak{Z}_{t}^{+}
.

When a exploitation phase begins, it lasts until regeneration or until regeneration is compromised by the discovery of a new transition that provokes a panic time.
The possibility that the support of
\hat{M}_{t}
is wrong and that components of
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t})
may not be closed in
M
has to be taken into consideration.
Indeed, in the early phases of
ECoE*
, some transitions
(S_{t},A_{t},S_{t+1})
have never been observed yet.
If they are first observed during an exploitation phase, it is possible that their discovery make
ECoE*
exit
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t})
and compromise the regenerative requirement of exploitation phases.
In these regards, we introduce below the notion of
co-exploration structure
and we partition exploitation times
\mathcal{T}^{+}
depending on it.

By design, the algorithm splits
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t})
into components
\mathcal{Z}_{t}^{1},\ldots,\mathcal{Z}_{t}^{c(t)}
that are the communicating components of
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t})
under the empirically observed model
\hat{M}_{t}
.
The decomposition itself is denoted
\mathfrak{Z}_{t}^{+}:=(\mathcal{Z}_{t}^{1},\ldots,\mathcal{Z}_{t}^{c(t)})
and is called the
co-exploration structure
at time
t
.
Given a co-exploration structure
\mathfrak{Z}_{0}
, we introduce
\mathcal{T}^{+}(\mathfrak{Z}_{0})
the exploitation times when the exploitation structure is
\mathfrak{Z}_{0}
at the associated exploitation time, i.e.,

\mathcal{T}^{+}(\mathfrak{Z}_{0}):=\bigcup\left\{\left\{t_{k},\ldots,t_{k+1}-1\right\}:k\geq 1,t_{k}\in\mathcal{T}^{+}_{0}\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\mathfrak{Z}_{t_{k}}^{+}=\mathfrak{Z}_{0}\right\}

(C.5)

and further write
\mathcal{T}^{+}_{0}(\mathfrak{Z}_{0})=\mathcal{T}^{+}(\mathfrak{Z}_{0})\cap\mathcal{T}^{+}_{0}
the associated initial exploitation times.
Depending on whether the co-exploration structure
\mathfrak{Z}_{0}
is closed in
M
(i.e., every
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\in\mathfrak{Z}_{0}
is communicating in
M
) or not (see
Definition

C.4
below), the analysis is different, see
Lemmas

C.7
and
C.8
.

Definition C.0

(Closed structure)
.

Given a Markov decision process
M\equiv(\mathcal{Z},p,\nu)
, a set of pairs
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
is said
closed
in
M
if
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
is equal to its unique communicating component (
Definition

8
), i.e.., if the model
M|_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}}\equiv(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime},p|_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}},\nu|_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}})
is well-defined and communicating;
A structure
\mathfrak{Z}\equiv(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{Z}_{c})
is said
closed
in
M
if every
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
is closed in
M
.

C.2.2
Proof of
Lemma

C.3
: Initial exploitation and exploitation times

All throughout the section, we fix
z_{0}\notin\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
.

Proof.

Let
t_{k}\in\mathcal{T}^{+}_{0}(\mathfrak{Z}_{0})
be an initial exploitation time with co-exploration structure
\mathfrak{Z}_{0}
such that
z_{0}\in\bigcup\mathfrak{Z}_{0}\equiv(\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{1},\ldots,\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{m})
where
\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{i}
is the
i
-th communicating component of
\mathfrak{Z}_{0}
in
\hat{M}_{t_{k}}
.
Denote
\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{u}
the component containing
S_{t_{k}}
.
We introduce the reward function

f_{t_{k}}(s,a)\equiv f(s,a):=\mathbf{1}(s=S_{t_{k}})+\mathbf{1}(s\notin\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{u}))

that tracks visits to the initial state and states outside of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{u}
.
Let
g^{f}
,
b^{f}
be the gain and bias functions obtained by iterating the uniform policy
\pi^{u}
on
\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{u}
, extended to the uniform policy outsie of
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{u})
for the reward function
f
.
Let
\Delta^{f}(s,a)=g^{f}(s)+b^{f}(s)-f(s,a)-p(s,a)b^{f}
be the associated gap function.

If
\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{u}
is closed in
M
, then we see that the iterates of
\pi^{u}
stating from
S_{t_{k}}
remain almost surely on
\mathcal{Z}^{u}_{0}
, so
\mathrm{sp}(g^{f}|{\scriptstyle\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{i})})=0
;
If
\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{u}
is not closed in
M
, then
\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{u}
is either transient or recurrent under
\pi^{u}
in
M
, and in either cases
\mathrm{sp}(g^{f})=0
.
Overall, we have
\mathrm{sp}(g^{f}|{\scriptstyle\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{u})})=0
, that
\alpha_{t_{k}}:=\min(g^{f}|{\scriptstyle\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{u})})>0
and that
\beta_{t_{k}}:=\max\{\mathrm{sp}(b^{f}),\lVert\Delta^{f}\rVert_{\infty}\}<\infty
.
Let
0<\alpha
,
\beta<\infty
be the respective minimum and maximum values that
\alpha_{t_{k}}
,
\beta_{t_{k}}
can take for all finitely many possible values for
\mathfrak{Z}_{0}
,
u
and
S_{t_{k}}
.

Now, during the phase
\{t_{k},\ldots,t_{k+1}-1\}
, note that
f(Z_{t})=1
holds only for
t=t_{k}
by construction of
f
and of exploitation phases.
Therefore,

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}-1}f_{t_{k}}(Z_{t})

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{=}\sum_{t=t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}-1}\left(g^{f_{t_{k}}}(S_{t})+\left(e_{S_{t}}-p(Z_{t})\right)b^{f_{t_{k}}}-\Delta^{f_{t_{k}}}(Z_{t})\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ddagger)}{\geq}\alpha\left(t_{k+1}-t_{k}\right)-\beta\mathbf{1}(S_{t_{k+1}}\neq S_{t_{k}})+\sum_{t=t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}-1}\left(\left(e_{S_{t+1}}-p(Z_{t})\right)b^{f_{t_{k}}}-\Delta^{f_{t_{k}}}(Z_{t})\right)

where

(\dagger)
follows by definition of
\Delta^{f}(Z_{t})
; and

(\ddagger)
follows from a telescopic sum.
Note that the right-most term is a martingale, hence its expectation is zero.
Indeed,
\mathbf{E}[e_{S_{t+1}}|Z_{t}]=p(Z_{t})
and by Poisson’s equation,
\mathbf{E}[\Delta^{f}(Z_{t})|S_{t}]=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}(S_{t})}\pi^{u}(a|S_{t})\Delta^{f}(S_{t},a_{t})=0
.

Summing for
t_{k}\in\mathcal{T}_{0}^{+}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\mathbf{1}(z_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}(t)}(\hat{M}_{t}),t\in\mathcal{T}^{+}_{0})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{\equiv}\mathbf{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\mathbf{1}(\mathcal{E}^{0}_{t},t\in\mathcal{T}^{+}_{0})\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{E}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}(t_{k}<T,\mathcal{E}^{0}_{t},t_{k}\in\mathcal{T}^{+}_{0})\Big(\alpha\left(\min(T,t_{k+1})-t_{k}\right)-\beta\mathbf{1}(S_{t_{k+1}}\neq S_{t_{k}})\Big)\right]

\displaystyle\geq\alpha\mathbf{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\mathbf{1}(\mathcal{E}^{0}_{t},t\in\mathcal{T}^{+})\right]-\beta\mathbf{E}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}(\mathcal{E}^{0}_{t},t_{k}\in\mathcal{T}^{+}_{0},S_{t_{k+1}}\neq S_{t_{k}})\right]

\displaystyle=\alpha\mathbf{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\mathbf{1}(\mathcal{E}^{0}_{t},t\in\mathcal{T}^{+})\right]-\beta\sum_{\mathfrak{Z}_{0}}\mathbf{E}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}(\mathcal{E}^{0}_{t},t_{k}\in\mathcal{T}^{+}_{0}(\mathfrak{Z}_{0}),S_{t_{k+1}}\neq S_{t_{k}})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(\ddagger)}{\geq}\alpha\mathbf{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\mathbf{1}(\mathcal{E}^{0}_{t},t\in\mathcal{T}^{+})\right]-\beta\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert

where

(\dagger)
introduces the short-hand
\mathcal{E}_{t}^{0}\equiv(z_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t}))
; and

(\ddagger)
relies on the observation that the number of exploitation phases killed by a panic is at most
\lvert\mathcal{S}\rvert\lvert\mathcal{Z}\rvert
, the maximal number of times a transition may be discovered.
We conclude accordingly.

\blacksquare

C.2.3
Wrong exploitation with correct data on leveled optimal pairs

We start by controlling the error term due to exploitation times for which
Z_{t}\notin\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
while
\mathcal{E}_{t}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{t}
hold, see (
C.3
).
In other words: The algorithm exploits a non-optimal pair, meaning that the optimal policy is wrongly estimated while the data is correct on the skeleton and on leveled optimal pairs because
\mathcal{E}_{t}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{t}
hold.

Lemma C.5
.

Let
z_{0}\notin\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
and let
\mathcal{E}_{t}
,
\mathcal{E}_{t}^{\prime}
be the events as given by (
C.3
).
Then

\mathbf{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\mathbf{1}(z_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}(t)}(\hat{M}_{t}),t\in\mathcal{T}^{+}_{0},\mathcal{E}_{t},\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{t})\right]\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\exp\left(-\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\mathrm{tst}}(t)\right)\log(t)\right).

The idea of the proof is that if the exploration policy is not optimal, then the empirical data
\hat{M}_{t}(z)
is significatively incorrect for some
z\in\mathcal{Z}
.
Yet, under
\mathcal{E}_{t}\cap\mathcal{E}_{t}^{\prime}
, the data is approximately correct on
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{skel}}_{t}\cup\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}(t)}(\hat{M}_{t})
; Yet, the algorithm cannot exploits unless the GLR exploration test of (
16
) has been passed, that is supposed to check the correctness of empirical data outside of
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{skel}}_{t}\cup\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}(t)}(\hat{M}_{t})
.
So, the error term bounded by
Lemma

C.5
is directly related to the correctness of the exploration test.

Proof.

Introduce
M_{t}^{\dagger}
the model given as follows:

M_{t}^{\dagger}(z):=\begin{cases}\hat{M}_{t}(z)&\text{if $z\in\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{skel}}_{t}\cup\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t})$;}\\
M(z)&\text{if $z\notin\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{skel}}_{t}\cup\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t})$.}\end{cases}

(C.6)

Note that since
\hat{M}_{t}\ll M
, we have
\hat{M}_{t}\ll M^{\dagger}_{t}
as well.

(
S
TEP
1
)

Under the event
\mathcal{E}_{t}\cap\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{t}\cap(z_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t}))
, the model
M^{\dagger}_{t}
as given by (
C.6
) satisfies
M_{t}^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(\hat{M}_{t})
.

Proof.

Under
\mathcal{E}_{t}\cap\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{t}
and following
Proposition

12
, we have
\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M^{\dagger}_{t})\subseteq\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
.
Now, under the event
(z_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t}))
, there exists a policy
\pi\in\Pi^{*}([\hat{M}_{t}]_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}(t)})
that is (
i
) unichain in
\hat{M}_{t}
(
ii
) making
z_{0}
recurrent in
\hat{M}_{t}
such that

g^{\pi}(\hat{M}_{t})\overset{(\dagger)}{\geq}g^{*}([\hat{M}_{t}]_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}(t)})-{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}(t)\overset{(\ddagger)}{=}g^{*}(\hat{M}_{t})-{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}(t)

(C.7)

where

(\dagger)
follows by definition of the leveling transform
[-]_{\epsilon}
, see
Definition

9
; and

(\ddagger)
follows by
Lemma

B.1
.
Under
\mathcal{E}_{t}\cap\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{t}
, we have
\hat{M}_{t}(z)\sim M(z)
for
z\in\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{skel}}_{t}\cup\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t})
and
\hat{M}_{t}(z)\ll M(z)
for other pairs, so
\pi
is unichain and
z_{0}
is recurrent under
\pi
in
M
as well.
Together with
z_{0}\notin\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
, we deduce that
g^{\pi}(s;M)\leq g^{*}(s;M)-\Delta^{g}(M)
for all
s\in\mathcal{S}
.
Now, by design of
\kappa(M)
in (
C.3
),

\displaystyle g^{\pi}(s;M_{t}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{\leq}g^{\pi}(s;M)+\kappa(M)\lVert M_{t}^{\dagger}-M\rVert^{*}

\displaystyle\overset{(\ddagger)}{\leq}g^{\pi^{*}}(s;M)-\Delta^{g}(M)+\kappa(M)\lVert M_{t}^{\dagger}-M\rVert^{*}

\displaystyle\overset{(\lx@sectionsign)}{\leq}g^{\pi^{*}}(s;M_{t}^{\dagger})-\Delta^{g}(M)+2\kappa(M)\lVert M_{t}^{\dagger}-M\rVert^{*}

(C.8)

for all
s\in\mathcal{S}
, where in the above

(\dagger)
follows by
Lemma

D.9
and by
\kappa(M)\geq 1+D^{*}(M)
;

(\ddagger)
introduces
\pi^{*}
an arbitrary bias optimal policy of
M
and
\Delta^{g}(M)
is the gain-gap (
Definition

A.5
); and

(\lx@sectionsign)
invokes
Lemma

D.9
and
\kappa(M)\geq 1+D^{*}(M)
again.
So, if
\hat{\pi}\in\Pi^{*}(\hat{M}_{t})
, then for all
s\in\mathcal{S}
, we have

\displaystyle g^{\hat{\pi}}(s;M_{t}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\overset{(\dagger)}{=}g^{\hat{\pi}}(s;\hat{M}_{t})

\displaystyle\overset{(\ddagger)}{\leq}g^{\pi}(s;\hat{M}_{t})+{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}(t)

\displaystyle\overset{(\lx@sectionsign)}{\leq}g^{\pi}(s;M^{\dagger}_{t})+{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}(t)\overset{(\$)}{\leq}g^{\pi^{*}}(s;M_{t}^{\dagger})-\Delta^{g}(M)+2\kappa(M)\lVert M_{t}^{\dagger}-M\rVert^{*}+{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}(t)

where

(\dagger)
follows from
M_{t}^{\dagger}=\hat{M}_{t}
on
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t})
;

(\ddagger)
follows from (
C.7
);

(\lx@sectionsign)
follows from
M_{t}^{\dagger}=\hat{M}_{t}
on
\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t})
again; and

(\$)
follows from (
C.8
).
As
\lVert M^{\dagger}_{t}-M\rVert^{*}\leq\lVert\hat{M}_{t}-M\rVert^{*}
, we conclude that if
2\kappa(M)\lVert{M}^{\dagger}_{t}-M\rVert^{*}+{\epsilon_{\mathrm{lvl}}}(t)<\Delta^{g}(M)
, then
\hat{\pi}\notin\Pi^{*}(M_{t}^{\dagger})
.

Hence
M_{t}^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(\hat{M}_{t})
.

\blacksquare

(
S
TEP
2
)

For all
z_{0}\notin\mathcal{Z}^{**}(M)
, we have

\mathbf{P}\left(z_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}^{**}_{\epsilon\mathrm{lvl}}(\hat{M}_{t}),t\in\mathcal{T}^{+}_{0},\mathcal{E}_{t},\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{t}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\mathrm{tst}}(t)\right)\log(t)\right).

(C.9)

Proof.

By definition of initial exploitation times, if
t\in\mathcal{T}_{0}^{+}
then the algorithm has passed its exploration test of (
16
), meaning that for all
M^{\dagger}\in\mathrm{Cnf}(\hat{M}_{t})
that coincides with
\hat{M}_{t}
on
